title: "MIN325 — full lesson text"

# MIN325 — Preaching in the Contemporary World

## Course objectives

- 1. Distinguish between modernism and postmodernism and explain how these movements influenced the church’s presentation of the gospel.
- 2. Defend the reliability of the Bible as the preacher’s foundation.
- 3. Summarize the process of developing sermons that effectively communicate biblical truth.
- 4. Identify elements of effective sermons for modernists and postmodernist audiences.
- 5. Describe the elements of effective communication in a modern and postmodern world.
- 6. Discuss the impact of the preacher’s personal life on the effectiveness of sermons.
- 7. Explain the relationship between creativity, technology, and effective worship experiences.
- 8. Compare and contrast the effectiveness of inductive, deductive, and narrative preaching in a modern and postmodern world.
- 9. Explain the value and process of leading a creative preaching planning team.
- 10. Analyze the relationship between the decline of church attendance in North America and the perceived relevance of the church.

## 1 - The Search for Truth

### The Modern View of Truth

#### 1.1.1 — Define truth from a modern viewpoint.

The Renaissance and Enlightenment

To understand exactly how the modern world defines truth , it is important to take a brief look at history to see how the modern view emerged. In the 1400s, the western world began a shift away from a time period now called the Middle Ages . Though learning still existed in the Middle Ages, most education, political ideals, and social life was wrapped up in the church. Clergy were responsible for education and religion, and their influence directly affected those in top political positions. Theology was seen as the “queen of sciences” and was studied as much for its perceived importance in the world as for truly religious devotion (Olson 1999, 312). No one dared contradict the teaching of the church, and most people maintained some sort of connection with it.

Define truth from a modern viewpoint.

In the 1400s, a new movement called the Renaissance began. This movement was so called because many living at this time believed they were living through a “rebirth” of the arts. Education, architecture, and other arts reverted to classical Roman and Greek teaching and expression. It was believed that the ancient philosophy of the Romans and Greeks was superior to what had been purported in the Middle Ages and many hoped to not only immerse themselves in the classical works, but also to become imitators. The Renaissance emphasized the ability to know, learn, and create, and this age marked a surge in asking questions and discovering answers.

Figure 1.1: The Annunciation, Leonardo da Vinci (c. 1472)

During the Middle Ages, when questions were asked, the church was the institution to which the people turned for answers. The church was central to knowledge, and it provided a worldview for understanding how the world was put together. When the Renaissance emerged, philosophers and teachers began to feel the church stifled true knowledge. The Middle Ages focused on the afterlife and living in such a way as to prepare for that other world. The Renaissance focused on the here and now. The goal was the betterment of society. Renaissance proponents accused the church of imposing its Christian worldview on the general public and believed the traditional teachings of the church would keep most people in the Middle Ages. As a result, the church’s influence declined. Learning and discovery were now placed in the hands of the public, who focused their studies on classical writing, believing that discovery could only improve society. No longer did the general public look to Christian teachings for understanding; their desire was to know objectively, scientifically, and rationally. Individuals stepped out of the obscurity of the Middle Ages and promoted themselves. Pleasure in the now was the order of the day, and the optimism it gave only increased the Renaissance’s momentum.

The foundation laid by the Renaissance pushed the world forward by leaps and bounds. The ensuing ages— Enlightenment , Romanticism , and Modernism —were all built on the foundation of the Renaissance. The Enlightenment, for example, propelled science forward in exponential ways. Faith that had once been placed in church and religious things was now placed in science that could be proven objectively and rationally. In fact, science was so lauded that it was believed it could answer all of life’s problems. It became widely accepted that the more science could discover, the more society would benefit and progress. In contrast to this, religion was believed to only benefit a few. Once Isaac Newton made his incredible discoveries, most notably his discovery of the law of gravity, reason became viewed as the key to unlocking the mysteries. Many people concluded that if a human could rationally come up with discoveries, such as Newton’s, faith should be placed nowhere but in science. Reason and experience became the standards of truth, and anything that had to be accepted by faith was considered subjective and weak. This meant the supernatural had no basis for explaining anything in a rational world, and religion, because of its nature of faith, was relegated to a second-class position. Its only perceived benefit was in providing a moral basis for society. Anything beyond this realm was left to science.

These changes in society, introduced by the Renaissance and solidified by the Enlightenment, still abound today, although another shift is starting to take place. Most people in the minister’s sphere of influence have a large measure of modernism in their worldviews. So how do modern people view truth? The modern view of truth says truth is bound to science. Truth is only truth when it can be explained objectively, proven scientifically, and understood rationally. Faith in mysteries, an invisible God, and spiritual things are outside the bounds of reason and science and cannot be ultimate truth.

Explain how modernism has influenced the church’s presentation of the gospel.

Modernism

This kind of “enlightened” thinking has affected the church and its ability to present the gospel. Originally, liberal theologians sought to meld religion and science into a working relationship. Religion was to provide moral direction for society’s behavior; science was to provide understanding about humankind and their place in the world. According to historian Roger Olson (1999, 542), “This modern way of being religious sought above all to avoid conflicts with science because science always seemed to win them.” Religion became more philosophical and less practical. Because of this, most people considered themselves moral, and thus, Christians in a cultural sense. Conservative Christians reacted to this moralizing of Christian beliefs and attempted to restore more orthodox beliefs of the Christian faith while presenting these beliefs in rational ways that would capture the minds of modern thinkers. They promoted a strong belief in the Bible as ultimate authority and sought to counter the diluting effects that modernism and liberal theologians had had on the church.

Because of this infiltration of modernism into the Christian worldview, presenting the gospel has challenges. First, faith is viewed with a measure of skepticism. Second, much of the western world views the church as a moral institution having no bearing on or answer for science. Third, religion is seen as a personal matter of faith, whereas science is seen as objective, verifiable principles under which all rational people can unify. Those who do not use scientific means to prove God’s existence sometimes discount science altogether. This separation allows for faith to be stressed; however, it does not provide acceptable answers for modern thinkers trying to justify faith with science. If faith is placed in competition to science, as Olson says, science usually wins. To reach beyond these challenges, some churches and ministers have developed ways to present the gospel in rational, verifiable ways. Ministers use apologetics (arguments that prove Christian principles) to reach the rational thinkers of society.

For example, in his book Mere Christianity , C. S. Lewis promoted the argument that Jesus is either the Lord, a lunatic, or a liar. When one reads the claims Jesus made about himself, one must come to one of three conclusions. The first option says Jesus is a lunatic. Only a crazy person would make the claims Jesus made and actually die for those claims. The second option says that Jesus’ claims, if He was not really the Lord, would make Him a liar. He claims to speak the truth and be the way and the life. If He said these things, knowing them to be untrue, He is the greatest liar ever to walk the earth. The problem is that most people consider Jesus to be a moral teacher. Based on these two options, one cannot hold that opinion without coming to the conclusion that Jesus is Lord. If He is a liar or a lunatic, He would not be considered moral. If He is moral, then His words must be true. If His words must be true, then Jesus must be the Lord and Messiah He claimed to be.

Rational arguments like this, as well as others that seek to prove God’s existence through logical demonstrations or scientific means, affect ministers’ sermons to modern people. Such sermons stress less the qualities of faith, but rather seek to prove Jesus rationally. Christians use Bill Bright’s “Four Spiritual Laws,” the “Lord, Lunatic, Liar” argument mentioned above, and other arguments to prove the need for belief in Jesus. While rational arguments can be helpful in addressing objections to Christianity and can help some individuals to seriously consider the claims of the gospel, this is only a first step. It must be kept in mind that the work of the Holy Spirit is essential to lead someone to faith in Christ. Ministers must rely on the work of the Holy Spirit even as they present persuasive evidence for the Christian faith.

**Q:** How was the church viewed during the Renaissance and Enlightenment?

**A:** The church was not seen as the center of life as it was in the Middle Ages. In the Renaissance, science and philosophy outranked religion, and the church was considered a moral place for the weak, not a true place of knowledge and learning. The Enlightenment only solidified this feeling and viewed the church as irrational and subjective, which was far inferior to the objectivity and rationality of science.

**Q:** According to modernism, how do religion and science differ?

**A:** Religion is for morality only; it is subjective, personal, and for those of weak faith. Science, on the other hand, is filled with rational, verifiable proofs that are the basis for true knowing.

**Q:** How did C. S. Lewis argue for Jesus’ lordship?

**A:** Jesus is either Lord, a lunatic, or a liar. To make the claims Jesus made, He would either be the world’s greatest liar, a complete lunatic, or just who He claimed to be—Lord.

### Postmodern Truth and Biblical Preaching

#### 1.2.1 — Identify similarities and differences between modernism and postmodernism.

Postmodernism

Like all worldviews, however, modernism is now in a time of transition. After roughly two hundred years of modern thinking and advancement, new generations are emerging that have begun to question modern tenets. Modernism is optimistic: human beings are essentially good, and the scientific advancements they make through reason and objectivity will only improve society and provide answers for the problems of the world. Those growing up in the 1960s observed that the tremendous advancements of science did not necessarily make the world a better place. War, famine, disease, and poverty still exist. People still have an innate longing for something beyond themselves, something they do not have to explain. Science was supposed to answer these life problems, but it could not. Thus, the 1960s and 1970s saw a rise in spiritual quests. People became intrigued by the idea of a mystical, spiritual side to life, one that needed no scientific proof, only experiential proof. The subjectivity that made religion weak in modernism has made it hugely popular in postmodernism .

Identify similarities and differences between modernism and postmodernism.

In a strong reaction to the modernism of their parents, children of postmodernism do not see the world as ordered, objective, or rational. For them, life is individualistic: what is right for one person is not necessarily right for another. Life does not look optimistic. Rules and facts are to be questioned in the search for personal truth. Morality is no longer seen as a function of the church, but rather a function of individual conviction. Strong standards of right and wrong are viewed as rigid and bigoted, and “good” religion is that which is pluralistic and all-embracing.

Just what is a postmodern definition of truth ? According to Graham Johnston (2001, 30), “Truth is up for grabs.” In contrast to modernism, the subjective world of morality and personal values is hugely important to postmodernists, but this means truth has no set definition. In essence, postmodernism says, “What you believe, experience, and perceive is your truth. What I believe, experience, and perceive is my truth. We can live together; just do not push your view of truth on me.”

Explain how the shift from modernism to postmodernism affects biblical preaching.

Postmodernism’s Effect on Biblical Preaching

How do ministers preach the truth of the gospel to a world in flux? This is a challenge facing all preachers, a challenge that is both daunting and exciting. The daunting task is that ministers have to consider both worldviews when preaching because people are still a mix of both worlds. The exciting part of this task is that today’s ministers are living through a historical, philosophical shift. The opportunities for influencing the world’s next era are amazing as people are more interested in spirituality now than they have been for decades. Commitment to biblical preaching affords great opportunities amid the obstacles.

First, the shift from modern to postmodern means ministers will have to be firm in their commitment to biblical preaching and the authority of the Bible. Modernism says, “Prove it.” Postmodernism says, “Do not push your opinions on me.” Preachers will need to be committed to the Bible as their authority. Paul instructs Timothy, “Preach the Word; be prepared in season and out of season; correct, rebuke and encourage—with great patience and careful instruction” (2 Timothy 4:2). Ministers cannot dilute their message for the sake of making the message tolerable.

Because ministers are unable to empirically prove their message through science and are unwilling to make their message so universally appealing that there is no longer any conviction of sin, they will not always be popular. Ministers committed to biblical preaching must come to grips with this and, like Paul, make the personal declaration, “I am not ashamed of the gospel, because it is the power of God for the salvation of everyone who believes” (Romans 1:16). At the same time, preachers cannot simply be dogmatic in their approach. They need to have humility, love, genuine interest, sincerity, and the willingness to listen to other beliefs while people search for truth. As Paul so aptly says, without love, words, faith, works, and sacrifice are nothing, gain nothing, and leave us simply making noise (1 Corinthians 13:1–3).

Second, ministers can no longer assume everyone has the same worldview. Modern people look to the church for morality. Postmodernists look to personal conviction for morality. People’s backgrounds, experience, and sometimes age will color the lenses through which they look at and interact with the world. As we will discuss in chapter 6, ministers can no longer assume people are at the same starting place when presented with Christianity. If the existence of God is questioned, how much more are His commands, the supernatural, and the Cross?

Rather than starting with a Christian worldview, preachers must now start with human needs. Preachers should start with something science cannot fix, something truth-searchers long to know. After identifying the need, ministers show the options that answer that need, with God being the best solution. Sermons cannot simply prove God; they must lead people to God.

Third, ministers have to rely on the power of the Holy Spirit to make the gospel understandable, appealing, and acceptable. Johnston (2001, 62) says, “If the preacher relinquishes the reliance upon the supernatural presence of the living Word, no cultural insight can fill that void. Either God has spoken and still speaks to the hearts of people or preachers are to be pitied above all ” (italics added). If ministers truly believe “Jesus Christ is the same yesterday and today and forever” (Hebrews 13:8), then they have to trust that His Word still captivates the hearts and minds of people—modern and postmodern. All of the proofs and truth searches will only get people so far. The Holy Spirit will do what ministers cannot do and bridge the gap from truth to each person’s realization of truth.

**Q:** How have you personally seen the shift from modernism to postmodernism affect the way people view truth and spirituality in your context or ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Why is the Holy Spirit vital for preaching?

**A:** Only the Holy Spirit can overcome the barriers in people’s lives and bring the ultimate conviction that God’s way is the right way. Without the Spirit, sermons are hollow, powerless, and unable to overcome the questions and doubts that people have.

## 2 - Biblical Authority

### The Preacher’s Foundation

#### 2.1.1 — Define God-breathed and explain what it says about Scripture.

The starting point for understanding Scripture is recognizing that it comes from God himself. Because God is its source, the Bible carries His authority and accomplishes His purposes. It reflects His perfect character, reveals His trustworthy nature, and faithfully communicates His message to humanity.

Define God-breathed and explain what it says about Scripture.

The Sovereign Word

In 2 Timothy 3:16–17, Paul writes, “All Scripture is God-breathed,” grounding the authority of Scripture not merely in its usefulness, but in its very origin. The phrase “God-breathed” (Greek theopneustos ) points to the divine source of Scripture—God himself is the One who breathes it out. This is not simply saying that Scripture is inspired in a general or emotional sense, but that it proceeds from the very life and breath of God. Just as breath carries one’s words, Scripture carries the very words and intentions of God. Paul’s emphasis is comprehensive—“all Scripture” indicates that Scripture’s divine origin shapes all the biblical witness, not just select portions.

At the same time, Paul highlights the purpose of Scripture: it is “useful” for teaching, rebuking, correcting, and training in righteousness. Because Scripture comes from God, it has the authority to shape belief (teaching), confront error (rebuking), restore what is broken (correcting), and form a life that reflects God’s character (training). The goal is deeply pastoral and practical “so that the servant of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work.” Scripture is not merely information to be studied, but formation for a life lived in obedience to God.

This divine origin also proves the trustworthiness of Scripture. Because God is truthful and unchanging, His Word reflects His character—it does not mislead, contradict, or fail. The human authors wrote in their own voices and contexts, yet the Holy Spirit superintended their writing so that what they produced faithfully communicates God’s intended message. For the preacher, this means standing on a firm foundation. The authority of Scripture is not derived from culture, preference, or persuasion, but from God himself. This means it provides both the confidence to proclaim and the power to transform lives.

Explain why a preacher can trust in the authority of the Bible.

Biblical Authority Is Trustworthy

In the present world, ministers are faced with the challenge of helping people discover truth. Modern people want verifiable proofs to convince them the Bible is true. Postmodern people are less apt to accept the Bible’s authority without a long, hard search filled with questions. These moments of uncertainty and the questions unbelievers and believers present to ministers can be daunting. It is not always popular to stand behind the Bible as the ultimate authority. So why should a minister trust the authority of the Bible?

First, it’s important to examine how the Bible understands itself. As noted above, the Bible sees itself as God’s Word because Scripture recognizes its origin in God. Crucially, the Bible understands the writings of the Old Testament and the New Testament have their source in God. In the New Testament, Jesus and the apostles all speak of the Old Testament as the words of God. They studied and memorized the text of the Old Testament intensely as Scripture. The apostle Paul read the Old Testament as a revelation of a God’s loving, trustworthy character and as an unfolding story of redemption that the Church is participating in (Hays 2005, xvi). During His temptation, Jesus used Scripture to fight Satan, demonstrating His trust in Scripture as true, useful, and an accurate reflection of God’s character. In Acts 1:16, Peter declares that Scripture, “which the Holy Spirit spoke long ago through the mouth of David,” had been fulfilled. David had penned the words, but it was really the Holy Spirit who authored them. The New Testament as well as the Old Testament are considered to be Scripture. The clearest example comes from 2 Peter. Peter considers Paul’s letters to bear the same authority as other Scripture. In 2 Peter 3:16, he says, “[Paul’s] letters contain some things that are hard to understand, which ignorant and unstable people distort, as they do the other Scriptures, to their own destruction.” By linking Paul’s writing with other Scripture, Peter places Paul’s letters on equal plane with the Old Testament. Their letters and narratives were held as sacred texts from the beginning of the Church (deSilva 2018, 4–8). Though each biblical author writes with specific purpose and with a unique perspective, the stories, doctrines, and claims they make are consistent from Genesis to Revelation because they all speak to God working out His redemptive plan with, through, and for humanity (Kaiser 2008, 19).

But how does this grand narrative of redemption resonate with modernists and postmodernists? Bob Greene (2025) advocates for preachers to lean into the Bible’s overarching narrative for postmodern audiences. He writes that theologians and preachers who embrace the Bible’s story as a method for preaching move beyond explaining the text to “inviting congregations into the redemptive narrative as active participants rather than passive spectators” (Greene 2025, 11). This is the move from learning something true to taking part in something true. Christopher Watkin, a scholar of the development of modern European philosophies, penned Biblical Critical Theory (2022) to demonstrate how the story of the Bible provides the best lens for understanding the world (xvii–xix). This thorough work uses the stories, ideas, and metaphors of the Bible to show how God’s story of redemption makes sense of life’s experience amid competing modern (and postmodern) ideas. Furthermore, a point of confidence that appeals to values of modernism is how little the text of the Bible has changed. Over thousands of years, the text of the Bible has enjoyed a stable history with relatively little variation. There are few variances between biblical manuscripts and those few differences typically resulted from copyist errors which are common in scribal communities (Klein et al. 2017, 184–187). In addition to this, it should be noted that the New Testament writers gathered their data from the oral tradition of the eyewitnesses of Jesus and their main goal was to accurately preserve the teaching of the apostles (Keener 2014, 39; deSilva 2018, 4). By rigorous, empirical standards, which appeal to modernists, the Bible is one of the best preserved books in the world (Klein et al. 2017, 187). This means that the Bible’s fundamental text and story have not been changed over the years.

For preachers, the most important thing to remember is the Bible’s trustworthiness stems from Scripture being a God-breathed invitation to God’s story of redemption. Therefore, people will only be ultimately convinced of the Bible’s authority through the Holy Spirit. Though this will be discussed further at the end of this chapter, it is important to understand now that the Holy Spirit is the One who leads God’s people into truth (John 16:13). Sin has distorted people’s ability to know and understand God. Paul says that while we live on this earth, we see “but a poor reflection as in a mirror” (1 Corinthians 13:12). Because of our faults and weaknesses, and because we are finite creatures created by God, we cannot fully understand God or His ways. God knew we would need help to live this life, so He gave us a written Word that helps us to know Him. However, because we have sin in our lives, we will sometimes doubt what we read and / or not understand what God is trying to say through His Word. To aid us in knowing Him through His Word, Jesus sent us the Holy Spirit as our guide. The Holy Spirit bridges the gap and helps people to see and know God and His Word.

Have you ever had a time when you read the Bible and felt God speak to you? Have you needed God’s help, and when you prayed about the matter, God brought Scripture to your mind that gave you the answer you needed? Has Scripture burned in your heart and felt alive as you read it? Has a Scripture guided your thoughts and given you comfort, help, joy, and strength that brought you through a tough time? Why have you believed the Bible and thought it was helpful and powerful in your life? Trusting the Bible is not something that comes out of our humanness. The Holy Spirit is the One who makes God’s Word real and alive. His conviction will bring other people the assurance and confidence that God’s Word is true. The Holy Spirit makes God’s Word knowable, understandable, and convincing, and as much as we would like it, nothing ministers can say or do will ultimately bring people to a place of accepting the Bible’s authority. The Holy Spirit is the One at work in people’s hearts and will bring the truth home.

If the work of convincing belongs to the Holy Spirit, does that mean ministers have no role to play? Not at all. Scripture calls us to be faithful stewards of what we have been given. The Holy Spirit is the one who brings conviction and opens hearts to the truth, but He does so through the faithful proclamation of His Word. This means ministers are called to study diligently, handle the Scriptures with care, and present them with clarity, humility, and integrity.

There is a beautiful partnership at work here. We do our part, praying, preparing, and preaching as faithfully as we can, and we trust God to do His part. We cannot change hearts, but we can faithfully sow the seed. And as we do, we can rest in the confidence that the Holy Spirit is already at work, making the truth of Scripture come alive in the hearts of those who hear it. This frees us from the pressure to convince or perform and instead invites us to minister with both diligence and deep trust. As we share the love and hope found in God’s redemptive plan, we can be assured that God himself is working in ways we cannot see, drawing people to himself.

Identify key verses that support the authority of Scripture.

Supporting Biblical Authority

Scripture itself affirms its authority and power in several key ways:

The Bible guides our lives.

According to Psalm 119, those who hide God’s Word in their hearts are guided in their steps and kept from sin.

Scripture becomes a steady light, directing daily decisions and shaping a life of obedience.

The Bible strengthens us against temptation.

Jesus Christ modeled this in the wilderness, resisting temptation by quoting Scripture.

In Deuteronomy 8:3, He declares, “Man does not live on bread alone but on every word that comes from the mouth of the Lord.”

God’s Word is not just truth—it is spiritual nourishment and defense.

The Bible brings life and transformation.

In John 6:63, Jesus teaches that His words are “spirit and . . . life.”

Scripture is not passive; it carries the life-giving power of God into the hearts of those who receive it.

The Bible is the source of truth and sanctification.

In John 17:17, Jesus prays, “Sanctify them by the truth; your word is truth.”

God’s Word sets believers apart and forms them into Christlikeness.

The Bible equips and grounds the believer.

As seen in 2 Timothy 3:16–17, all Scripture is “God-breathed” and useful for teaching, correcting, and training in righteousness.

It provides the foundation for sound doctrine and godly living.

The Bible is a weapon in spiritual battle.

In Ephesians 6:17, Paul describes the Word of God as the “sword of the Spirit.”

Just as a soldier depends on a weapon, the believer relies on Scripture for spiritual victory.

The Bible is living and convicting.

The author of Hebrews teaches that God’s Word is “living and active . . . sharper than any double-edged sword.”

It penetrates the heart, reveals truth, and brings conviction that leads to repentance.

The Bible is invaluable to both the minister and those to whom the minister preaches. The Bible directs people in how to live and what to believe. According to Psalm 119, those who hide the Word in their hearts are guided in their steps and led away from sin. Jesus showed an example of this during His temptation in the wilderness. Three times He was tempted, and each time He quoted Scripture to resist the temptation. Quoting Deuteronomy 8:3, Jesus even told Satan, “Man does not live on bread alone but on every word that comes from the mouth of the Lord.” Later, after preaching to the crowds that followed Him, Jesus said, “The words I have spoken to you are spirit and they are life” (John 6:63). Following this, in John 17:17, Jesus prayed for all believers and made this request and declaration: “Sanctify them by the truth; your word is truth.”

As has already been mentioned in 2 Timothy 3:16–17, Paul declares all Scripture to be “God-breathed” and helpful in establishing doctrine, making judgments, and living a godly life. Paul also attributes great power to the Bible and says it is part of a Christian’s armor when heading into spiritual battle. No soldier is complete without a weapon, and for the believer, Paul says this weapon is the “sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God” (Ephesians 6:17). The author of Hebrews supports Paul’s “sword” and says God’s Word is sharper than a two-edged sword. It is an instrument that is living and active, and works to judge people’s hearts and bring conviction that leads to repentance.

**Q:** What does God-breathed mean?

**A:** The Bible’s origin is in God himself and proceeds from His very being.

**Q:** Where does our ability to trust the Bible come from?

**A:** This ability does not come from our humanness. The Holy Spirit helps us to trust God’s Word as He makes it real and alive to us.

**Q:** Name three ways the Bible affirms its own authority.

**A:** It declares itself to be God’s Word (2 Timothy 3:16–17). Jesus affirmed its truth and authority (John 17:17). It describes its power to transform lives (Ephesians 6:17; Hebrews 4:12).

#### 2.1.2 — Explain why a preacher can trust in the authority of the Bible.

The starting point for understanding Scripture is recognizing that it comes from God himself. Because God is its source, the Bible carries His authority and accomplishes His purposes. It reflects His perfect character, reveals His trustworthy nature, and faithfully communicates His message to humanity.

Define God-breathed and explain what it says about Scripture.

The Sovereign Word

In 2 Timothy 3:16–17, Paul writes, “All Scripture is God-breathed,” grounding the authority of Scripture not merely in its usefulness, but in its very origin. The phrase “God-breathed” (Greek theopneustos ) points to the divine source of Scripture—God himself is the One who breathes it out. This is not simply saying that Scripture is inspired in a general or emotional sense, but that it proceeds from the very life and breath of God. Just as breath carries one’s words, Scripture carries the very words and intentions of God. Paul’s emphasis is comprehensive—“all Scripture” indicates that Scripture’s divine origin shapes all the biblical witness, not just select portions.

At the same time, Paul highlights the purpose of Scripture: it is “useful” for teaching, rebuking, correcting, and training in righteousness. Because Scripture comes from God, it has the authority to shape belief (teaching), confront error (rebuking), restore what is broken (correcting), and form a life that reflects God’s character (training). The goal is deeply pastoral and practical “so that the servant of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work.” Scripture is not merely information to be studied, but formation for a life lived in obedience to God.

This divine origin also proves the trustworthiness of Scripture. Because God is truthful and unchanging, His Word reflects His character—it does not mislead, contradict, or fail. The human authors wrote in their own voices and contexts, yet the Holy Spirit superintended their writing so that what they produced faithfully communicates God’s intended message. For the preacher, this means standing on a firm foundation. The authority of Scripture is not derived from culture, preference, or persuasion, but from God himself. This means it provides both the confidence to proclaim and the power to transform lives.

Explain why a preacher can trust in the authority of the Bible.

And as we do, we can rest in the confidence that the Holy Spirit is already at work, making the truth of Scripture come alive in the hearts of those who hear it. This frees us from the pressure to convince or perform and instead invites us to minister with both diligence and deep trust. As we share the love and hope found in God’s redemptive plan, we can be assured that God himself is working in ways we cannot see, drawing people to himself.

Identify key verses that support the authority of Scripture.

Supporting Biblical Authority

Scripture itself affirms its authority and power in several key ways:

The Bible guides our lives.

According to Psalm 119, those who hide God’s Word in their hearts are guided in their steps and kept from sin.

Scripture becomes a steady light, directing daily decisions and shaping a life of obedience.

The Bible strengthens us against temptation.

Jesus Christ modeled this in the wilderness, resisting temptation by quoting Scripture.

In Deuteronomy 8:3, He declares, “Man does not live on bread alone but on every word that comes from the mouth of the Lord.”

God’s Word is not just truth—it is spiritual nourishment and defense.

The Bible brings life and transformation.

In John 6:63, Jesus teaches that His words are “spirit and . . . life.”

Scripture is not passive; it carries the life-giving power of God into the hearts of those who receive it.

The Bible is the source of truth and sanctification.

In John 17:17, Jesus prays, “Sanctify them by the truth; your word is truth.”

God’s Word sets believers apart and forms them into Christlikeness.

The Bible equips and grounds the believer.

As seen in 2 Timothy 3:16–17, all Scripture is “God-breathed” and useful for teaching, correcting, and training in righteousness.

It provides the foundation for sound doctrine and godly living.

The Bible is a weapon in spiritual battle.

In Ephesians 6:17, Paul describes the Word of God as the “sword of the Spirit.”

Just as a soldier depends on a weapon, the believer relies on Scripture for spiritual victory.

The Bible is living and convicting.

The author of Hebrews teaches that God’s Word is “living and active . . . sharper than any double-edged sword.”

It penetrates the heart, reveals truth, and brings conviction that leads to repentance.

The Bible is invaluable to both the minister and those to whom the minister preaches. The Bible directs people in how to live and what to believe. According to Psalm 119, those who hide the Word in their hearts are guided in their steps and led away from sin. Jesus showed an example of this during His temptation in the wilderness. Three times He was tempted, and each time He quoted Scripture to resist the temptation. Quoting Deuteronomy 8:3, Jesus even told Satan, “Man does not live on bread alone but on every word that comes from the mouth of the Lord.” Later, after preaching to the crowds that followed Him, Jesus said, “The words I have spoken to you are spirit and they are life” (John 6:63). Following this, in John 17:17, Jesus prayed for all believers and made this request and declaration: “Sanctify them by the truth; your word is truth.”

As has already been mentioned in 2 Timothy 3:16–17, Paul declares all Scripture to be “God-breathed” and helpful in establishing doctrine, making judgments, and living a godly life. Paul also attributes great power to the Bible and says it is part of a Christian’s armor when heading into spiritual battle. No soldier is complete without a weapon, and for the believer, Paul says this weapon is the “sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God” (Ephesians 6:17). The author of Hebrews supports Paul’s “sword” and says God’s Word is sharper than a two-edged sword. It is an instrument that is living and active, and works to judge people’s hearts and bring conviction that leads to repentance.

**Q:** What does God-breathed mean?

**A:** The Bible’s origin is in God himself and proceeds from His very being.

**Q:** Where does our ability to trust the Bible come from?

**A:** This ability does not come from our humanness. The Holy Spirit helps us to trust God’s Word as He makes it real and alive to us.

**Q:** Name three ways the Bible affirms its own authority.

**A:** It declares itself to be God’s Word (2 Timothy 3:16–17). Jesus affirmed its truth and authority (John 17:17). It describes its power to transform lives (Ephesians 6:17; Hebrews 4:12).

### The Word as Power

#### 2.2.1 — Describe the role of the Word in salvation and in daily life.

The Bible in Salvation

Because of its authority, truthfulness, and trustworthiness, the Bible has great power to work in people’s lives. People find salvation through the presentation of God’s Word, one-on-one or in group settings. Paul asks the questions in Romans 10:14: “How, then, can [people] call on the one they have not believed in? And how can they believe in the one of whom they have not heard? And how can they hear without someone preaching to them?” He asks these questions to stress the importance of people presenting God’s Word for salvation to occur. He concludes, “Faith comes from hearing the message, and the message is heard through the word of Christ” (Romans 10:17). Paul firmly believed in the powerful role of God’s Word in salvation. He declares to Timothy in 2 Timothy 3:15: “You have known the holy Scriptures, which are able to make you wise for salvation through faith in Christ Jesus.”

Describe the role of the Word in salvation and in daily life.

Why is the Bible so pivotal in the work of salvation? Without the Bible, humans would not know the story of how God created the world and planned to save it. We would walk aimlessly through this world with nothing to guide us. Menzies and Horton (1993, 21) explain that only the Bible contains the special revelation necessary to bring salvation. This revelation is “the wonderful news that God has invaded the human situation, acted to redeem us, and offered a way by which we can enter into that redemption.” John boldly states this purpose in his New Testament book, “These [miracles] are written that you may believe that Jesus is the Christ, the Son of God, and that by believing you may have life in his name” (John 20:31). The Bible reveals to us the story of Jesus; as we learn His story, the Holy Spirit can work in our hearts and make what we read become a living faith.

In Daily Life

Not only does the Bible play a huge part in bringing people into salvation, it also plays an equally significant role in helping Christians live a godly life. Scripture is useful for “teaching, rebuking, correcting and training in righteousness, so that the man [or woman] of God may be thoroughly equipped for every good work” (2 Timothy 3:16–17). God’s Word lays out the plan for how Christians are to live. When situations arise that cause Christians to question what to do, they can turn to the Bible and find instructions that will help them live the correct way. The Bible gives stories that teach people how to live in love, mercy, and compassion for others. There are sections that give commands and rules by which to live. There are verses that teach about right attitudes and motives. Knowing God’s Word helps Christians be strong during temptation, know truth from error, and live lives that honor God and witness to others. Without the Bible, Christians would have no clear direction for how they should live and act. The Bible is important for every step in one’s spiritual journey, from the working of salvation to daily life in Him.

**Q:** In 2 Timothy 3:15, what does Paul say the Bible is able to do?

**A:** He said that the Bible has a powerful role in salvation, that it is able to make a person wise enough to receive salvation.

**Q:** How, specifically, does a knowledge of God’s Word help Christians?

**A:** The Bible helps Christians to be strong in temptation, to know truth from error, and to live lives that honor God and witness to others.

### Biblical Mystery and Authority

#### 2.3.1 — Explain why the Bible does not explain everything there is to know.

Mysteries Withheld

In Isaiah 55:8–9, God declares, “My thoughts are not your thoughts, neither are your ways my ways . . . As the heavens are higher than the earth, so are my ways higher than your ways and my thoughts than your thoughts.” Even John records in Revelation that some of the great wonders he saw during his vision were to remain secret until God decided to let everyone know all the mysteries of the end-time.

Explain why the Bible does not explain everything there is to know.

The idea that an infinite God would care so deeply about His creation that He stepped into our limited, human world to save us is a profound mystery. It’s something Christians don’t fully understand, but believe by faith and through the conviction of the Holy Spirit. Life in itself is a great mystery and though, through His Word, God gives people guiding principles for each situation in life, He does not always give direct commands or explanations about where to go, what to do, and why. If the Bible explained everything, people would have no need for faith and would have no reason to believe and trust in the God who is higher than them. The Bible reveals great mysteries—creation, incarnation, Trinity, atonement, transformation, etc.—that leave people in wonder and remind them of their need for the One who is infinitely greater, stronger, and more knowledgeable than they are (McLaren 2000, 78–79). Paul exclaims in Romans 11:33–36,

Oh, the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable his judgments, and his paths beyond tracing out! “Who has known the mind of the Lord? Or who has been his counselor?” “Who has ever given to God, that God should repay him?” For from him and through him and to him are all things. To him be the glory forever! Amen.

The fact that the Bible leaves some things a mystery is actually good for the postmodern mind. Brian McLaren says postmodern people crave the ability to think through issues rather than being given simplistic answers to difficult life questions. Rather than trying to find answers for all of life’s questions, ministers should point postmodernists to the Bible and say, “Here’s the place to work with your questions, live your questions, explore possible answers, and find direction to live by” (McLaren 2000, 78). The Bible teaches about faith and speaks about mysteries, and “life,” says McLaren, “is at heart a mystery to be explored, using faith” (2000, 79). Though the Bible will not always give the most detailed description and explanation for the questions seekers ask, it will always point people in the right direction, and be the perfect place to learn about the mysteries of God, discover truth, and journey in their faith. In this type of journey, ministers can always trust the Holy Spirit to reveal spiritual truths that are not part of people’s natural thoughts. Paul explains this well in 1 Corinthians 2:6–15. Mystery is exciting and full of opportunities to share the gospel.

Explain the Holy Spirit’s role in biblical authority.

The Spirit and the Word

The Holy Spirit has been involved with the Word since its beginning. The Holy Spirit influenced the writers, called things to mind, explained events, and guided their words. “God superintended their writing so that what they wrote comprised his message precisely” (Klein et al. 2017, 213). This Holy Spirit’s directing of the Scripture writers is called inspiration . God, through the Holy Spirit, breathed on the writers and influenced them to write accurately and with revelation.

The Holy Spirit’s role in the Bible has not ended with the completion of the Bible. He is still actively involved with it, making the Word known to people today. This illuminating work of the Holy Spirit helps people reading the Bible to understand its words, comprehend some of the mysteries it presents, and believe with great assurance that the words in the Bible are true. The Holy Spirit does not bring new revelation in addition to Scripture, but rather makes Scripture alive and comprehendible for those who read it. Ministers begin their sermon preparation by actively listening to the Holy Spirit as they read, study, and contemplate the Bible. The Holy Spirit makes the Bible alive and powerful, giving the minister authority in presenting God’s Word and helping listeners understand what is preached. The Holy Spirit cannot be separated from the Bible’s authority and power today.

The Holy Spirit is a powerful player in connecting biblical authority with individual people. Peter says in 2 Peter 1:20–21, “Above all, you must understand that no prophecy of Scripture came about by the prophet’s own interpretation. For prophecy never had its origin in the will of man, but men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit.” The Holy Spirit is the One who makes God’s Word alive in the hearts of those who hear it. Throughout the New Testament, He has been identified as the One who reveals God’s truth. Following are several instances:

“Spirit of truth” (John 14:17)

“The Counselor . . . whom the Father will send in my name, will teach you all things and will remind you of everything I have said to you.” (John 14:26)

“The Spirit of truth . . . will testify about me.” (John 15:26)

“Spirit of truth will . . . guide you into all truth.” (John 16:13)

The Holy Spirit breaks through the sinful mind to bring understanding and revelation. Without the work of the Spirit, people’s minds and hearts would remain in bondage to sin and be unable to truly hear, understand, and believe the good news.

The Bible is a powerful instrument for ministers. Knowing and understanding its authority and truthfulness makes presenting a challenging, life-changing message to the contemporary world possible. Preachers can trust the Bible. Haddon Robinson (2001, 82) says, “A statement is not true because it is in the Bible; it is in the Bible because it is true . . . The Bible states reality as it exists in the universe, as God has made it and as He governs it.” The Bible is God’s Word and can be embraced as fully truthful and fully trustworthy, and it is through the working of the Holy Spirit that each person can come to this realization.

Explain the importance of prayer in sermon preparation.

Praying Before Preaching

No matter how prepared a leader feels, preaching does not work without prayer. Paul often asked for prayer from the churches that his preaching would be bold and effective (Ephesians 6:19).

If preaching were simply dispensing content or information about God, it may not require much prayer. However, every time there is opportunity to share God’s Word, there is a supernatural component. Sermons are not meant to only inform, but truly to transform. Great content, incredible delivery, and big enthusiasm can undoubtedly hold the audience’s attention, but the goal is not to just entertain the listeners, but to take part in the work God intends to do through His Word. A pastor’s inspiring speech cannot heal marriages, overcome addictions, or bring real repentance. However, the Holy Spirit can and will do all that work and more! The pastor is simply the vessel being used as a conduit of God’s agenda.

Below are some prayers to use when praying through sermons.

1. Jesus, guard my lips.

Psalm 141:3 says, “Set a guard over my mouth, O Lord; keep watch over the door of my lips.” Each time you share God’s Word, ask Him to guard every word out of your mouth. Often the tongue can get ahead of the mind. We can blurt out underdeveloped thoughts, half ideas, or unclear statements. A quick tongue and a weak filter can be dangerous when teaching the Scripture. A seemingly innocent “off the cuff” comment said in a moment can create a mess that takes much longer to rectify.

Another potential pitfall is saying the right thing but in the wrong way. How you say what you say is as important as what you say. This is critical when preaching so the heart of God is on display. When we repeat what God said in a harsh or angry tone, we misrepresent the heart of the Father. Jesus said many hard things, but mirroring the tone in the text is vital to communicating His main points.

The more times you preach, the more opportunity there is for this type of error. The goal is to speak biblically and sensitively all the time. God can sanctify our words and guard our lips as we ask Him to help us.

2. Jesus, prepare the listener.

God can use your obedience of speaking to send a personalized message to any of the listeners. It’s important to pray for ears to hear what God is saying through the message and outside of the message. God may want to say something through one story, one word, or one Scripture. Surrender to God each part of your message. He has the right to use your words in any way He sees fit.

In the parable of the sower in Matthew 13, the Scripture highlights something very humbling for preachers to understand. This parable teaches us that not everyone will be receptive to the Word of God. No matter how it is said or how exciting it is presented, some will respond, and some will not. John 6:44 reminds us, “No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him.” The receptivity of the heart of the listener rests in the hands of Jesus. This idea should motivate a preacher to pray constantly.

3. Jesus, eliminate distractions.

There are always so many possible distractions. Temperature, coughing, cell phones buzzing, loud trucks outside, tripped smoke alarms, etc. It may all seem random, but distractions are actually a gimmick the enemy uses to make people lose focus. It is a plot of the enemy to take the listener’s mind and heart off of the work God is doing.

Potiphar’s wife tried to distract Joseph from his purposes. However, Joseph recognized this distraction and rejected her. He was able to stay focused on the destiny and calling God had for him. Unfortunately, Esau didn’t have the same story. Esau’s rumbling stomach distracted him so much he fell victim to a temptation. He eventually exchanged his own birthright for a bowl of pottage.

The Spirit of God can identify distractions in our lives and drain them of power. We can take every thought captive and keep focused on Jesus Christ. Pray for you and the listeners to have an armor of protection against distractions. Pray that everything would come under the submission and authority of Jesus during the time the Word is being delivered.

4. Jesus, show me the next step.

Ending each sermon with a call to action gives the listener a clear next step. A sermon can end with many different actions, but asking God for the specific one He wants you to execute is imperative. The Word of God is living and active and calls for a response every time we hear or read it.

Some examples of next steps include: a call to salvation, signing up to be part of a small group, joining a serving team, inviting a friend, engaging in reading specific Scriptures, focused prayer time, giving of resources, and more.

Isaiah 55:11 teaches that every God-inspired word that is preached or taught will not create an empty return. Each sermon preached, under the submission of God, will accomplish everything it purposes. It’s amazing to think there has never been, and will never be, a sermon preached when the Word didn’t do the work God intended it to do. The Scripture softens, converts, convicts, sharpens, rebukes, and transforms. We can trust that God’s Word is moving us forward. The preacher’s job is to find the specific step He is asking each person to take and challenge the listener with it.

5. Spend time praying in the Spirit.

For at least five minutes per message, lay your hands on the papers it is written on and pray in the Spirit. Ask God to show you if anything needs to be changed, adjusted, or deleted. Ask that God would fill the gaps with His Spirit. Ask God for any words of wisdom or prophecy He would want to add to the message.

Romans 8:26 reminds us that praying in tongues helps us when we don’t know how to pray. We won’t always know all the things we need to bring before the Lord before we preach, but the Holy Spirit does. He can navigate circumstances that we may never see. He can work against the enemy and all that comes against the powerful name of Jesus. He can cover all our weaknesses and insecurities as we pray in tune with His heart.

**Q:** What does Brian McLaren say that postmodern people crave?

**A:** He says that they crave the ability to think through issues rather than being given simplistic answers to life’s difficult questions. Therefore, ministers should present the Bible as the place to explore answers and find direction for living.

**Q:** How should ministers begin their sermon preparation?

**A:** They should begin by actively listening to the Holy Spirit as they read, study, and contemplate the Bible.

Life in itself is a great mystery and though, through His Word, God gives people guiding principles for each situation in life, He does not always give direct commands or explanations about where to go, what to do, and why. If the Bible explained everything, people would have no need for faith and would have no reason to believe and trust in the God who is higher than them. The Bible reveals great mysteries—creation, incarnation, Trinity, atonement, transformation, etc.—that leave people in wonder and remind them of their need for the One who is infinitely greater, stronger, and more knowledgeable than they are (McLaren 2000, 78–79). Paul exclaims in Romans 11:33–36,

Oh, the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable his judgments, and his paths beyond tracing out! “Who has known the mind of the Lord? Or who has been his counselor?” “Who has ever given to God, that God should repay him?” For from him and through him and to him are all things. To him be the glory forever! Amen.

The fact that the Bible leaves some things a mystery is actually good for the postmodern mind. Brian McLaren says postmodern people crave the ability to think through issues rather than being given simplistic answers to difficult life questions. Rather than trying to find answers for all of life’s questions, ministers should point postmodernists to the Bible and say, “Here’s the place to work with your questions, live your questions, explore possible answers, and find direction to live by” (McLaren 2000, 78). The Bible teaches about faith and speaks about mysteries, and “life,” says McLaren, “is at heart a mystery to be explored, using faith” (2000, 79). Though the Bible will not always give the most detailed description and explanation for the questions seekers ask, it will always point people in the right direction, and be the perfect place to learn about the mysteries of God, discover truth, and journey in their faith. In this type of journey, ministers can always trust the Holy Spirit to reveal spiritual truths that are not part of people’s natural thoughts. Paul explains this well in 1 Corinthians 2:6–15. Mystery is exciting and full of opportunities to share the gospel.

Explain the Holy Spirit’s role in biblical authority.

The Spirit and the Word

The Holy Spirit has been involved with the Word since its beginning. The Holy Spirit influenced the writers, called things to mind, explained events, and guided their words. “God superintended their writing so that what they wrote comprised his message precisely” (Klein et al. 2017, 213). This Holy Spirit’s directing of the Scripture writers is called inspiration . God, through the Holy Spirit, breathed on the writers and influenced them to write accurately and with revelation.

The Holy Spirit’s role in the Bible has not ended with the completion of the Bible. He is still actively involved with it, making the Word known to people today. This illuminating work of the Holy Spirit helps people reading the Bible to understand its words, comprehend some of the mysteries it presents, and believe with great assurance that the words in the Bible are true. The Holy Spirit does not bring new revelation in addition to Scripture, but rather makes Scripture alive and comprehendible for those who read it. Ministers begin their sermon preparation by actively listening to the Holy Spirit as they read, study, and contemplate the Bible. The Holy Spirit makes the Bible alive and powerful, giving the minister authority in presenting God’s Word and helping listeners understand what is preached. The Holy Spirit cannot be separated from the Bible’s authority and power today.

The Holy Spirit is a powerful player in connecting biblical authority with individual people. Peter says in 2 Peter 1:20–21, “Above all, you must understand that no prophecy of Scripture came about by the prophet’s own interpretation. For prophecy never had its origin in the will of man, but men spoke from God as they were carried along by the Holy Spirit.” The Holy Spirit is the One who makes God’s Word alive in the hearts of those who hear it. Throughout the New Testament, He has been identified as the One who reveals God’s truth. Following are several instances:

“Spirit of truth” (John 14:17)

“The Counselor . . . whom the Father will send in my name, will teach you all things and will remind you of everything I have said to you.” (John 14:26)

“The Spirit of truth . . . will testify about me.” (John 15:26)

“Spirit of truth will . . . guide you into all truth.” (John 16:13)

The Holy Spirit breaks through the sinful mind to bring understanding and revelation. Without the work of the Spirit, people’s minds and hearts would remain in bondage to sin and be unable to truly hear, understand, and believe the good news.

The Bible is a powerful instrument for ministers. Knowing and understanding its authority and truthfulness makes presenting a challenging, life-changing message to the contemporary world possible. Preachers can trust the Bible. Haddon Robinson (2001, 82) says, “A statement is not true because it is in the Bible; it is in the Bible because it is true . . . The Bible states reality as it exists in the universe, as God has made it and as He governs it.” The Bible is God’s Word and can be embraced as fully truthful and fully trustworthy, and it is through the working of the Holy Spirit that each person can come to this realization.

Explain the importance of prayer in sermon preparation.

Praying Before Preaching

No matter how prepared a leader feels, preaching does not work without prayer. Paul often asked for prayer from the churches that his preaching would be bold and effective (Ephesians 6:19).

If preaching were simply dispensing content or information about God, it may not require much prayer. However, every time there is opportunity to share God’s Word, there is a supernatural component. Sermons are not meant to only inform, but truly to transform. Great content, incredible delivery, and big enthusiasm can undoubtedly hold the audience’s attention, but the goal is not to just entertain the listeners, but to take part in the work God intends to do through His Word. A pastor’s inspiring speech cannot heal marriages, overcome addictions, or bring real repentance. However, the Holy Spirit can and will do all that work and more! The pastor is simply the vessel being used as a conduit of God’s agenda.

Below are some prayers to use when praying through sermons.

1. Jesus, guard my lips.

Psalm 141:3 says, “Set a guard over my mouth, O Lord; keep watch over the door of my lips.” Each time you share God’s Word, ask Him to guard every word out of your mouth. Often the tongue can get ahead of the mind. We can blurt out underdeveloped thoughts, half ideas, or unclear statements. A quick tongue and a weak filter can be dangerous when teaching the Scripture. A seemingly innocent “off the cuff” comment said in a moment can create a mess that takes much longer to rectify.

Another potential pitfall is saying the right thing but in the wrong way. How you say what you say is as important as what you say. This is critical when preaching so the heart of God is on display. When we repeat what God said in a harsh or angry tone, we misrepresent the heart of the Father. Jesus said many hard things, but mirroring the tone in the text is vital to communicating His main points.

The more times you preach, the more opportunity there is for this type of error. The goal is to speak biblically and sensitively all the time. God can sanctify our words and guard our lips as we ask Him to help us.

2. Jesus, prepare the listener.

God can use your obedience of speaking to send a personalized message to any of the listeners. It’s important to pray for ears to hear what God is saying through the message and outside of the message. God may want to say something through one story, one word, or one Scripture. Surrender to God each part of your message. He has the right to use your words in any way He sees fit.

In the parable of the sower in Matthew 13, the Scripture highlights something very humbling for preachers to understand. This parable teaches us that not everyone will be receptive to the Word of God. No matter how it is said or how exciting it is presented, some will respond, and some will not. John 6:44 reminds us, “No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him.” The receptivity of the heart of the listener rests in the hands of Jesus. This idea should motivate a preacher to pray constantly.

3. Jesus, eliminate distractions.

There are always so many possible distractions. Temperature, coughing, cell phones buzzing, loud trucks outside, tripped smoke alarms, etc. It may all seem random, but distractions are actually a gimmick the enemy uses to make people lose focus. It is a plot of the enemy to take the listener’s mind and heart off of the work God is doing.

Potiphar’s wife tried to distract Joseph from his purposes. However, Joseph recognized this distraction and rejected her. He was able to stay focused on the destiny and calling God had for him. Unfortunately, Esau didn’t have the same story. Esau’s rumbling stomach distracted him so much he fell victim to a temptation. He eventually exchanged his own birthright for a bowl of pottage.

The Spirit of God can identify distractions in our lives and drain them of power. We can take every thought captive and keep focused on Jesus Christ. Pray for you and the listeners to have an armor of protection against distractions. Pray that everything would come under the submission and authority of Jesus during the time the Word is being delivered.

4. Jesus, show me the next step.

Ending each sermon with a call to action gives the listener a clear next step. A sermon can end with many different actions, but asking God for the specific one He wants you to execute is imperative. The Word of God is living and active and calls for a response every time we hear or read it.

Some examples of next steps include: a call to salvation, signing up to be part of a small group, joining a serving team, inviting a friend, engaging in reading specific Scriptures, focused prayer time, giving of resources, and more.

Isaiah 55:11 teaches that every God-inspired word that is preached or taught will not create an empty return. Each sermon preached, under the submission of God, will accomplish everything it purposes. It’s amazing to think there has never been, and will never be, a sermon preached when the Word didn’t do the work God intended it to do. The Scripture softens, converts, convicts, sharpens, rebukes, and transforms. We can trust that God’s Word is moving us forward. The preacher’s job is to find the specific step He is asking each person to take and challenge the listener with it.

5. Spend time praying in the Spirit.

For at least five minutes per message, lay your hands on the papers it is written on and pray in the Spirit. Ask God to show you if anything needs to be changed, adjusted, or deleted. Ask that God would fill the gaps with His Spirit. Ask God for any words of wisdom or prophecy He would want to add to the message.

Romans 8:26 reminds us that praying in tongues helps us when we don’t know how to pray. We won’t always know all the things we need to bring before the Lord before we preach, but the Holy Spirit does. He can navigate circumstances that we may never see. He can work against the enemy and all that comes against the powerful name of Jesus. He can cover all our weaknesses and insecurities as we pray in tune with His heart.

**Q:** What does Brian McLaren say that postmodern people crave?

**A:** He says that they crave the ability to think through issues rather than being given simplistic answers to life’s difficult questions. Therefore, ministers should present the Bible as the place to explore answers and find direction for living.

**Q:** How should ministers begin their sermon preparation?

**A:** They should begin by actively listening to the Holy Spirit as they read, study, and contemplate the Bible.

## 3 - True to the Text

### What Is the Text Saying?

#### 3.1.1 — Explain why the text should determine the preacher’s message.

The Text and the True Message

Biases are common to the human experience. Therefore, it is impossible to think that ministers do not approach the Word with their own biases. Michael Quicke (2003, 141) writes, “Preachers do not come to Scripture neutrally, as though they can dissect a text without involving their own personalities, experiences, and context . . . I cannot help but look at [the text] with my heart and my mind.” To start with one’s own opinions first and then look for a text to support them, a preacher reads into Scripture, overlooks vital facts that reveal truth, and clings to interpretations that present truth only as he or she sees it or believes it to be.

Explain why the text should determine the preacher’s message.

Rather than reading with the intent of supporting one’s presuppositions , preachers should approach God’s Word with open hearts and minds and allow the Holy Spirit to make God’s Word alive and clear. The result might be surprising. Haddon Robinson (2001, 22) says that when we engage the text, “we must be willing to reexamine our doctrinal convictions and to reject the judgments of our most respected teachers. We must make a U-turn in our own previous understandings of the Bible should these conflict with the concepts of the biblical writer.” Robinson does not say that ministers should reject everything they have been taught. Rather, he encourages ministers to face the Word of God with the understanding that everyone has biases and perspectives that come out of their experiences, their upbringing, and their culture. Instead of allowing these influences to tint one’s reading of Scripture, the minister should be open to God and allow Him to speak boldly through His Word in ways that might sometimes challenge things the minister has always believed and been taught.

Preaching should have an edge that speaks to the culture at hand, it should challenge people with the revolutionary message of God’s kingdom invading earth, and it should be grounded firmly in Scripture. When a preacher approaches the text with a personal agenda, preconceived ideas, the desire to speak a pleasing message, or a mind closed to revelation, he or she diminishes the power of Scripture and compromises truth. The text must determine the message.

Identify the steps used to determine a text’s purpose.

The Text’s Purpose

Anyone can open the Bible, read one sentence, and come up with an erroneous teaching. For example, read Genesis 22:2: “God said, ‘Take your son, your only son, Isaac, whom you love, and go to the region of Moriah. Sacrifice him there as a burnt offering on one of the mountains I will tell you about.’”

If someone simply opened the Bible and read this verse, he or she could reasonably assume that the God of the Bible requires human sacrifice as a part of one’s devotion to Him. However, when this verse is read in context , the idea of human sacrifice as a part of godly devotion is without support. A single verse, thought, or sentence is easily misunderstood without the input of context. Because of this, careful study should be made of the context surrounding the text, starting with the immediate verses and working out.

Thus, to determine a text’s purpose, several steps should be taken:

Choose a range of verses that capture a complete story or thought, ideally from both the Old and New Testaments.

Understand the chosen text within the context of the verses / chapter immediately surrounding it.

Understand the passage within the entire book.

Understand the passage within the entire Bible.

Choose a range of verses that capture a complete story or thought.

Genesis 22:1–19 is a much better text than Genesis 22:2 alone. A single verse cannot be used as the basis of a message without the consideration of the verses that surround it. Subtitles, paragraph breaks, and verse and chapter designations in most translations can be helpful, but remember that these are only scholarly attempts to break the Bible into thematic segments. You may not always agree with these preset divisions or titles. As you read the passage, watch for the flow and direction of the verses, and set your perimeters on manageable portions that capture the complete thought or narrative.

Understand the chosen text within the context of surrounding verses / chapters.

Understanding the chosen text within the context of other chapters or verses helps the preacher better grasp the author’s purpose and meaning, and see the bigger picture. For instance, while Genesis 22:2 seems to promote child sacrifice when read alone, when it is read as part of a whole story, the verse presents a vastly different idea. Verse 1 tells the reader that God is putting Abraham’s faith and obedience to a test. From the chapters surrounding the text, it is known that Isaac is the son born to Abraham and Sarah in their old age. God has promised to give Abraham an heir, make Abraham the father of many nations, and bless all the nations of the earth through a descendant that will come through Abraham’s heir. Isaac is this promised heir. No other children are born to Abraham and Sarah as a couple; no more children are promised. Isaac alone is the fulfillment of God’s promise, and God’s entire promise rests on him. When Genesis 22:2 is read within the background of God’s promise and with the understanding that God is testing Abraham, it cannot be concluded that child sacrifice is part of godly devotion. Instead, the idea in verse two is that following God requires obedience, even when it does not make sense. The sacrifice God calls for demands Abraham to give up the very promise God had given him. Will Abraham cling to the gift of God or to the God who gives? The context preceding verse two gives considerable weight and meaning to this verse; however, stopping here still does not tell the whole story. To stop at verse two would leave the impression that God is One who gives only to snatch it away for no reason. If this were true, then God’s promises would not be trustworthy. But this is not the case. God’s promises are “Yes” and “Amen” (2 Corinthians 1:20). God’s directive to Abraham only makes sense when the story is read to its conclusion.

Several things become apparent when all nineteen verses are read. First, God asks Abraham for the very promise He has given. Second, Abraham is so convinced of God’s trustworthiness, provision, and promises, that he places his faith not in Isaac (and all Isaac represents), but in God. It does not make sense to give up the promise, but Abraham is unwavering in his faith and obedience. He takes his son up the mountain to sacrifice. Third, even though Abraham is stopped before he strikes the fatal blow, God recognizes Abraham’s obedience and accepts his obedience as a completed sacrifice. Fourth, God does not demand Isaac’s life; He provides a substitute. According to Allen P. Ross (1997, 393), “In truth, Abraham had made the sacrifice. In the actual outworking of his obedience, however, an animal would be substituted for the lad.” Looking at verse two in context gives a rich understanding of the life of faith.

Understand your passage within the entire book.

Each biblical author has a purpose for his work. Robinson (2001, 87) says that to understand what a passage means, we must first “sit down before the biblical writer and try to understand what he wanted to convey to his original readers.” Today’s preachers should look at Genesis 22:1–19 in the context of the entire book. From the perspective of the whole Book of Genesis, the text shows the climax of Abraham’s faith and obedience and the continuation of God’s Genesis 3:15 promise to bring salvation to the world. Here the story of Adam and Eve ties in with Abraham’s faith. Though sin was in the world, it would be defeated through a descendant of Abraham, who would bring blessing to the entire globe. Salvation was on its way, and Abraham’s obedience shows a glimpse of how it will come to pass in the world. The Genesis author gives Christians history, an understanding of where they came from, and a look at how God has been working out salvation for us, even since the beginning of time. Seeing the passage in this framework only increases its power and punch. This leads to the last step in determining a text’s purpose.

Understand your passage within the entire Bible.

Though the Bible is made up of sixty-six books written by numerous authors of varying backgrounds, the Bible is one story. The passage that a preacher selects will fit into the entire message of Scripture. It will not contradict other parts of the Bible. It is extremely important to understand the text in light of all of Scripture; however, one caution must be given. To understand a text correctly, it must first be understood from the perspective of the original readers. Only after this is done can the whole Bible be taken into consideration. For example, in Genesis 22:1–19, the original readers would understand Abraham’s story to show the obedience that true faith in God requires, the complete surrender required for worship, and God’s perfect provision in response to faith and obedience. It is often preached that through this experience God shows Abraham a picture of how God, in the future, will willingly give Jesus as a sacrifice for humankind. It is preached that the ram is Jesus, who dies in place of sinners, and brings salvation. Original readers would not understand this. Through this passage they would see that the requirement of complete devotion to God was that nothing could be held back from God. He had to be number one. When Abraham gave everything up for God, God provided everything for him. This is the original understanding of this passage. Once this original intent is clear, the text is now examined in light of Scripture. No longer is this substitution of the ram for Isaac understood as Jesus substituting for sinners. Rather, this passage “is portraying an obedient servant worshiping God in faith at great cost, and in the final analysis, God provide[s] for the sacrifice” (Ross 1997, 393). This attitude of sacrifice carries over to the nation of Israel, where sacrifice is made for worship and sins. It is the cost—the sacrifice—that allows the worshiper acceptance with God. The worshiper does whatever God asks, and God provides for him or her. In the manner that God provides for Abraham, God provides for all of humanity. The greatest act of divine provision is that God “did not spare his own Son, but gave him up for us all” (Romans 8:32). God demands everything, but what is demanded of us will never cost us more than the cost of what God has given for us. Our cost is small compared to His provision—Jesus Christ.

Without the context of the entire Bible, this powerful portion of Scripture would be greatly reduced to a story of faith that lacks great relevance to our lives. Through understanding all of Scripture, the cost of obeying God and the provision that is guaranteed for that obedience is clearly illustrated.

**Q:** What kinds of problems can arise if a preacher does not allow the text to determine the sermon?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Can you think of another example of a verse out of context that causes a theological error?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What is revealed about Genesis 22:1–19 from studying the context of surrounding chapters?

**A:** Answers will vary. Most will deal with the promise of a son that God gave to Abraham and Sarah.

**Q:** How does Genesis 22:1–19 support the author’s purpose for the Book of Genesis?

**A:** Answers will vary. Most answers may describe the life example of Abraham and God’s promise to bring a Savior to the world to deal with sin.
