### Dangers to Avoid

#### 3.2.1 — Describe at least five ways preachers can misuse Scripture.

The Misuse of Scripture

Good intentions do not always serve the text well. Preachers can misuse Scripture in several ways. The following items are common mistakes made from the pulpit.

Describe at least five ways preachers can misuse Scripture.

Proof-Texting

Rather than allowing the text to determine the message, the preacher reverses the order and makes the text fit the message. This often happens when using topical preaching. This is called proof-texting . For example, a minister decides to preach on how God helps Christians live godly lives daily. He or she writes out the points: God gives Christians what they need every day, God gives Christians the ability to do whatever He calls them to do, and God gives Christians the strength to face trials with joy. The minister comes up with illustrations and points of application, and then comes to the Bible to look for verses to support these beliefs. For point two, this minister turns to Philippians 4:13, “I can do everything through him who gives me strength.” It is true that God gives His children strength to do what He calls them to do; however, it cannot be proved from this verse. In context, Philippians 4:13 talks about God helping Paul to be content regardless of the circumstances he faced: hunger, plenty, want, or fullness. Through Christ, Paul learned contentment and could rely on God’s strength to face any situation with a godly attitude and a heart of thankfulness.

Allegorizing

Allegorizing is another form of reading into the text. The ideas might be true, but they cannot be found in the chosen text. For example, in our sample text (Genesis 22:1–19), a preacher using allegory would say that Abraham going up Mount Moriah symbolizes our need to have a one-on-one encounter with God. The ropes with which Isaac is bound represent how sin binds us. The knife represents the death that sin causes. The ram represents Jesus dying in our place. The concepts presented are true: sin binds us and leads to death, and Jesus has died in our place, freeing us from sin. But these concepts cannot be found in this passage. The original author did not include these details to represent symbols of future salvation; these were simply details in the story of God testing Abraham’s faith and obedience. To read these kinds of allegories into the story takes away from its power and is the wrong way to explain what this passage means. As Robinson (2001, 88) says, “Commentators who use allegory deserve high marks for creativity but low marks for approaching the biblical account as literature.”

Reading into the Text

Reading into the text puts words, thoughts, and intents into the story that were never intended. In John 7:39, John assesses Jesus’ words and, having the insight into Jesus’ intent, explains: “By this he meant the Spirit.” These kinds of author insights give rich understanding and revelation; however, where these insights are not given, the preacher should not interject his or her own insights. For example, in John 8, an adulterous woman is brought before Jesus. He is asked to condemn her, but instead of bringing any charges against her, Jesus simply bends down and writes on the ground. What does He write? Scripture does not say, yet there have been numerous sermons that supposed Jesus began to write out the sins of the Pharisees standing there with stones in their hands. This makes for interesting, exciting, and climactic preaching, but it is not biblical. Ministers should be bold where the Bible is bold and silent where the Bible is silent.

Majoring on Minors

Focusing on trivial matters is another way to mishandle God’s Word. A Bible professor, while on an overseas ministry trip, was asked to teach a course on the Book of John. As he neared the end of his class, he discussed the personal way in which the risen Jesus revealed himself to His disciples. Just like He had first called His disciples by helping them make a miraculous catch of fish, Jesus revealed himself to them the same way. A student interrupted the professor with a question that was burning in his mind: “What do the 153 fish represent?” Troubled by the mention that the disciples had hauled in 153 fish, the student was unable to see the big picture because of this minor detail. The professor finally answered, “Have you ever talked to a fisherman who did not tell you exactly how many fish he caught and how big they were?” The number of fish caught was irrelevant to the point of the passage; Jesus was alive and was proving His resurrection to His disciples in a very personal, memorable way. To focus on the number of fish ignores the purpose and major point for this passage.

Missing a Major Point

This is the flip side to “majoring on minors.” Biblical writers were inspired and anointed by the Holy Spirit to write truth. The stories they include, the insights they give, and the way they piece together their writings have significance. Preachers should never throw out details that do not seem to fit their interpretation of a text’s meaning. Details are important to the purpose of the text, and throwing out what does not seem to fit can cause major pieces to be ignored. At times, Ephesians 5:22–24 is used to preach a submissive role of women in the world—women should never be the head, the leader, or independent from the rule and headship of men. Focusing on a few verses changes the message of this passage: “Submit to one another out of reverence for Christ” (Ephesians 5:21). In showing how godly lives are to be lived, Paul gives numerous examples of people in submission to one another: wives submitted to husbands (5:22–24), husbands submitted to their wives (5:25–33), children submitted to their parents (Ephesians 6:1–3), parents submitted to the well-being of their children (Ephesians 6:4), slaves submitted to their masters (Ephesians 6:5–8), and masters submitted to the godly care of their slaves (Ephesians 6:9). Focusing on the woman’s submission roles changes Paul’s point: in all relationships, Christians should reflect the honor, respect, consideration, and selflessness that they themselves have received from Christ. As imitators of God, each should be submitted to the well-being, success, and honor of others. In this way, Christ and the other persons are honored.

Ignoring Context

This has already been mentioned in the last section; however, it is worth repeating. No verse can be preached or explained outside of its context. As Haddon Robinson (2001, 57) plainly says, “However we select the passage, we must allow it to speak for itself. Often a passage will not say what we expected it to say.”

Erroneous Word Studies

Several ways of studying words can lead to error. Though the following only scratch the surface, a full listing of word study errors can be found in D. A. Carson’s Exegetical Fallacies .

a) One fallacy is using an English dictionary to explain a word rather than examining it in the original Hebrew or Greek to learn how the author used the word and desired it to be understood.

b) Another erroneous approach is looking in a lexicon (i.e., a Hebrew or Greek dictionary) and applying every possible definition of a word to the word in the text at hand. No word can mean everything at once. The word lost , for example, according to Webster’s Dictionary means: not made use of, won, or claimed; no longer possessed; no longer known; ruined or destroyed physically or morally; taken away or beyond reach or attainment; unable to find the way; and not appreciated or understood. If someone read the sentence, “I got lost in the woods” and applied every possible definition of lost to the sentence, it would mean a jumble of things that would be incorrect. The sentence clearly means I was unable to find my way in the woods. It does not, at the same time, mean I did not win, not own, not know, and not appreciate the woods. In the same way, when studying a Greek or Hebrew word in a lexicon, the reader must not apply every definition to it. It is a poor use of the lexicon and will bring a great misunderstanding of the word.

c) Creating a major point out of a word when the word is simply a variation of the same concept is also problematic. A popular misconception is that agape love and phileo love are different— agape love is a godly, Christian love; phileo love is the love in a friendship or among brothers or sisters. In actuality, these words both mean love and simply provide variety in speech, much like we do today. The full range of meaning for these words does provide some differences, but ultimately the punch of these words is that they are both ways to describe love. In contemporary novels, authors use phrases like she said , he replied , she answered , he responded , and she told in the course of dialogue to bring variety and spice. These words essentially mean the same thing; they are just varying ways to say what the author intends. The same can be said for some of the words used in Scripture. Careful exegesis will sort this out for the preacher.

d) Meaning should not be created from the root of a word. At times, the root of a word will shed light on the word’s meaning; however, great caution must be taken when looking at the root. Many words can share root letters but have no relation. For instance, in Hebrew, words are placed into family groups based on their three-root letters. While some words, like king and reign, share both meaning and the same root letters ( lm ), other words, like bread and war, share only the root letters ( lxm ) and not their meaning. To force bread to have a connection with war distorts both words and leads the preaching down a shaky path (Carson 1996, 30). In addition to this, careful study should be made of how a word changes over time. Some words can sound like English words and share root letters, but have no relation at all. For example, in Ephesians 2:10, the word poiema ( poi,hma ) means “creation” or “workmanship.” However, this word has often been preached as being the root word of the English word poem . The two look alike and it sounds great to think “we are God’s poem.” But the two words have no relation. We are God’s handicraft, His special creation, but not His poem.

Explain why preachers need determination in their commitment to speaking biblical truth.

Nothing can take the place of sound exegesis , time in study, and prayer. Stepping into a pulpit requires more than skills in public speaking. Preaching is important because it is one way God’s Word can be presented to people. When proper time, prayer, and study have been given to a sermon, a preacher can step to a pulpit in reverence and confidence, knowing God will speak to His people. Preachers need conviction, passion, and determination in their commitment to speaking God’s Word. The preacher, like Jeremiah, should be able to say, “[God’s] word is in my heart like a fire, a fire shut up in my bones. I am weary of holding it in; indeed, I cannot” (Jeremiah 20:9). Not everything the preacher says will be popular. Not everything will be easy to say. Not everything will be accepted. Graham Johnston (2001, 62) writes:

Inevitably every preacher will be required to proclaim godly truths that are counterculture, principles that run against the grain of general society. But that, remember, was the nature of following Christ. You cannot lessen the demands of authentic discipleship . . . [by] simplifying the Christian life into ten easy steps, or twisting the promises of God into Jesus’ plan for health and happiness. Distortions of God’s message, for whatever reason, are a disservice to the Lord; genuine concern for biblical integrity and one’s listeners will demand that they come to understand God’s truth and calling in all its complexity and fullness.

Paul’s admonition to Timothy is valid today: “Do your best to present yourself to God as one approved, a workman who does not need to be ashamed and who correctly handles the word of truth” (2 Timothy 2:15).

**Q:** How is the preparation for preaching different from the preparation for making a speech?

**A:** Preaching is a sacred event that requires prayer and a commitment to preach what is true, even when it is not popular. Speeches present facts and persuade people to opinions; sermons present truth and work toward eternal life change.

## 4 - Center Your Sermon

### The Power of One Idea

#### 4.1.1 — Explain the importance of focusing on a single idea in the sermon.

The Single Point

If sermons fail, it is usually not because of poor exegesis, but because they deal with too many unrelated ideas. “A sermon should be a bullet, not buckshot,” says Haddon Robinson (2001, 35). “A sermon is the explanation, interpretation, or application of a single dominant idea supported by other ideas all drawn from one passage or several passages of Scripture” (Robinson 2001, 35, italics added). This is important for several reasons. First, by focusing on one idea, preachers are less likely to misuse, misapply, and misinterpret Scripture. Second, a single point in a sermon helps listeners to hear, understand, and remember what is said. The old adage “Repetition is the key to learning” is very true. Third, preachers have the weighty task of presenting God’s Word so people can understand. Without clarity, preachers fail in this important responsibility. As preachers, you want your listeners to grasp truth from Scripture that they can apply; you do not want to leave them confused and wondering. With one point that controls the entire message, preachers are better able to give a message that is clear and easy for the congregation to understand.

Explain the importance of focusing on a single idea in the sermon.

Finding the single point or focus of a sermon is one of the most challenging aspects of sermon preparation. In many homiletics settings, students are asked to articulate the central theme of their sermon in a single sentence. This exercise often exposes a lack of clarity, as multiple ideas surface without a unifying focus. The difficulty of this task highlights an important reality: if the central theme cannot be clearly stated, it has likely not yet been clearly formed. Arriving at a concise, all-encompassing sentence typically requires intentional study, prayer, and disciplined reflection.

Though this process can feel demanding, the ability to identify and articulate a sermon’s central idea is essential for effective preaching. As Graham Johnston observes, “If you can’t identify what you’re saying in one, clear sentence, it means that you probably aren’t clear yourself. You may say some good things, but don’t be surprised when no one seems to grasp the thrust of your message” (2001, 172).

One of the most famous speeches in the United States was given by Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr. “I have a dream” was King’s resounding declaration, and everything in it related to his dream of equality among races. The focus of this speech is without question, and the impact is undeniable. Had King rambled, or put together several points, his speech would likely have been overlooked in history books. Because of King’s clear theme, his speech stands as one of the most powerful, life-changing, and poignant speeches of our time. Though not every sermon will have this kind of power in history, the sermon should have this kind of singularity of thought and impact.

Describe how a sermon’s central proposition controls the message.

The Central Proposition

Your sermon should have one central proposition that grounds everything else. Having a single point to the sermon not only aids in the clarity of the message but also helps guide the message. The central proposition (also called a theme) sets boundaries for the sermon and helps preachers know what to include and what to exclude. As you write and study your sermon, ask the question, “Does this support the main point of this sermon?” Illustrations, explanations, and applications should all be guided by the theme. Once these parts of the sermon are in line with the proposition, ask, “Will my audience understand this main point?” If there is any question about the audience’s ability to understand and grasp the point of the sermon, keep working on it. Sermons should say what the preacher means and not leave things to question. Work, struggle, wrestle, and labor to get a proposition that explains the text’s point in a simple, clear sentence; then, use this hard-earned proposition to control every aspect of the sermon.

Explain the process of finding a central proposition.

It is one thing to know a sermon needs a central proposition. It is another thing to actually come up with the proposition. The central theme of a sermon can be found in a variety of ways. If you read ten homiletics books, you will likely find ten different approaches to accomplishing this important step. Though there are more ways to complete this step, one of the best ways requires the formation of three outlines: an exegetical outline , a theological outline , and a homiletical outline .

The Exegetical Outline

An exegetical outline starts with the text under consideration. After reading the text several times in different translations and perhaps in the original language, form an outline based on what the original readers of the text would have understood. This outline is written in past tense because it looks at the text from the perspective of the past. Read Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31.

After reading this text, ask, “What would the original readers have understood this passage to mean?” From this question the exegetical outline is formed. First, come up with one sentence that summarizes the point of this passage. For Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31, the summary sentence would be something like, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines, because although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.” This is a rather long sentence, but it correctly summarizes the text’s point. Everything else in this passage should support the main idea. Each detail that sheds light on the main purpose becomes a subpoint to the main idea. For example, in the text, we read that God prepared the Israelites for battle by marching them out of Egypt like an army, but keeping them away from battle while they gained confidence. This idea of preparation is a detail that supports the main purpose, so it becomes a subpoint. The passage says that not only did God lead them like an army, but He also gave them visible signs to prove He was guiding them on the wilderness journey. Since guidance is a detail that is different from preparation, it, too, becomes a subpoint. Finally, we read that God specifically led the Israelites to the Red Sea to prove they could trust His leading. What looked like a trap became a place God demonstrated His power. Again, this idea of proving trust is different from preparation or guidance, so it also becomes a subpoint. These three ideas are the three details that support the main idea, and together they form the exegetical outline. The outline would look like this:

Proposition: God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.

1. God helped prepare Israel for battle by taking them on the long route through the desert (13:17–18).

2. God guided Israel with the pillar of fire and the pillar of cloud and taught them to trust in His guidance (13:21–22).

3. God brought them to a place where He could demonstrate His power and glory by defeating Pharaoh and his army. This would build the Israelites’ trust in God, which they would need when facing future battles (14:1–4; 14:31).

The Theological Outline

The exegetical outline simply says what the text says. However, by breaking the passage into thoughts, the preacher is able to gain some direction as to what the passage is saying. After this step is done, it is time to move on to the theological outline. The theological outline takes the exegetical outline and rewords the points and proposition to state universal, timeless truths. While the exegetical outline is written in the past tense and states what was true for the original readers, the theological outline is written in the present tense because it states what is true for all readers, both past and present. This requires a lot of work, prayer, and study, but this middle step is extremely important for arriving at a biblically sound sermon. The exegetical outline tells us that God had a reason for taking the Israelites through the wilderness. Obviously, God will not take everyone on a physical journey through a desert. However, a universal truth is found in the statement, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.” What is this truth? God leads everyone on unexpected journeys in unexpected places because He has a plan that is bigger than our plans. To make this smoother and shorter, we might restate this as “God uses detours to accomplish His purposes.” The same work is done on each subpoint until the entire exegetical outline is converted to an outline that states universal truths. For point one, the universal truth is that God uses detours to prepare us. Point two says God guided the Israelites with the cloud and the fire. Though God does not guide all believers with these visible things, it is true that God guides us, even in tough times. Point three says God puts Israel into situations that allowed Him to display His power. Restated, this point says God builds our trust in Him, both in His character and His actions. With these points now converted into timeless truths, the theological outline would probably look like this:

Proposition: God uses detours to accomplish His purposes.

1. God prepares us.

2. God guides us.

3. God builds our trust in Him.

This theological outline gives the timeless truths that anyone of any era can see. God does not always take people on a straight path from A to B, but His detours are not without reason. God’s detours prepare, guide, and build trust within His people. This brings us much closer to a sermon that is ready to preach in the contemporary world. Only one step remains: the homiletical outline.

The Homiletical Outline

The homiletical outline takes the theological outline and restates it in contemporary language that tells people what the text means now, in the present. Sometimes the theological proposition will need to be reworded; sometimes it will stay relatively the same. Regardless of how much changes from the theological outline to the homiletical outline, remember that the main idea is the sermon’s central proposition. This is the statement that now controls the entire message and will “hook” the audience. The preacher must try to find contemporary language to state this main idea in a way that causes the audience to engage with the sermon and desire to know more. Since the desire is to preach a message that brings change, the central theme should be stated in an imperative tense. An imperative is a command tense that tells the listener what must, should, can, or ought to happen. If the central theme is simply, “God takes us on detours,” the audience will fail to understand how they can apply that statement to their lives. If, however, the proposition is stated with an imperative, “Our life’s purpose can be accomplished through detours,” the audience is immediately hooked. They will ask the question, “How do detours accomplish God’s purpose for my life?” and will wait for the answers. Once the proposition is restated in contemporary language that relates to the present, the points are also reworded.

The final sermon outline from which the preacher now preaches is this:

Proposition: Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.

1. Detours in the journey of life are often for preparation.

2. We can use detours to learn how to hear and follow God’s voice.

3. God displays His power through the detours we face.

The proposition controls all of the points; each point is directly connected to the proposition and gives it clarity. The proposition will now also control every illustration and application that is added to the sermon outline. This clear theme gives the preacher a specific point for the sermon and aids the congregation in hearing and understanding the message. While the proposition serves to control the entire message, its actual placement in the sermon can vary based on the sermon’s structure. Options for placement will be presented in chapter 5.

**Q:** What are some of the more memorable sermons you have heard? Why were they so memorable?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Why is the sermon’s central proposition so important?

**A:** The central proposition controls the entire message and sets the boundaries for the subpoints, illustration, and applications. The proposition states the sermon’s theme and is the control statement for every other part of the sermon.

**Q:** Write a sentence that summarizes the main idea in Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31.

**A:** Answers will vary. An example might be, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so that they would not turn back when faced with battle against the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.”

**Q:** How does the theological outline differ from the exegetical outline?

**A:** The exegetical outline simply outlines the text as it is written. It is written in past tense and explains the passage as the original reader would have understood it. The theological outline is based on the exegetical outline. It looks at each point of the exegetical outline and states the universal truth that each point makes. The universal truth is how all people, of all eras and times, can understand this passage. It states the timeless truth that everyone can get from the text.

**Q:** Why is the homiletical outline necessary?

**A:** The homiletical outline takes the theological outline and restates it in contemporary language that helps people see how the biblical message is relevant today.

#### 4.1.2 — Describe how a sermon’s central proposition controls the message.

The Single Point

If sermons fail, it is usually not because of poor exegesis, but because they deal with too many unrelated ideas. “A sermon should be a bullet, not buckshot,” says Haddon Robinson (2001, 35). “A sermon is the explanation, interpretation, or application of a single dominant idea supported by other ideas all drawn from one passage or several passages of Scripture” (Robinson 2001, 35, italics added). This is important for several reasons. First, by focusing on one idea, preachers are less likely to misuse, misapply, and misinterpret Scripture. Second, a single point in a sermon helps listeners to hear, understand, and remember what is said. The old adage “Repetition is the key to learning” is very true. Third, preachers have the weighty task of presenting God’s Word so people can understand. Without clarity, preachers fail in this important responsibility. As preachers, you want your listeners to grasp truth from Scripture that they can apply; you do not want to leave them confused and wondering. With one point that controls the entire message, preachers are better able to give a message that is clear and easy for the congregation to understand.

Explain the importance of focusing on a single idea in the sermon.

Finding the single point or focus of a sermon is one of the most challenging aspects of sermon preparation. In many homiletics settings, students are asked to articulate the central theme of their sermon in a single sentence. This exercise often exposes a lack of clarity, as multiple ideas surface without a unifying focus. The difficulty of this task highlights an important reality: if the central theme cannot be clearly stated, it has likely not yet been clearly formed. Arriving at a concise, all-encompassing sentence typically requires intentional study, prayer, and disciplined reflection.

Though this process can feel demanding, the ability to identify and articulate a sermon’s central idea is essential for effective preaching. As Graham Johnston observes, “If you can’t identify what you’re saying in one, clear sentence, it means that you probably aren’t clear yourself. You may say some good things, but don’t be surprised when no one seems to grasp the thrust of your message” (2001, 172).

One of the most famous speeches in the United States was given by Dr. Martin Luther King, Jr. “I have a dream” was King’s resounding declaration, and everything in it related to his dream of equality among races. The focus of this speech is without question, and the impact is undeniable. Had King rambled, or put together several points, his speech would likely have been overlooked in history books. Because of King’s clear theme, his speech stands as one of the most powerful, life-changing, and poignant speeches of our time. Though not every sermon will have this kind of power in history, the sermon should have this kind of singularity of thought and impact.

Describe how a sermon’s central proposition controls the message.

The Central Proposition

Your sermon should have one central proposition that grounds everything else. Having a single point to the sermon not only aids in the clarity of the message but also helps guide the message. The central proposition (also called a theme) sets boundaries for the sermon and helps preachers know what to include and what to exclude. As you write and study your sermon, ask the question, “Does this support the main point of this sermon?” Illustrations, explanations, and applications should all be guided by the theme. Once these parts of the sermon are in line with the proposition, ask, “Will my audience understand this main point?” If there is any question about the audience’s ability to understand and grasp the point of the sermon, keep working on it. Sermons should say what the preacher means and not leave things to question. Work, struggle, wrestle, and labor to get a proposition that explains the text’s point in a simple, clear sentence; then, use this hard-earned proposition to control every aspect of the sermon.

Explain the process of finding a central proposition.

It is one thing to know a sermon needs a central proposition. It is another thing to actually come up with the proposition. The central theme of a sermon can be found in a variety of ways. If you read ten homiletics books, you will likely find ten different approaches to accomplishing this important step. Though there are more ways to complete this step, one of the best ways requires the formation of three outlines: an exegetical outline , a theological outline , and a homiletical outline .

The Exegetical Outline

An exegetical outline starts with the text under consideration. After reading the text several times in different translations and perhaps in the original language, form an outline based on what the original readers of the text would have understood. This outline is written in past tense because it looks at the text from the perspective of the past. Read Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31.

After reading this text, ask, “What would the original readers have understood this passage to mean?” From this question the exegetical outline is formed. First, come up with one sentence that summarizes the point of this passage. For Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31, the summary sentence would be something like, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines, because although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.” This is a rather long sentence, but it correctly summarizes the text’s point. Everything else in this passage should support the main idea. Each detail that sheds light on the main purpose becomes a subpoint to the main idea. For example, in the text, we read that God prepared the Israelites for battle by marching them out of Egypt like an army, but keeping them away from battle while they gained confidence. This idea of preparation is a detail that supports the main purpose, so it becomes a subpoint. The passage says that not only did God lead them like an army, but He also gave them visible signs to prove He was guiding them on the wilderness journey. Since guidance is a detail that is different from preparation, it, too, becomes a subpoint. Finally, we read that God specifically led the Israelites to the Red Sea to prove they could trust His leading. What looked like a trap became a place God demonstrated His power. Again, this idea of proving trust is different from preparation or guidance, so it also becomes a subpoint. These three ideas are the three details that support the main idea, and together they form the exegetical outline. The outline would look like this:

Proposition: God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.

1. God helped prepare Israel for battle by taking them on the long route through the desert (13:17–18).

2. God guided Israel with the pillar of fire and the pillar of cloud and taught them to trust in His guidance (13:21–22).

3. God brought them to a place where He could demonstrate His power and glory by defeating Pharaoh and his army. This would build the Israelites’ trust in God, which they would need when facing future battles (14:1–4; 14:31).

The Theological Outline

The exegetical outline simply says what the text says. However, by breaking the passage into thoughts, the preacher is able to gain some direction as to what the passage is saying. After this step is done, it is time to move on to the theological outline. The theological outline takes the exegetical outline and rewords the points and proposition to state universal, timeless truths. While the exegetical outline is written in the past tense and states what was true for the original readers, the theological outline is written in the present tense because it states what is true for all readers, both past and present. This requires a lot of work, prayer, and study, but this middle step is extremely important for arriving at a biblically sound sermon. The exegetical outline tells us that God had a reason for taking the Israelites through the wilderness. Obviously, God will not take everyone on a physical journey through a desert. However, a universal truth is found in the statement, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so they would not turn back when faced with battling the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.” What is this truth? God leads everyone on unexpected journeys in unexpected places because He has a plan that is bigger than our plans. To make this smoother and shorter, we might restate this as “God uses detours to accomplish His purposes.” The same work is done on each subpoint until the entire exegetical outline is converted to an outline that states universal truths. For point one, the universal truth is that God uses detours to prepare us. Point two says God guided the Israelites with the cloud and the fire. Though God does not guide all believers with these visible things, it is true that God guides us, even in tough times. Point three says God puts Israel into situations that allowed Him to display His power. Restated, this point says God builds our trust in Him, both in His character and His actions. With these points now converted into timeless truths, the theological outline would probably look like this:

Proposition: God uses detours to accomplish His purposes.

1. God prepares us.

2. God guides us.

3. God builds our trust in Him.

This theological outline gives the timeless truths that anyone of any era can see. God does not always take people on a straight path from A to B, but His detours are not without reason. God’s detours prepare, guide, and build trust within His people. This brings us much closer to a sermon that is ready to preach in the contemporary world. Only one step remains: the homiletical outline.

The Homiletical Outline

The homiletical outline takes the theological outline and restates it in contemporary language that tells people what the text means now, in the present. Sometimes the theological proposition will need to be reworded; sometimes it will stay relatively the same. Regardless of how much changes from the theological outline to the homiletical outline, remember that the main idea is the sermon’s central proposition. This is the statement that now controls the entire message and will “hook” the audience. The preacher must try to find contemporary language to state this main idea in a way that causes the audience to engage with the sermon and desire to know more. Since the desire is to preach a message that brings change, the central theme should be stated in an imperative tense. An imperative is a command tense that tells the listener what must, should, can, or ought to happen. If the central theme is simply, “God takes us on detours,” the audience will fail to understand how they can apply that statement to their lives. If, however, the proposition is stated with an imperative, “Our life’s purpose can be accomplished through detours,” the audience is immediately hooked. They will ask the question, “How do detours accomplish God’s purpose for my life?” and will wait for the answers. Once the proposition is restated in contemporary language that relates to the present, the points are also reworded.

The final sermon outline from which the preacher now preaches is this:

Proposition: Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.

1. Detours in the journey of life are often for preparation.

2. We can use detours to learn how to hear and follow God’s voice.

3. God displays His power through the detours we face.

The proposition controls all of the points; each point is directly connected to the proposition and gives it clarity. The proposition will now also control every illustration and application that is added to the sermon outline. This clear theme gives the preacher a specific point for the sermon and aids the congregation in hearing and understanding the message. While the proposition serves to control the entire message, its actual placement in the sermon can vary based on the sermon’s structure. Options for placement will be presented in chapter 5.

**Q:** What are some of the more memorable sermons you have heard? Why were they so memorable?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Why is the sermon’s central proposition so important?

**A:** The central proposition controls the entire message and sets the boundaries for the subpoints, illustration, and applications. The proposition states the sermon’s theme and is the control statement for every other part of the sermon.

**Q:** Write a sentence that summarizes the main idea in Exodus 13:17–14:4; 14:31.

**A:** Answers will vary. An example might be, “God took the Israelites on a detour out of Egypt so that they would not turn back when faced with battle against the Philistines because, although they were armed for battle, they were not fully trusting the Lord as their deliverer.”

**Q:** How does the theological outline differ from the exegetical outline?

**A:** The exegetical outline simply outlines the text as it is written. It is written in past tense and explains the passage as the original reader would have understood it. The theological outline is based on the exegetical outline. It looks at each point of the exegetical outline and states the universal truth that each point makes. The universal truth is how all people, of all eras and times, can understand this passage. It states the timeless truth that everyone can get from the text.

**Q:** Why is the homiletical outline necessary?

**A:** The homiletical outline takes the theological outline and restates it in contemporary language that helps people see how the biblical message is relevant today.

### Keeping God in the Center

#### 4.2.1 — Describe the dangers preachers face when sermons are not centered on God.

The Sermon’s Center

Many sermons are received with great enthusiasm because they are entertaining, full of stories, relevant to current issues, and inspiring. It is desirable that preachers have these kinds of sermons; however, it is important that the focus of these sermons not be entertainment, stories, current issues, or inspiration. The center of any sermon must be God. At first glance, this point can seem rather obvious; however, with the desire to be relevant, powerful, and motivating, it is easy for preachers to re-center their sermons on current events, political action, and soapboxes, things that leave God on the sidelines. Sermons should be structured and presented in such a way that they clearly answer the question, “What does this reveal about God and our relationship with Him?”

Describe the dangers preachers face when sermons are not centered on God.

Sermons have a specific purpose: to present the message of God’s kingdom invading earth. When sermons focus on other areas, God’s Word is compromised and truth is distorted. A discussion of such deviations follows.

1. Sermons on politics polarize the congregation, make people with certain viewpoints feel outcast, usually present opinions, and create conflict. These sermons often force positions on God and present a “gospel” message that is nothing of the true gospel message. At times, God will ask you to address current events, but in these situations, God must still be the focus. Preaching His Word clearly will help people understand what they should do, as Christians, in the world of politics, without the church dictating a specific political stance.

2. Sermons that begin with opinion often fall into the danger of “reading into the text.” When a preacher is staunch about an opinion, he or she is rarely open to the Holy Spirit presenting an alternate point from Scripture. Proof-texting comes into play as this minister tries to find biblical support for his or her position. The pulpit is not the place for giving opinions. As Timothy was instructed, “Correctly [handle] the word of truth” and “Preach the Word” (2 Timothy 2:15; 4:2). Opinions do not accomplish this.

3. Sermons focused on entertainment rarely dig deep into God’s Word to present life-challenging messages. It is easy to entertain, give messages that make people feel good, and always present happy, upbeat sermons that keep people smiling. Sermons centered on entertainment, however, do not do justice to the life-changing message of Scripture. Some teachings in the Bible are happy and entertaining. Other teachings are hard: love your enemy, serve to reign, suffer to know joy, death leads to life. People can find entertainment in a host of places; however, they usually come to church to find answers to life’s biggest questions, to get help in times of hardship and suffering, and to figure out who God really is. Entertainment can aid in the presentation of the message, but entertainment alone is shallow and poorly reflects the power of the Kingdom.

4. Sermons designed for inspiration alone lack godly motivation and the involvement of the Holy Spirit. People are easily manipulated into response. You can try to inspire people into homeless ministries by showing a moving video or giving the statistics on the homeless of the world. The inspiring stories and statistics will certainly evoke immediate responses from people, but in a few weeks or months, the inspiration wears off, and people return to their “regular” lives. Inspiration moves people temporarily but does not often lead to life change. Sermons can and should address current topics like homelessness, addictions, poverty, and the lack of education, but these cannot be the center. The center must focus on God and the revolutionary, radical way God’s kingdom can transform our world. When God is at the center, following His model of loving Him by caring for others leads toward life change that makes caring for the impoverished, homeless, addicted, and uneducated a part of Christian living, not a temporary duty encouraged by an inspirational presentation. Inspiration is helpful in illustrations, but the aim of the sermon should always be life change through our knowledge of and relationship with God. Sermons are for life change, for presenting the powerful truths of Scripture, and for aiding the invasion of God’s kingdom on earth. Taking God out of the message or pushing Him to the sidelines is a grave misuse of the pulpit, the sermon, and the preacher’s calling. God as center cannot be compromised.

Indicate the role of the Holy Spirit in the formation of the sermon.

The Holy Spirit and the Sermon

God earnestly desires for people to know Him and His Word. The Holy Spirit is the One who takes God’s words and helps Christians understand, know, and live by them. When it comes to sermon preparation, the minister is utterly helpless without the Holy Spirit’s working in his or her life—revealing truth, giving understanding, guiding study, and applying the Word. The Holy Spirit aids the minister in discovering the main point of the text, keeping that point centered in every facet of the sermon, and helping the minister create a life-challenging message. Without the Spirit, ministers can present well-written speeches, but these speeches will be nothing more than good speeches. A sermon, by its very definition, handles God’s Word and seeks to explain and apply it to people’s lives. No one can dig into and discover the amazing truths and treasures of Scripture without the Spirit.

The Holy Spirit guides the preacher in forming the message and guides in the presentation of the message. A minister can do many things to aid the reception of the sermon, but these alone do not apply God’s Word to the hearts and heads of the listeners. The Holy Spirit is the Guide (John 16:13) who makes God’s Word alive and applicable to each person. No preacher should enter the pulpit without extensive prayer and reliance on the Holy Spirit to make the words alive. At times a preacher will step to the pulpit—having prayed, studied, and labored over a message—and wonder if this is the right message for the people, if it is clear enough, and if the points and illustrations are correct. When the preacher has done everything he or she knows to do, as he or she delivers the message, the only thing left is to rely on the Spirit to bring life and change through the words. It is amazing to see what God does in people’s lives when the Spirit takes hold of a sermon and helps it strike the mark in a person’s heart. Quicke (2003, 164) says the Holy Spirit is “the sermon designer par excellence.” He is instrumental in every part of the sermon process—the discovery of the text, the exegesis, the sermon formation, the illustrations and applications, the delivery, and the reception of the message. The Holy Spirit separates sermons from speeches and makes the message of Christ powerful and relevant today.

**Q:** What are some ways that preachers can get off center in their sermons?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Describe a time the Holy Spirit helped you prepare a sermon, lesson, or testimony.

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 4.2.2 — Indicate the role of the Holy Spirit in the formation of the sermon.

The Sermon’s Center

Many sermons are received with great enthusiasm because they are entertaining, full of stories, relevant to current issues, and inspiring. It is desirable that preachers have these kinds of sermons; however, it is important that the focus of these sermons not be entertainment, stories, current issues, or inspiration. The center of any sermon must be God. At first glance, this point can seem rather obvious; however, with the desire to be relevant, powerful, and motivating, it is easy for preachers to re-center their sermons on current events, political action, and soapboxes, things that leave God on the sidelines. Sermons should be structured and presented in such a way that they clearly answer the question, “What does this reveal about God and our relationship with Him?”

Describe the dangers preachers face when sermons are not centered on God.

Sermons have a specific purpose: to present the message of God’s kingdom invading earth. When sermons focus on other areas, God’s Word is compromised and truth is distorted. A discussion of such deviations follows.

1. Sermons on politics polarize the congregation, make people with certain viewpoints feel outcast, usually present opinions, and create conflict. These sermons often force positions on God and present a “gospel” message that is nothing of the true gospel message. At times, God will ask you to address current events, but in these situations, God must still be the focus. Preaching His Word clearly will help people understand what they should do, as Christians, in the world of politics, without the church dictating a specific political stance.

2. Sermons that begin with opinion often fall into the danger of “reading into the text.” When a preacher is staunch about an opinion, he or she is rarely open to the Holy Spirit presenting an alternate point from Scripture. Proof-texting comes into play as this minister tries to find biblical support for his or her position. The pulpit is not the place for giving opinions. As Timothy was instructed, “Correctly [handle] the word of truth” and “Preach the Word” (2 Timothy 2:15; 4:2). Opinions do not accomplish this.

3. Sermons focused on entertainment rarely dig deep into God’s Word to present life-challenging messages. It is easy to entertain, give messages that make people feel good, and always present happy, upbeat sermons that keep people smiling. Sermons centered on entertainment, however, do not do justice to the life-changing message of Scripture. Some teachings in the Bible are happy and entertaining. Other teachings are hard: love your enemy, serve to reign, suffer to know joy, death leads to life. People can find entertainment in a host of places; however, they usually come to church to find answers to life’s biggest questions, to get help in times of hardship and suffering, and to figure out who God really is. Entertainment can aid in the presentation of the message, but entertainment alone is shallow and poorly reflects the power of the Kingdom.

4. Sermons designed for inspiration alone lack godly motivation and the involvement of the Holy Spirit. People are easily manipulated into response. You can try to inspire people into homeless ministries by showing a moving video or giving the statistics on the homeless of the world. The inspiring stories and statistics will certainly evoke immediate responses from people, but in a few weeks or months, the inspiration wears off, and people return to their “regular” lives. Inspiration moves people temporarily but does not often lead to life change. Sermons can and should address current topics like homelessness, addictions, poverty, and the lack of education, but these cannot be the center. The center must focus on God and the revolutionary, radical way God’s kingdom can transform our world. When God is at the center, following His model of loving Him by caring for others leads toward life change that makes caring for the impoverished, homeless, addicted, and uneducated a part of Christian living, not a temporary duty encouraged by an inspirational presentation. Inspiration is helpful in illustrations, but the aim of the sermon should always be life change through our knowledge of and relationship with God. Sermons are for life change, for presenting the powerful truths of Scripture, and for aiding the invasion of God’s kingdom on earth. Taking God out of the message or pushing Him to the sidelines is a grave misuse of the pulpit, the sermon, and the preacher’s calling. God as center cannot be compromised.

Indicate the role of the Holy Spirit in the formation of the sermon.

The Holy Spirit and the Sermon

God earnestly desires for people to know Him and His Word. The Holy Spirit is the One who takes God’s words and helps Christians understand, know, and live by them. When it comes to sermon preparation, the minister is utterly helpless without the Holy Spirit’s working in his or her life—revealing truth, giving understanding, guiding study, and applying the Word. The Holy Spirit aids the minister in discovering the main point of the text, keeping that point centered in every facet of the sermon, and helping the minister create a life-challenging message. Without the Spirit, ministers can present well-written speeches, but these speeches will be nothing more than good speeches. A sermon, by its very definition, handles God’s Word and seeks to explain and apply it to people’s lives. No one can dig into and discover the amazing truths and treasures of Scripture without the Spirit.

The Holy Spirit guides the preacher in forming the message and guides in the presentation of the message. A minister can do many things to aid the reception of the sermon, but these alone do not apply God’s Word to the hearts and heads of the listeners. The Holy Spirit is the Guide (John 16:13) who makes God’s Word alive and applicable to each person. No preacher should enter the pulpit without extensive prayer and reliance on the Holy Spirit to make the words alive. At times a preacher will step to the pulpit—having prayed, studied, and labored over a message—and wonder if this is the right message for the people, if it is clear enough, and if the points and illustrations are correct. When the preacher has done everything he or she knows to do, as he or she delivers the message, the only thing left is to rely on the Spirit to bring life and change through the words. It is amazing to see what God does in people’s lives when the Spirit takes hold of a sermon and helps it strike the mark in a person’s heart. Quicke (2003, 164) says the Holy Spirit is “the sermon designer par excellence.” He is instrumental in every part of the sermon process—the discovery of the text, the exegesis, the sermon formation, the illustrations and applications, the delivery, and the reception of the message. The Holy Spirit separates sermons from speeches and makes the message of Christ powerful and relevant today.

**Q:** What are some ways that preachers can get off center in their sermons?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Describe a time the Holy Spirit helped you prepare a sermon, lesson, or testimony.

**A:** Answers will vary.

## 5 - Structure Your Sermon

### Anticipate Challenges to the Sermon

#### 5.1.1 — Explain why it is important for preachers to “prove” their messages.

Questions Preachers Need to Answer

In the postmodern world, people are not looking for simplistic answers to tough questions. They eagerly seek faith, spiritual experience, and belief, but they do not seek blind faith, blind spiritual experience, or blind belief. Postmodernists embrace a specific religion or faith because it proves to be the best explanation of their experiences. Postmodernists want to know the answers to why and how more than simply what. Sermons should jump into the hard questions and tackle the whys and the hows:

Explain why it is important for preachers to “prove” their messages.

Why does faith in Christ make sense?

How does following the Bible stand up in hard times?

How does God speak today?

The contemporary world has transitioned from scientific proofs to persuasive arguments. People hear a variety of messages from the world, so when they sit in front of a preacher, they want to know why the message of Christ is different from and better than the other messages they hear. Preachers need to prove their messages and be persuasive in their presentations. Ed Dobson (as quoted by Graham Johnston (2001, 108) observes:

In an earlier generation, it was enough for a preacher to announce the truth, and the congregation would ratify it. Today such pronouncements are met with resistance. Today, I have to persuade people, even in the church, of the gospel and its implications. I must respect the right of an audience to make up its own mind.

The goal is not manipulation, but presenting the message in such a clear, structured way that the message is persuasive and powerful in the hearts of the hearers. The Holy Spirit will ultimately bring the sermon home to each person’s heart. However, the preacher should anticipate challenges to the message and prepare answers that diffuse doubts that could prevent someone from embracing God’s way.

Identify questions preachers can use to evaluate the clarity of their messages.

Evaluating Sermon Clarity

How can a preacher anticipate challenges to the message to present a clear sermon? The preacher should ask several questions before stepping to the pulpit:

1. Is this relevant for the audience?

One of the first questions to ask is whether the message is relevant for the audience being addressed. The topics addressed to a youth group might vastly differ from those addressed to senior citizens. Consider the audience being addressed and recognize ways to make the sermon more relevant to that particular crowd. For example, a sermon on the godly management of the family has much greater relevance to parents than to singles. If the crowd to whom you will preach is predominantly single, focus the sermon on qualities of a godly life rather than on a godly home.

2. So what?

This question allows the preacher to bring the sermon home. A sermon can be an exegetical masterpiece and still fail miserably. Why? Because at the end of the sermon, listeners ask, “So what?” The “So what?” question is vital.

How will the sermon you prepare affect your audience?

What kind of change are you hoping will occur in the congregation?

Do the points in your sermon really matter?

A sermon should be more than biblical knowledge and stories. The audience might know that Jonah was swallowed by a big fish and that the Israelites walked through the Red Sea, but what does it have to do with life today? Why do David killing Goliath and Peter going to Cornelius’ house matter in the twenty-first century? If you can answer “So what?” to the satisfaction of your audience, receptivity to your sermon will be much better.

3. How?

Many sermons do well in presenting necessary aspects of following Christ but do not always bridge the gap to show the listeners how to accomplish and apply the truth they have just heard. For example, if a preacher only preaches on the need for faith, the audience will often leave saying, “Okay, so we know faith is important, but how do we have faith?” In the example sermon of chapter 4, asking, “How do God’s detours affect my life?” can help the preacher make sure the points of the sermon answer this question. Since God prepares, guides, and builds our trust, we should not always wish tough situations away. We should learn to hear God’s voice in the wilderness and pay attention to how He works in these times. How can I bring clarity to every part of the sermon and keep the preacher from assuming too much? Remember, today’s audience does not just want to know what , they also want to know how and why . Ask this question during the sermon preparation so it can clearly be answered in the sermon.

4. Are my sermon points clear?

Sermon points should directly address the central proposition. Propositions that express ability should answer how . Propositions that express obligation should answer why . For example, if the proposition states, “Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail,” the main points should answer the question, “ How can our life’s purposes be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail?” If the proposition states, “We should live lives of faith,” the main points should answer the question, “Why should we live lives of faith?” If the point does not answer the proposition’s question, it should either be reworded or not used.

5. Do the illustrations and applications make sense?

Nothing is worse than having a powerful illustration that makes a different point than the point for which it is used. A point on how to have faith should not be illustrated with a story that mentions faith only briefly but focuses on having joy in times of suffering. The illustration might be good in itself, but if it misses the point you are presenting, it confuses people. Good illustrations bring clarity and power. The preacher must make sure the illustration is not only illustrating the right thing, but also illustrates the major, and not a minor, point.

6. Does the presentation of the sermon create barriers or offend?

Tragically, a well-intentioned preacher can offend a hearer and cause that person to miss what God is saying. Look for possible offenses in the illustrations and work to avoid them. Haddon Robinson says, “I do everything I can to show people I respect them and I’m on their side. I’m careful about terms. Even though you’re sure you don’t have a bias, a listener may think you do if your phrasing offends them ” (Brown, Robinson, and Willimon 1993, 72, italics added). Using barrier language, like “pagan,” “heathen,” or “lost,” tells seekers in the audience that they are outsiders; they are not part of the group. Do not make political, sexist, racist, homophobic, xenophobic, or religious jabs. Male preachers should not exclusively give illustrations from a typically male perspective; in the same way, female preachers should not concentrate solely on the typical feminine perspective. Know your audience and work to have illustrations from all perspectives. Illustrate for singles and for couples, for children and for grandparents, for the educated and the less educated, for parents and those without children, for two-parent families and for single-parent families. Jesus used variety in His illustrations, but always made sure they were appropriate and clear. The minister must also be selective in the quantity and types of illustrations that come from his or her family. This family should not be the brunt of pulpit jokes, nor should everything that happens within the minister’s home be told publicly. At times, a family observation might be the perfect illustration for the point at hand, but be careful not to overuse or exploit the family for the sake of the sermon.

**Q:** What do postmodern people most want to know?

**A:** They want to know how to believe and why to believe more than what to believe.

**Q:** What are some challenges a preacher can anticipate when preparing messages?

**A:** Answers will vary.

### Organize the Sermon for Understanding

#### 5.2.1 — Summarize key components of a sermon.

Sermon Organization

Once the homiletical outline is complete, organize the material for clear presentation. Every sermon should include the following: introduction, central proposition, main points, illustrations, applications, and conclusion.

Summarize key components of a sermon.

Introduction

The introduction is one of the most important parts of a sermon. An audience not hooked in the introduction will likely tune out the rest of the message. Launching directly into the central proposition and main points without getting the audience involved in the experience will cause many people to shut out the message and think about their lunch plans, to-do lists, or other daydreams. A strong introduction gets the audience’s attention, introduces the subject at hand, presents a reason this should matter to them, and leaves them anticipating the rest of the sermon.

Sermons can start in a variety of ways: with a quote, statistics, an anecdote, a joke, a drama, or a video clip. Use variety and carefully choose openings that best fit the topic. For example, the sermon on God’s detours might begin with a humorous story about a road trip that included a detour. Most in the audience can identify with such a detour and will find a personal connection with the preacher’s story. After this initial catch, the preacher can then relate this illustration to real life—the times people think they are headed one direction, only to find themselves in a wilderness. Through this application, the audience is able to identify wilderness times in their own lives. Memories are stirred, painful experiences are brought up, and everyone is emotionally charged.

Central Proposition

Taking advantage of this engagement, the preacher then sets up the idea that perhaps these confusing, hard times are actually designed by God. Through this statement, the audience begins to evaluate their circumstances and wonder, “Why would God send me to the wilderness?” “Does that make sense?” “But what about my situation?” Then the preacher can state the central proposition of the sermon, “Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.” The audience is now caught. They can identify with life’s detours but wonder, “Can these detours really accomplish God’s purposes?” Some will anticipate the sermon with excitement: “Wow, this is going to make sense of my life!” Others might anticipate the sermon with skepticism: “No way can detours in life actually be good.” Regardless of each person’s position, the people in the congregation are now ready to hear arguments about how detours can bring about God’s purposes in people’s lives. From this point, the minister is ready to launch into the main points.

The minister has options as to when to put in the central proposition. If the preacher wants the congregation to know up front that the sermon will prove the usefulness of detours, he or she should state the proposition in the introduction. However, the preacher might want to talk about detours, show the possible explanations for detours, and then help the congregation come to a specific conclusion. In these circumstances, the preacher will wait to present the central proposition until the conclusion. The reasons for these alternate sermon structures will be discussed later in this chapter.

Main Points

The main points of the sermon, as already discussed, give explanation of and support to the central proposition, regardless of where the proposition is mentioned. The main points should be presented clearly. For example, after the introduction is finished and the text is read, the first point might be stated as, “Journeys off the beaten trail can still accomplish our life’s purposes because, first, they are often for preparation.” This transitional statement hits on the central proposition and directly ties the first point to it. Each point should explain how or why it supports the central proposition and then include both an illustration and an application that will drive the point home.

Illustrations

Illustrations such as stories, quotes, or demonstrations help to further explain the main point in a memorable way. The third point in a sermon I preached on Leah and Rachel from Genesis 29 and 30 was, “God remembers us.” To illustrate this point, I told, in great detail, of the day my grandmother turned sixty-five.

This was the one birthday she had looked forward to for years. It meant retirement. It meant the long-promised sports car from grandpa. It was a milestone for her, so when she woke up that day, she had great anticipation. But something was wrong. It didn’t seem like anyone remembered! My grandfather, who had not forgotten a birthday in their forty-nine years of marriage, went about the morning without the hint of “happy birthday” on his lips. He smiled, said, “Good morning,” and got ready for church without saying anything. Grandma was crushed. How could he, of all birthdays, have forgotten this one? She didn’t want to say anything because she kept thinking he was going to surprise her. She looked around every room she entered, thinking he might have hidden her card somewhere, but in every room she was disappointed. Breakfast was rather quiet that morning. When they went to church, nothing was said. No one at church knew it was her big day, and she began to feel depressed. This was a huge birthday. Couldn’t someone have remembered? By the time church was over, Grandma was crushed, hurt, and sad and could hardly say a word. It was silent in the car on the way home, and she kept wondering, “How could everyone have forgotten that today was my day?” Tears were welling up and it was all she could do to hold them in. When they got home, Grandma quietly got out of the car and walked to the door. With a great sigh, she slowly turned the handle and swung the door open. “SURPRISE!” She was shocked to find her house filled with her children and grandchildren. She turned to Grandpa, who had a big smile on his face and a bright twinkle in his eye, and heard, “Happy birthday, darling.” She was not forgotten! Grandpa hadn’t forgotten. Her kids hadn’t forgotten. No . . . they had more than remembered. They were waiting for the perfect time to help her celebrate her big day. Grandma was remembered!

An illustration like this will most likely draw people in. Most have either been part of or received a surprise birthday. Most will identify with the feelings of being forgotten and the joy of being remembered. The illustration helps connect the fact that “God remembers us” with real life experiences. I used it to hook people into the idea of being remembered, and then used this illustration to give insight into Rachel’s plight.

For years, Rachel had wondered, “God, how could you have forgotten me?” Day after day she hoped for a son, but that hope began to fade. It seemed God remembered everyone but her. Did He even know she existed? Did He care? Everywhere she looked brought disappointment. She had prayed. She had begged. She had tried other means, but she still was barren. Every day she got up and thought, “Maybe today.” But nothing happened. All she found was silence. She was tired. She was sad. Then, one day, when she thought all hope was gone, she took hold of the doorknob. As the door swung open, there was God with His arms wide open saying, “Surprise! Rachel, I didn’t forget you. NO! I remember you. Here. Here is the son I have promised you.”

The illustration makes the text come alive. It takes a good point and makes it extraordinary. Suddenly, everyone can identify with Rachel and recognize the power of God remembering us. Illustrations help explain difficult or confusing points and are vital to presenting a powerful, memorable sermon.

Applications

In addition to the illustration, main points should include applications that make biblical text relevant to present-day life. For example, consider a sermon on the mandate to rejoice, even in times of suffering. One point could be, “Even though the present might require intense suffering, believers can rejoice because of Christ’s victory.” To illustrate this point, the preacher turns to Job and describes his horrific plight. Job loses everything in a matter of moments: his children, his servants, his home, his flocks, and his health. His wife rejects him, most of his friends desert him, and now he sits, with boils covering his body, considering his situation. He has suffered perhaps the most intense losses we can imagine, yet he says, “I know that my Redeemer lives, and that in the end he will stand upon the earth. And after my skin has been destroyed, yet in my flesh I will see God” (Job 19:25–26). After building up Job’s condition and quoting his amazing words, the application could be something like this:

The doctor says my cancer is terminal, but I know my Redeemer lives! I have lost my job and have no means to pay the bills, yet I know my Redeemer lives! I just lost my husband. I know my Redeemer lives! Life does not make sense, and I do not know what to do next, but I know my Redeemer lives!

The application presents real-life situations that help people recognize how the point being made really does apply to their lives. People may see how amazing it was for Job to say these words, but they fail to relate his words to their own experiences. By identifying possible life situations and responding to those situations with Job’s words, the congregation is suddenly thrust into the text and challenged with the idea that they can praise God, even in the midst of suffering, because He is victorious. Often, a person will both hear and agree with the point but not truly embrace it or have an “A-ha” moment until the application. Ask God to direct you and help you give applications that will be specific enough to directly speak to people about their present life situations.

Conclusion

When the sermon is finished, it is important to have a solid conclusion that will tie together all the points and make a final attempt to drive the message home. A sermon without a conclusion makes an abrupt stop, leaves people hanging, and diminishes the power of the sermon. As the sermon draws to an end, recap the proposition and main points, and end with one final illustration and / or application that will reverberate in the listener’s heart and mind. The conclusion should be as well thought out as the rest of the sermon. You should know where you want the sermon to go and stop when you get there. Use the conclusion to clarify the message, wrap things up, and make sure the congregation lands where you have desired they land.

The goal of preaching is life change. The entire sermon works toward this goal, and the conclusion is where life change is addressed. Those who were curious and skeptical during your introduction should hopefully embrace your proposition by the conclusion. Now they understand that life’s purposes can be accomplished through detours, and they want time to process this new understanding in light of their own circumstances.

If you give people a list of things to remember, they will most likely remember the first and last items. The same is true of sermons. Even if the audience cannot recall all of the main points, they will remember the beginning and the end. It is important that in the introduction and conclusion, you expend all you can in helping people remember the central thrust of the message.

Indicate how attention to sermon organization aids in the presentation and reception of the message.

A neat, organized, and well-planned sermon will help the audience hear, understand, and remember what is said. Sermons that jump around, are jumbled, and have no clear direction make it difficult for the audience to catch meaningful thoughts to help their Christian life. Think about memorable sermons in your own life. They were clear, easy to follow, and catching, right? The elements that make a sermon easy to follow are the elements your audience will look for as well. This kind of sermon does not just happen.

Clear, powerful sermons require time and work. A preacher committed to life change will spend the time and energy needed to create a meaningful sermon. It may be tempting to put off the sermon until Saturday afternoon and hope God’s anointing will bring it all together when you step to the pulpit Sunday morning. However, this kind of procrastination will show and will ultimately decrease your effectiveness in preaching. The hard work of struggling for a catchy introduction; a clear, correct central proposition; well-worded main points; helpful illustrations and applications; and a fitting conclusion will be worth the effort when you see lives changed for eternity.

Define deductive preaching and inductive preaching .

Deductive and Inductive Preaching

The clarity of a message comes from its structure. The components of a sermon (introduction, proposition, main points, illustrations / applications, and conclusion) will not change. However, how they are structured can be changed to increase the sermon’s effectiveness. The two ways to format a sermon are deductively and inductively. Both types of sermons can be effective, but it is imperative to know your audience to determine which best suits them.

Deductive preaching begins with a declaration and then lays out the support for this declaration. This is most helpful for modern audiences who want to hear a truth and then see its proofs, although it can be effectively used with postmodernists when it is the best way to present the biblical text. This type of preaching “works from the whole (this is what I’m telling you today) to the particulars (this is why), and from the known to the unknown” (Johnston 2001, 151). If our Exodus sermon is preached deductively, the proposition will be stated up front and the proofs will follow. From the beginning, the sermon states that detours can be used to accomplish life’s purpose, and the main points serve to support this.

Inductive preaching reverses the order. The sermon begins by stating a need, presents answers or solutions for meeting this need, and ends by allowing the hearer to come to the same conclusion as the preacher. Rather than stating up front what the preacher wants the audience to believe, the preacher guides people into weighing all the evidence and arriving at the same conclusion. If the Exodus sermon is preached inductively, the minister would begin with the need—everyone experiences detours in life. Why do these detours happen? The main points would present the world’s answers (Murphy’s Law, things just happen, there is no reason to life, etc.), other religious answers (karma, fate, evil, etc.), and biblical answers (God’s desire to prepare, guide, and build trust). By the end of the illustrations and the evidence, the preacher and the audience arrive at the same conclusion: life’s purpose can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail because God is in control and has the bigger picture in mind. He sees our lives, has purposes for our lives, and can use all situations to bring about His purposes for us.

Strengths and Weaknesses

The form of preaching used depends largely on the audience and the text. Modernists want to hear truths up front, followed by supporting evidence. Deductive sermons excel at this. Placing the central proposition first evokes questions from the audience: “What does this mean?” “Can this be true?” “How do I do this?” The goal is to present powerful evidence that supports the proposition and ultimately convinces people of its truth. While this approach can be helpful for modernists, the postmodern members of your audience might be offended by the assertion of truth at the beginning. Postmodernists do not want to be told what to believe. They want to journey, discover, and embrace a position once all the evidence has been examined. A deductive sermon can be viewed as being closed-minded and cold, causing this audience to be hostile from the beginning.

The deductive approach is also ineffective if it places the climax of the sermon in the introduction and leaves the rest of the sermon in its wake. If the sermon can state an attention-grabbing statement that hooks listeners and then list supporting proofs, it is powerful. If the central proposition “steals its own thunder,” the deductive sermon fails miserably.

In the deductive Exodus example, the proposition does not present the climax.
