If it had stated that God uses detours to prepare us for His purposes, the audience would have received the entire sermon in one sentence and had nothing else to look forward to. Our proposition, however, states a rather shocking truth: hard times can be for good reason, but it does not resolve the mystery. Each sermon point reveals more supporting evidence and builds momentum through the conclusion. A deductive sermon that keeps the audience hooked from start to finish is a great success.

Inductive sermons are most helpful for postmodernists. Truths are presented in ways that allow more people to embrace them. Since postmodernists want to journey, the inductive sermon presents a variety of solutions to the problem. This type of sermon engages listeners, draws them into the sermon process, and allows them to discover scriptural truths. The listeners can hear the evidence, think through all aspects of the issue, and decide for themselves that the godly answer is the best answer.

Modernists are not as excited with this form of preaching, for inductive sermons will not present answers only from the Bible. While the purpose is to show how worldly answers fall short of God’s answers, some in the audience will object to anything being presented from the pulpit that does not come straight from Scripture. The minister must clearly present non-biblical answers as being answers the world gives. Evidence in the sermon must be set up in such a way as to clearly demonstrate that God’s way is best. Inductive sermons guide people to the proposition. However, if the sermon cannot present a clear message with the proposition at the end, the deductive method should be used instead.

Justify the relevance of inductive preaching in a postmodern world.

Effective Preaching in a Postmodern World

In today’s cultural landscape, many people are not looking to be told what to believe—they are looking to discover truth for themselves. In a world shaped by skepticism, personal experience, and competing truth claims, listeners often resist conclusions that feel imposed upon them. Instead, they are more open to a journey that allows them to wrestle with questions and arrive at truth with a sense of ownership. For this reason, the inductive method of preaching remains especially effective. It invites listeners into a process of discovery rather than demanding immediate agreement.

The ministry of Jesus provides a compelling model of this approach. In John 4, Jesus does not begin with a direct declaration of His identity. Rather than opening with, “I am the Messiah,” He begins with a simple, relational request: “Will you give me a drink?” This starting point engages the woman within her everyday reality. As the conversation unfolds, Jesus gently exposes deeper layers of her life—her isolation, her broken relationships, and her spiritual confusion. He does not force a conclusion upon her; instead, He leads her step by step toward recognizing her own need. Only after she arrives at that realization does Jesus reveal himself as the Messiah. Because she participates in the discovery, the truth is not resisted; it is embraced.

This approach speaks directly to our present context. Modern listeners, shaped by social media, cultural pluralism, and distrust of authority, often react defensively to bold theological assertions made without relational or intellectual engagement. A statement such as “God is holy,” while true, may initially be perceived as abstract, irrelevant, or even confrontational. However, when a preacher begins with shared human experience—our longing for justice, our outrage at evil, our desire for things to be made right, listeners begin to recognize a deeper moral reality already at work within them. From there, the truth of God’s holiness emerges not as an imposed idea, but as the most reasonable and compelling conclusion.

Effective preaching today requires both theological faithfulness and cultural awareness. The preacher must carefully study the biblical text while also understanding the questions, doubts, and longings of the contemporary world. This dual attentiveness allows sermons to bridge the gap between Scripture and lived experience. As Graham Johnston (2001, 153) suggests, when listeners are guided through the weight of evidence, truths that might have initially been rejected are often received with openness.

Ultimately, the task of preaching is not simply to declare truth, but to guide people into it. This requires dependence on the Holy Spirit, disciplined preparation, and a commitment to clarity. As preachers engage both God’s Word and the world it addresses, they participate in the ongoing work of leading people into the knowledge of God. For this reason, it remains essential to faithfully and accurately handle Scripture, “rightly dividing the word of truth,” so that lives may be transformed not just momentarily, but for eternity.

**Q:** How does a strong introduction help a sermon?

**A:** The introduction gives the first impression of the preacher and the sermon. If an audience is bored, offended, or confused by the introduction, they will not likely tune in for the rest of the sermon. A good introduction will hook the audience, build confidence in the preacher, and keep people listening all the way to the end.

**Q:** What is the purpose of illustrations?

**A:** Illustrations give a more detailed explanation of the point through an example, catchy quote, or statistics that will increase the audience’s ability to understand what is being said. It is often a place where the biblical text meets real-life situations and helps the audience identify with what is being said.

**Q:** What is the role of a conclusion in a sermon?

**A:** The conclusion wraps up the sermon, gives it a feeling of completion, and brings the entire sermon to a place of decision. In the conclusion, the audience and preacher are now on the same page and the listeners are brought to a point of decision. The conclusion recaps the message and makes one final push to highlight the main proposition.

**Q:** Are you more receptive to deductive or inductive preaching? Why?

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 5.2.2 — Indicate how attention to sermon organization aids in the presentation and reception of the message.

Sermon Organization

Once the homiletical outline is complete, organize the material for clear presentation. Every sermon should include the following: introduction, central proposition, main points, illustrations, applications, and conclusion.

Summarize key components of a sermon.

Introduction

The introduction is one of the most important parts of a sermon. An audience not hooked in the introduction will likely tune out the rest of the message. Launching directly into the central proposition and main points without getting the audience involved in the experience will cause many people to shut out the message and think about their lunch plans, to-do lists, or other daydreams. A strong introduction gets the audience’s attention, introduces the subject at hand, presents a reason this should matter to them, and leaves them anticipating the rest of the sermon.

Sermons can start in a variety of ways: with a quote, statistics, an anecdote, a joke, a drama, or a video clip. Use variety and carefully choose openings that best fit the topic. For example, the sermon on God’s detours might begin with a humorous story about a road trip that included a detour. Most in the audience can identify with such a detour and will find a personal connection with the preacher’s story. After this initial catch, the preacher can then relate this illustration to real life—the times people think they are headed one direction, only to find themselves in a wilderness. Through this application, the audience is able to identify wilderness times in their own lives. Memories are stirred, painful experiences are brought up, and everyone is emotionally charged.

Central Proposition

Taking advantage of this engagement, the preacher then sets up the idea that perhaps these confusing, hard times are actually designed by God. Through this statement, the audience begins to evaluate their circumstances and wonder, “Why would God send me to the wilderness?” “Does that make sense?” “But what about my situation?” Then the preacher can state the central proposition of the sermon, “Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.” The audience is now caught. They can identify with life’s detours but wonder, “Can these detours really accomplish God’s purposes?” Some will anticipate the sermon with excitement: “Wow, this is going to make sense of my life!” Others might anticipate the sermon with skepticism: “No way can detours in life actually be good.” Regardless of each person’s position, the people in the congregation are now ready to hear arguments about how detours can bring about God’s purposes in people’s lives. From this point, the minister is ready to launch into the main points.

The minister has options as to when to put in the central proposition. If the preacher wants the congregation to know up front that the sermon will prove the usefulness of detours, he or she should state the proposition in the introduction. However, the preacher might want to talk about detours, show the possible explanations for detours, and then help the congregation come to a specific conclusion. In these circumstances, the preacher will wait to present the central proposition until the conclusion. The reasons for these alternate sermon structures will be discussed later in this chapter.

Main Points

The main points of the sermon, as already discussed, give explanation of and support to the central proposition, regardless of where the proposition is mentioned. The main points should be presented clearly. For example, after the introduction is finished and the text is read, the first point might be stated as, “Journeys off the beaten trail can still accomplish our life’s purposes because, first, they are often for preparation.” This transitional statement hits on the central proposition and directly ties the first point to it. Each point should explain how or why it supports the central proposition and then include both an illustration and an application that will drive the point home.

Illustrations

Illustrations such as stories, quotes, or demonstrations help to further explain the main point in a memorable way. The third point in a sermon I preached on Leah and Rachel from Genesis 29 and 30 was, “God remembers us.” To illustrate this point, I told, in great detail, of the day my grandmother turned sixty-five.

This was the one birthday she had looked forward to for years. It meant retirement. It meant the long-promised sports car from grandpa. It was a milestone for her, so when she woke up that day, she had great anticipation. But something was wrong. It didn’t seem like anyone remembered! My grandfather, who had not forgotten a birthday in their forty-nine years of marriage, went about the morning without the hint of “happy birthday” on his lips. He smiled, said, “Good morning,” and got ready for church without saying anything. Grandma was crushed. How could he, of all birthdays, have forgotten this one? She didn’t want to say anything because she kept thinking he was going to surprise her. She looked around every room she entered, thinking he might have hidden her card somewhere, but in every room she was disappointed. Breakfast was rather quiet that morning. When they went to church, nothing was said. No one at church knew it was her big day, and she began to feel depressed. This was a huge birthday. Couldn’t someone have remembered? By the time church was over, Grandma was crushed, hurt, and sad and could hardly say a word. It was silent in the car on the way home, and she kept wondering, “How could everyone have forgotten that today was my day?” Tears were welling up and it was all she could do to hold them in. When they got home, Grandma quietly got out of the car and walked to the door. With a great sigh, she slowly turned the handle and swung the door open. “SURPRISE!” She was shocked to find her house filled with her children and grandchildren. She turned to Grandpa, who had a big smile on his face and a bright twinkle in his eye, and heard, “Happy birthday, darling.” She was not forgotten! Grandpa hadn’t forgotten. Her kids hadn’t forgotten. No . . . they had more than remembered. They were waiting for the perfect time to help her celebrate her big day. Grandma was remembered!

An illustration like this will most likely draw people in. Most have either been part of or received a surprise birthday. Most will identify with the feelings of being forgotten and the joy of being remembered. The illustration helps connect the fact that “God remembers us” with real life experiences. I used it to hook people into the idea of being remembered, and then used this illustration to give insight into Rachel’s plight.

For years, Rachel had wondered, “God, how could you have forgotten me?” Day after day she hoped for a son, but that hope began to fade. It seemed God remembered everyone but her. Did He even know she existed? Did He care? Everywhere she looked brought disappointment. She had prayed. She had begged. She had tried other means, but she still was barren. Every day she got up and thought, “Maybe today.” But nothing happened. All she found was silence. She was tired. She was sad. Then, one day, when she thought all hope was gone, she took hold of the doorknob. As the door swung open, there was God with His arms wide open saying, “Surprise! Rachel, I didn’t forget you. NO! I remember you. Here. Here is the son I have promised you.”

The illustration makes the text come alive. It takes a good point and makes it extraordinary. Suddenly, everyone can identify with Rachel and recognize the power of God remembering us. Illustrations help explain difficult or confusing points and are vital to presenting a powerful, memorable sermon.

Applications

In addition to the illustration, main points should include applications that make biblical text relevant to present-day life. For example, consider a sermon on the mandate to rejoice, even in times of suffering. One point could be, “Even though the present might require intense suffering, believers can rejoice because of Christ’s victory.” To illustrate this point, the preacher turns to Job and describes his horrific plight. Job loses everything in a matter of moments: his children, his servants, his home, his flocks, and his health. His wife rejects him, most of his friends desert him, and now he sits, with boils covering his body, considering his situation. He has suffered perhaps the most intense losses we can imagine, yet he says, “I know that my Redeemer lives, and that in the end he will stand upon the earth. And after my skin has been destroyed, yet in my flesh I will see God” (Job 19:25–26). After building up Job’s condition and quoting his amazing words, the application could be something like this:

The doctor says my cancer is terminal, but I know my Redeemer lives! I have lost my job and have no means to pay the bills, yet I know my Redeemer lives! I just lost my husband. I know my Redeemer lives! Life does not make sense, and I do not know what to do next, but I know my Redeemer lives!

The application presents real-life situations that help people recognize how the point being made really does apply to their lives. People may see how amazing it was for Job to say these words, but they fail to relate his words to their own experiences. By identifying possible life situations and responding to those situations with Job’s words, the congregation is suddenly thrust into the text and challenged with the idea that they can praise God, even in the midst of suffering, because He is victorious. Often, a person will both hear and agree with the point but not truly embrace it or have an “A-ha” moment until the application. Ask God to direct you and help you give applications that will be specific enough to directly speak to people about their present life situations.

Conclusion

When the sermon is finished, it is important to have a solid conclusion that will tie together all the points and make a final attempt to drive the message home. A sermon without a conclusion makes an abrupt stop, leaves people hanging, and diminishes the power of the sermon. As the sermon draws to an end, recap the proposition and main points, and end with one final illustration and / or application that will reverberate in the listener’s heart and mind. The conclusion should be as well thought out as the rest of the sermon. You should know where you want the sermon to go and stop when you get there. Use the conclusion to clarify the message, wrap things up, and make sure the congregation lands where you have desired they land.

The goal of preaching is life change. The entire sermon works toward this goal, and the conclusion is where life change is addressed. Those who were curious and skeptical during your introduction should hopefully embrace your proposition by the conclusion. Now they understand that life’s purposes can be accomplished through detours, and they want time to process this new understanding in light of their own circumstances.

If you give people a list of things to remember, they will most likely remember the first and last items. The same is true of sermons. Even if the audience cannot recall all of the main points, they will remember the beginning and the end. It is important that in the introduction and conclusion, you expend all you can in helping people remember the central thrust of the message.

Indicate how attention to sermon organization aids in the presentation and reception of the message.

A neat, organized, and well-planned sermon will help the audience hear, understand, and remember what is said. Sermons that jump around, are jumbled, and have no clear direction make it difficult for the audience to catch meaningful thoughts to help their Christian life. Think about memorable sermons in your own life. They were clear, easy to follow, and catching, right? The elements that make a sermon easy to follow are the elements your audience will look for as well. This kind of sermon does not just happen.

Clear, powerful sermons require time and work. A preacher committed to life change will spend the time and energy needed to create a meaningful sermon. It may be tempting to put off the sermon until Saturday afternoon and hope God’s anointing will bring it all together when you step to the pulpit Sunday morning. However, this kind of procrastination will show and will ultimately decrease your effectiveness in preaching. The hard work of struggling for a catchy introduction; a clear, correct central proposition; well-worded main points; helpful illustrations and applications; and a fitting conclusion will be worth the effort when you see lives changed for eternity.

Define deductive preaching and inductive preaching .

Deductive and Inductive Preaching

The clarity of a message comes from its structure. The components of a sermon (introduction, proposition, main points, illustrations / applications, and conclusion) will not change. However, how they are structured can be changed to increase the sermon’s effectiveness. The two ways to format a sermon are deductively and inductively. Both types of sermons can be effective, but it is imperative to know your audience to determine which best suits them.

Deductive preaching begins with a declaration and then lays out the support for this declaration. This is most helpful for modern audiences who want to hear a truth and then see its proofs, although it can be effectively used with postmodernists when it is the best way to present the biblical text. This type of preaching “works from the whole (this is what I’m telling you today) to the particulars (this is why), and from the known to the unknown” (Johnston 2001, 151). If our Exodus sermon is preached deductively, the proposition will be stated up front and the proofs will follow. From the beginning, the sermon states that detours can be used to accomplish life’s purpose, and the main points serve to support this.

Inductive preaching reverses the order. The sermon begins by stating a need, presents answers or solutions for meeting this need, and ends by allowing the hearer to come to the same conclusion as the preacher. Rather than stating up front what the preacher wants the audience to believe, the preacher guides people into weighing all the evidence and arriving at the same conclusion. If the Exodus sermon is preached inductively, the minister would begin with the need—everyone experiences detours in life. Why do these detours happen? The main points would present the world’s answers (Murphy’s Law, things just happen, there is no reason to life, etc.), other religious answers (karma, fate, evil, etc.), and biblical answers (God’s desire to prepare, guide, and build trust). By the end of the illustrations and the evidence, the preacher and the audience arrive at the same conclusion: life’s purpose can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail because God is in control and has the bigger picture in mind. He sees our lives, has purposes for our lives, and can use all situations to bring about His purposes for us.

Strengths and Weaknesses

The form of preaching used depends largely on the audience and the text. Modernists want to hear truths up front, followed by supporting evidence. Deductive sermons excel at this. Placing the central proposition first evokes questions from the audience: “What does this mean?” “Can this be true?” “How do I do this?” The goal is to present powerful evidence that supports the proposition and ultimately convinces people of its truth. While this approach can be helpful for modernists, the postmodern members of your audience might be offended by the assertion of truth at the beginning. Postmodernists do not want to be told what to believe. They want to journey, discover, and embrace a position once all the evidence has been examined. A deductive sermon can be viewed as being closed-minded and cold, causing this audience to be hostile from the beginning.

The deductive approach is also ineffective if it places the climax of the sermon in the introduction and leaves the rest of the sermon in its wake. If the sermon can state an attention-grabbing statement that hooks listeners and then list supporting proofs, it is powerful. If the central proposition “steals its own thunder,” the deductive sermon fails miserably.

In the deductive Exodus example, the proposition does not present the climax. If it had stated that God uses detours to prepare us for His purposes, the audience would have received the entire sermon in one sentence and had nothing else to look forward to. Our proposition, however, states a rather shocking truth: hard times can be for good reason, but it does not resolve the mystery. Each sermon point reveals more supporting evidence and builds momentum through the conclusion. A deductive sermon that keeps the audience hooked from start to finish is a great success.

Inductive sermons are most helpful for postmodernists. Truths are presented in ways that allow more people to embrace them. Since postmodernists want to journey, the inductive sermon presents a variety of solutions to the problem. This type of sermon engages listeners, draws them into the sermon process, and allows them to discover scriptural truths. The listeners can hear the evidence, think through all aspects of the issue, and decide for themselves that the godly answer is the best answer.

Modernists are not as excited with this form of preaching, for inductive sermons will not present answers only from the Bible. While the purpose is to show how worldly answers fall short of God’s answers, some in the audience will object to anything being presented from the pulpit that does not come straight from Scripture. The minister must clearly present non-biblical answers as being answers the world gives. Evidence in the sermon must be set up in such a way as to clearly demonstrate that God’s way is best. Inductive sermons guide people to the proposition. However, if the sermon cannot present a clear message with the proposition at the end, the deductive method should be used instead.

Justify the relevance of inductive preaching in a postmodern world.

Effective Preaching in a Postmodern World

In today’s cultural landscape, many people are not looking to be told what to believe—they are looking to discover truth for themselves. In a world shaped by skepticism, personal experience, and competing truth claims, listeners often resist conclusions that feel imposed upon them. Instead, they are more open to a journey that allows them to wrestle with questions and arrive at truth with a sense of ownership. For this reason, the inductive method of preaching remains especially effective. It invites listeners into a process of discovery rather than demanding immediate agreement.

The ministry of Jesus provides a compelling model of this approach. In John 4, Jesus does not begin with a direct declaration of His identity. Rather than opening with, “I am the Messiah,” He begins with a simple, relational request: “Will you give me a drink?” This starting point engages the woman within her everyday reality. As the conversation unfolds, Jesus gently exposes deeper layers of her life—her isolation, her broken relationships, and her spiritual confusion. He does not force a conclusion upon her; instead, He leads her step by step toward recognizing her own need. Only after she arrives at that realization does Jesus reveal himself as the Messiah. Because she participates in the discovery, the truth is not resisted; it is embraced.

This approach speaks directly to our present context. Modern listeners, shaped by social media, cultural pluralism, and distrust of authority, often react defensively to bold theological assertions made without relational or intellectual engagement. A statement such as “God is holy,” while true, may initially be perceived as abstract, irrelevant, or even confrontational. However, when a preacher begins with shared human experience—our longing for justice, our outrage at evil, our desire for things to be made right, listeners begin to recognize a deeper moral reality already at work within them. From there, the truth of God’s holiness emerges not as an imposed idea, but as the most reasonable and compelling conclusion.

Effective preaching today requires both theological faithfulness and cultural awareness. The preacher must carefully study the biblical text while also understanding the questions, doubts, and longings of the contemporary world. This dual attentiveness allows sermons to bridge the gap between Scripture and lived experience. As Graham Johnston (2001, 153) suggests, when listeners are guided through the weight of evidence, truths that might have initially been rejected are often received with openness.

Ultimately, the task of preaching is not simply to declare truth, but to guide people into it. This requires dependence on the Holy Spirit, disciplined preparation, and a commitment to clarity. As preachers engage both God’s Word and the world it addresses, they participate in the ongoing work of leading people into the knowledge of God. For this reason, it remains essential to faithfully and accurately handle Scripture, “rightly dividing the word of truth,” so that lives may be transformed not just momentarily, but for eternity.

**Q:** How does a strong introduction help a sermon?

**A:** The introduction gives the first impression of the preacher and the sermon. If an audience is bored, offended, or confused by the introduction, they will not likely tune in for the rest of the sermon. A good introduction will hook the audience, build confidence in the preacher, and keep people listening all the way to the end.

**Q:** What is the purpose of illustrations?

**A:** Illustrations give a more detailed explanation of the point through an example, catchy quote, or statistics that will increase the audience’s ability to understand what is being said. It is often a place where the biblical text meets real-life situations and helps the audience identify with what is being said.

**Q:** What is the role of a conclusion in a sermon?

**A:** The conclusion wraps up the sermon, gives it a feeling of completion, and brings the entire sermon to a place of decision. In the conclusion, the audience and preacher are now on the same page and the listeners are brought to a point of decision. The conclusion recaps the message and makes one final push to highlight the main proposition.

**Q:** Are you more receptive to deductive or inductive preaching? Why?

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 5.2.3 — Define deductive preaching and inductive preaching .

Sermon Organization

Once the homiletical outline is complete, organize the material for clear presentation. Every sermon should include the following: introduction, central proposition, main points, illustrations, applications, and conclusion.

Summarize key components of a sermon.

Introduction

The introduction is one of the most important parts of a sermon. An audience not hooked in the introduction will likely tune out the rest of the message. Launching directly into the central proposition and main points without getting the audience involved in the experience will cause many people to shut out the message and think about their lunch plans, to-do lists, or other daydreams. A strong introduction gets the audience’s attention, introduces the subject at hand, presents a reason this should matter to them, and leaves them anticipating the rest of the sermon.

Sermons can start in a variety of ways: with a quote, statistics, an anecdote, a joke, a drama, or a video clip. Use variety and carefully choose openings that best fit the topic. For example, the sermon on God’s detours might begin with a humorous story about a road trip that included a detour. Most in the audience can identify with such a detour and will find a personal connection with the preacher’s story. After this initial catch, the preacher can then relate this illustration to real life—the times people think they are headed one direction, only to find themselves in a wilderness. Through this application, the audience is able to identify wilderness times in their own lives. Memories are stirred, painful experiences are brought up, and everyone is emotionally charged.

Central Proposition

Taking advantage of this engagement, the preacher then sets up the idea that perhaps these confusing, hard times are actually designed by God. Through this statement, the audience begins to evaluate their circumstances and wonder, “Why would God send me to the wilderness?” “Does that make sense?” “But what about my situation?” Then the preacher can state the central proposition of the sermon, “Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.” The audience is now caught. They can identify with life’s detours but wonder, “Can these detours really accomplish God’s purposes?” Some will anticipate the sermon with excitement: “Wow, this is going to make sense of my life!” Others might anticipate the sermon with skepticism: “No way can detours in life actually be good.” Regardless of each person’s position, the people in the congregation are now ready to hear arguments about how detours can bring about God’s purposes in people’s lives. From this point, the minister is ready to launch into the main points.

The minister has options as to when to put in the central proposition. If the preacher wants the congregation to know up front that the sermon will prove the usefulness of detours, he or she should state the proposition in the introduction. However, the preacher might want to talk about detours, show the possible explanations for detours, and then help the congregation come to a specific conclusion. In these circumstances, the preacher will wait to present the central proposition until the conclusion. The reasons for these alternate sermon structures will be discussed later in this chapter.

Main Points

The main points of the sermon, as already discussed, give explanation of and support to the central proposition, regardless of where the proposition is mentioned. The main points should be presented clearly. For example, after the introduction is finished and the text is read, the first point might be stated as, “Journeys off the beaten trail can still accomplish our life’s purposes because, first, they are often for preparation.” This transitional statement hits on the central proposition and directly ties the first point to it. Each point should explain how or why it supports the central proposition and then include both an illustration and an application that will drive the point home.

Illustrations

Illustrations such as stories, quotes, or demonstrations help to further explain the main point in a memorable way. The third point in a sermon I preached on Leah and Rachel from Genesis 29 and 30 was, “God remembers us.” To illustrate this point, I told, in great detail, of the day my grandmother turned sixty-five.

This was the one birthday she had looked forward to for years. It meant retirement. It meant the long-promised sports car from grandpa. It was a milestone for her, so when she woke up that day, she had great anticipation. But something was wrong. It didn’t seem like anyone remembered! My grandfather, who had not forgotten a birthday in their forty-nine years of marriage, went about the morning without the hint of “happy birthday” on his lips. He smiled, said, “Good morning,” and got ready for church without saying anything. Grandma was crushed. How could he, of all birthdays, have forgotten this one? She didn’t want to say anything because she kept thinking he was going to surprise her. She looked around every room she entered, thinking he might have hidden her card somewhere, but in every room she was disappointed. Breakfast was rather quiet that morning. When they went to church, nothing was said. No one at church knew it was her big day, and she began to feel depressed. This was a huge birthday. Couldn’t someone have remembered? By the time church was over, Grandma was crushed, hurt, and sad and could hardly say a word. It was silent in the car on the way home, and she kept wondering, “How could everyone have forgotten that today was my day?” Tears were welling up and it was all she could do to hold them in. When they got home, Grandma quietly got out of the car and walked to the door. With a great sigh, she slowly turned the handle and swung the door open. “SURPRISE!” She was shocked to find her house filled with her children and grandchildren. She turned to Grandpa, who had a big smile on his face and a bright twinkle in his eye, and heard, “Happy birthday, darling.” She was not forgotten! Grandpa hadn’t forgotten. Her kids hadn’t forgotten. No . . . they had more than remembered. They were waiting for the perfect time to help her celebrate her big day. Grandma was remembered!

An illustration like this will most likely draw people in. Most have either been part of or received a surprise birthday. Most will identify with the feelings of being forgotten and the joy of being remembered. The illustration helps connect the fact that “God remembers us” with real life experiences. I used it to hook people into the idea of being remembered, and then used this illustration to give insight into Rachel’s plight.

For years, Rachel had wondered, “God, how could you have forgotten me?” Day after day she hoped for a son, but that hope began to fade. It seemed God remembered everyone but her. Did He even know she existed? Did He care? Everywhere she looked brought disappointment. She had prayed. She had begged. She had tried other means, but she still was barren. Every day she got up and thought, “Maybe today.” But nothing happened. All she found was silence. She was tired. She was sad. Then, one day, when she thought all hope was gone, she took hold of the doorknob. As the door swung open, there was God with His arms wide open saying, “Surprise! Rachel, I didn’t forget you. NO! I remember you. Here. Here is the son I have promised you.”

The illustration makes the text come alive. It takes a good point and makes it extraordinary. Suddenly, everyone can identify with Rachel and recognize the power of God remembering us. Illustrations help explain difficult or confusing points and are vital to presenting a powerful, memorable sermon.

Applications

In addition to the illustration, main points should include applications that make biblical text relevant to present-day life. For example, consider a sermon on the mandate to rejoice, even in times of suffering. One point could be, “Even though the present might require intense suffering, believers can rejoice because of Christ’s victory.” To illustrate this point, the preacher turns to Job and describes his horrific plight. Job loses everything in a matter of moments: his children, his servants, his home, his flocks, and his health. His wife rejects him, most of his friends desert him, and now he sits, with boils covering his body, considering his situation. He has suffered perhaps the most intense losses we can imagine, yet he says, “I know that my Redeemer lives, and that in the end he will stand upon the earth. And after my skin has been destroyed, yet in my flesh I will see God” (Job 19:25–26). After building up Job’s condition and quoting his amazing words, the application could be something like this:

The doctor says my cancer is terminal, but I know my Redeemer lives! I have lost my job and have no means to pay the bills, yet I know my Redeemer lives! I just lost my husband. I know my Redeemer lives! Life does not make sense, and I do not know what to do next, but I know my Redeemer lives!

The application presents real-life situations that help people recognize how the point being made really does apply to their lives. People may see how amazing it was for Job to say these words, but they fail to relate his words to their own experiences. By identifying possible life situations and responding to those situations with Job’s words, the congregation is suddenly thrust into the text and challenged with the idea that they can praise God, even in the midst of suffering, because He is victorious. Often, a person will both hear and agree with the point but not truly embrace it or have an “A-ha” moment until the application. Ask God to direct you and help you give applications that will be specific enough to directly speak to people about their present life situations.

Conclusion

When the sermon is finished, it is important to have a solid conclusion that will tie together all the points and make a final attempt to drive the message home. A sermon without a conclusion makes an abrupt stop, leaves people hanging, and diminishes the power of the sermon. As the sermon draws to an end, recap the proposition and main points, and end with one final illustration and / or application that will reverberate in the listener’s heart and mind. The conclusion should be as well thought out as the rest of the sermon. You should know where you want the sermon to go and stop when you get there. Use the conclusion to clarify the message, wrap things up, and make sure the congregation lands where you have desired they land.

The goal of preaching is life change. The entire sermon works toward this goal, and the conclusion is where life change is addressed. Those who were curious and skeptical during your introduction should hopefully embrace your proposition by the conclusion. Now they understand that life’s purposes can be accomplished through detours, and they want time to process this new understanding in light of their own circumstances.

If you give people a list of things to remember, they will most likely remember the first and last items. The same is true of sermons. Even if the audience cannot recall all of the main points, they will remember the beginning and the end. It is important that in the introduction and conclusion, you expend all you can in helping people remember the central thrust of the message.

Indicate how attention to sermon organization aids in the presentation and reception of the message.

A neat, organized, and well-planned sermon will help the audience hear, understand, and remember what is said. Sermons that jump around, are jumbled, and have no clear direction make it difficult for the audience to catch meaningful thoughts to help their Christian life. Think about memorable sermons in your own life. They were clear, easy to follow, and catching, right? The elements that make a sermon easy to follow are the elements your audience will look for as well. This kind of sermon does not just happen.

Clear, powerful sermons require time and work. A preacher committed to life change will spend the time and energy needed to create a meaningful sermon. It may be tempting to put off the sermon until Saturday afternoon and hope God’s anointing will bring it all together when you step to the pulpit Sunday morning. However, this kind of procrastination will show and will ultimately decrease your effectiveness in preaching. The hard work of struggling for a catchy introduction; a clear, correct central proposition; well-worded main points; helpful illustrations and applications; and a fitting conclusion will be worth the effort when you see lives changed for eternity.

Define deductive preaching and inductive preaching .

Deductive and Inductive Preaching

The clarity of a message comes from its structure. The components of a sermon (introduction, proposition, main points, illustrations / applications, and conclusion) will not change. However, how they are structured can be changed to increase the sermon’s effectiveness. The two ways to format a sermon are deductively and inductively. Both types of sermons can be effective, but it is imperative to know your audience to determine which best suits them.

Deductive preaching begins with a declaration and then lays out the support for this declaration. This is most helpful for modern audiences who want to hear a truth and then see its proofs, although it can be effectively used with postmodernists when it is the best way to present the biblical text. This type of preaching “works from the whole (this is what I’m telling you today) to the particulars (this is why), and from the known to the unknown” (Johnston 2001, 151). If our Exodus sermon is preached deductively, the proposition will be stated up front and the proofs will follow. From the beginning, the sermon states that detours can be used to accomplish life’s purpose, and the main points serve to support this.

Inductive preaching reverses the order. The sermon begins by stating a need, presents answers or solutions for meeting this need, and ends by allowing the hearer to come to the same conclusion as the preacher. Rather than stating up front what the preacher wants the audience to believe, the preacher guides people into weighing all the evidence and arriving at the same conclusion. If the Exodus sermon is preached inductively, the minister would begin with the need—everyone experiences detours in life. Why do these detours happen? The main points would present the world’s answers (Murphy’s Law, things just happen, there is no reason to life, etc.), other religious answers (karma, fate, evil, etc.), and biblical answers (God’s desire to prepare, guide, and build trust). By the end of the illustrations and the evidence, the preacher and the audience arrive at the same conclusion: life’s purpose can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail because God is in control and has the bigger picture in mind. He sees our lives, has purposes for our lives, and can use all situations to bring about His purposes for us.

Strengths and Weaknesses

The form of preaching used depends largely on the audience and the text. Modernists want to hear truths up front, followed by supporting evidence. Deductive sermons excel at this. Placing the central proposition first evokes questions from the audience: “What does this mean?” “Can this be true?” “How do I do this?” The goal is to present powerful evidence that supports the proposition and ultimately convinces people of its truth. While this approach can be helpful for modernists, the postmodern members of your audience might be offended by the assertion of truth at the beginning. Postmodernists do not want to be told what to believe. They want to journey, discover, and embrace a position once all the evidence has been examined. A deductive sermon can be viewed as being closed-minded and cold, causing this audience to be hostile from the beginning.

The deductive approach is also ineffective if it places the climax of the sermon in the introduction and leaves the rest of the sermon in its wake. If the sermon can state an attention-grabbing statement that hooks listeners and then list supporting proofs, it is powerful. If the central proposition “steals its own thunder,” the deductive sermon fails miserably.

In the deductive Exodus example, the proposition does not present the climax. If it had stated that God uses detours to prepare us for His purposes, the audience would have received the entire sermon in one sentence and had nothing else to look forward to. Our proposition, however, states a rather shocking truth: hard times can be for good reason, but it does not resolve the mystery. Each sermon point reveals more supporting evidence and builds momentum through the conclusion. A deductive sermon that keeps the audience hooked from start to finish is a great success.

Inductive sermons are most helpful for postmodernists. Truths are presented in ways that allow more people to embrace them. Since postmodernists want to journey, the inductive sermon presents a variety of solutions to the problem. This type of sermon engages listeners, draws them into the sermon process, and allows them to discover scriptural truths. The listeners can hear the evidence, think through all aspects of the issue, and decide for themselves that the godly answer is the best answer.

Modernists are not as excited with this form of preaching, for inductive sermons will not present answers only from the Bible. While the purpose is to show how worldly answers fall short of God’s answers, some in the audience will object to anything being presented from the pulpit that does not come straight from Scripture. The minister must clearly present non-biblical answers as being answers the world gives. Evidence in the sermon must be set up in such a way as to clearly demonstrate that God’s way is best. Inductive sermons guide people to the proposition. However, if the sermon cannot present a clear message with the proposition at the end, the deductive method should be used instead.

Justify the relevance of inductive preaching in a postmodern world.

Effective Preaching in a Postmodern World

In today’s cultural landscape, many people are not looking to be told what to believe—they are looking to discover truth for themselves. In a world shaped by skepticism, personal experience, and competing truth claims, listeners often resist conclusions that feel imposed upon them. Instead, they are more open to a journey that allows them to wrestle with questions and arrive at truth with a sense of ownership. For this reason, the inductive method of preaching remains especially effective. It invites listeners into a process of discovery rather than demanding immediate agreement.

The ministry of Jesus provides a compelling model of this approach. In John 4, Jesus does not begin with a direct declaration of His identity. Rather than opening with, “I am the Messiah,” He begins with a simple, relational request: “Will you give me a drink?” This starting point engages the woman within her everyday reality. As the conversation unfolds, Jesus gently exposes deeper layers of her life—her isolation, her broken relationships, and her spiritual confusion. He does not force a conclusion upon her; instead, He leads her step by step toward recognizing her own need. Only after she arrives at that realization does Jesus reveal himself as the Messiah. Because she participates in the discovery, the truth is not resisted; it is embraced.

This approach speaks directly to our present context. Modern listeners, shaped by social media, cultural pluralism, and distrust of authority, often react defensively to bold theological assertions made without relational or intellectual engagement. A statement such as “God is holy,” while true, may initially be perceived as abstract, irrelevant, or even confrontational. However, when a preacher begins with shared human experience—our longing for justice, our outrage at evil, our desire for things to be made right, listeners begin to recognize a deeper moral reality already at work within them. From there, the truth of God’s holiness emerges not as an imposed idea, but as the most reasonable and compelling conclusion.

Effective preaching today requires both theological faithfulness and cultural awareness. The preacher must carefully study the biblical text while also understanding the questions, doubts, and longings of the contemporary world. This dual attentiveness allows sermons to bridge the gap between Scripture and lived experience. As Graham Johnston (2001, 153) suggests, when listeners are guided through the weight of evidence, truths that might have initially been rejected are often received with openness.

Ultimately, the task of preaching is not simply to declare truth, but to guide people into it. This requires dependence on the Holy Spirit, disciplined preparation, and a commitment to clarity. As preachers engage both God’s Word and the world it addresses, they participate in the ongoing work of leading people into the knowledge of God. For this reason, it remains essential to faithfully and accurately handle Scripture, “rightly dividing the word of truth,” so that lives may be transformed not just momentarily, but for eternity.

**Q:** How does a strong introduction help a sermon?

**A:** The introduction gives the first impression of the preacher and the sermon. If an audience is bored, offended, or confused by the introduction, they will not likely tune in for the rest of the sermon. A good introduction will hook the audience, build confidence in the preacher, and keep people listening all the way to the end.

**Q:** What is the purpose of illustrations?

**A:** Illustrations give a more detailed explanation of the point through an example, catchy quote, or statistics that will increase the audience’s ability to understand what is being said. It is often a place where the biblical text meets real-life situations and helps the audience identify with what is being said.

**Q:** What is the role of a conclusion in a sermon?

**A:** The conclusion wraps up the sermon, gives it a feeling of completion, and brings the entire sermon to a place of decision. In the conclusion, the audience and preacher are now on the same page and the listeners are brought to a point of decision. The conclusion recaps the message and makes one final push to highlight the main proposition.

**Q:** Are you more receptive to deductive or inductive preaching? Why?

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 5.2.4 — Justify the relevance of inductive preaching in a postmodern world.

Sermon Organization

Once the homiletical outline is complete, organize the material for clear presentation. Every sermon should include the following: introduction, central proposition, main points, illustrations, applications, and conclusion.

Summarize key components of a sermon.

Introduction

The introduction is one of the most important parts of a sermon. An audience not hooked in the introduction will likely tune out the rest of the message. Launching directly into the central proposition and main points without getting the audience involved in the experience will cause many people to shut out the message and think about their lunch plans, to-do lists, or other daydreams. A strong introduction gets the audience’s attention, introduces the subject at hand, presents a reason this should matter to them, and leaves them anticipating the rest of the sermon.

Sermons can start in a variety of ways: with a quote, statistics, an anecdote, a joke, a drama, or a video clip. Use variety and carefully choose openings that best fit the topic. For example, the sermon on God’s detours might begin with a humorous story about a road trip that included a detour. Most in the audience can identify with such a detour and will find a personal connection with the preacher’s story. After this initial catch, the preacher can then relate this illustration to real life—the times people think they are headed one direction, only to find themselves in a wilderness. Through this application, the audience is able to identify wilderness times in their own lives. Memories are stirred, painful experiences are brought up, and everyone is emotionally charged.

Central Proposition

Taking advantage of this engagement, the preacher then sets up the idea that perhaps these confusing, hard times are actually designed by God. Through this statement, the audience begins to evaluate their circumstances and wonder, “Why would God send me to the wilderness?” “Does that make sense?” “But what about my situation?” Then the preacher can state the central proposition of the sermon, “Our life’s purposes can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail.” The audience is now caught. They can identify with life’s detours but wonder, “Can these detours really accomplish God’s purposes?” Some will anticipate the sermon with excitement: “Wow, this is going to make sense of my life!” Others might anticipate the sermon with skepticism: “No way can detours in life actually be good.” Regardless of each person’s position, the people in the congregation are now ready to hear arguments about how detours can bring about God’s purposes in people’s lives. From this point, the minister is ready to launch into the main points.

The minister has options as to when to put in the central proposition. If the preacher wants the congregation to know up front that the sermon will prove the usefulness of detours, he or she should state the proposition in the introduction. However, the preacher might want to talk about detours, show the possible explanations for detours, and then help the congregation come to a specific conclusion. In these circumstances, the preacher will wait to present the central proposition until the conclusion. The reasons for these alternate sermon structures will be discussed later in this chapter.

Main Points

The main points of the sermon, as already discussed, give explanation of and support to the central proposition, regardless of where the proposition is mentioned. The main points should be presented clearly. For example, after the introduction is finished and the text is read, the first point might be stated as, “Journeys off the beaten trail can still accomplish our life’s purposes because, first, they are often for preparation.” This transitional statement hits on the central proposition and directly ties the first point to it. Each point should explain how or why it supports the central proposition and then include both an illustration and an application that will drive the point home.

Illustrations

Illustrations such as stories, quotes, or demonstrations help to further explain the main point in a memorable way. The third point in a sermon I preached on Leah and Rachel from Genesis 29 and 30 was, “God remembers us.” To illustrate this point, I told, in great detail, of the day my grandmother turned sixty-five.

This was the one birthday she had looked forward to for years. It meant retirement. It meant the long-promised sports car from grandpa. It was a milestone for her, so when she woke up that day, she had great anticipation. But something was wrong. It didn’t seem like anyone remembered! My grandfather, who had not forgotten a birthday in their forty-nine years of marriage, went about the morning without the hint of “happy birthday” on his lips. He smiled, said, “Good morning,” and got ready for church without saying anything. Grandma was crushed. How could he, of all birthdays, have forgotten this one? She didn’t want to say anything because she kept thinking he was going to surprise her. She looked around every room she entered, thinking he might have hidden her card somewhere, but in every room she was disappointed. Breakfast was rather quiet that morning. When they went to church, nothing was said. No one at church knew it was her big day, and she began to feel depressed. This was a huge birthday. Couldn’t someone have remembered? By the time church was over, Grandma was crushed, hurt, and sad and could hardly say a word. It was silent in the car on the way home, and she kept wondering, “How could everyone have forgotten that today was my day?” Tears were welling up and it was all she could do to hold them in. When they got home, Grandma quietly got out of the car and walked to the door. With a great sigh, she slowly turned the handle and swung the door open. “SURPRISE!” She was shocked to find her house filled with her children and grandchildren. She turned to Grandpa, who had a big smile on his face and a bright twinkle in his eye, and heard, “Happy birthday, darling.” She was not forgotten! Grandpa hadn’t forgotten. Her kids hadn’t forgotten. No . . . they had more than remembered. They were waiting for the perfect time to help her celebrate her big day. Grandma was remembered!

An illustration like this will most likely draw people in. Most have either been part of or received a surprise birthday. Most will identify with the feelings of being forgotten and the joy of being remembered. The illustration helps connect the fact that “God remembers us” with real life experiences. I used it to hook people into the idea of being remembered, and then used this illustration to give insight into Rachel’s plight.

For years, Rachel had wondered, “God, how could you have forgotten me?” Day after day she hoped for a son, but that hope began to fade. It seemed God remembered everyone but her. Did He even know she existed? Did He care? Everywhere she looked brought disappointment. She had prayed. She had begged. She had tried other means, but she still was barren. Every day she got up and thought, “Maybe today.” But nothing happened. All she found was silence. She was tired. She was sad. Then, one day, when she thought all hope was gone, she took hold of the doorknob. As the door swung open, there was God with His arms wide open saying, “Surprise! Rachel, I didn’t forget you. NO! I remember you. Here. Here is the son I have promised you.”

The illustration makes the text come alive. It takes a good point and makes it extraordinary. Suddenly, everyone can identify with Rachel and recognize the power of God remembering us. Illustrations help explain difficult or confusing points and are vital to presenting a powerful, memorable sermon.

Applications

In addition to the illustration, main points should include applications that make biblical text relevant to present-day life. For example, consider a sermon on the mandate to rejoice, even in times of suffering. One point could be, “Even though the present might require intense suffering, believers can rejoice because of Christ’s victory.” To illustrate this point, the preacher turns to Job and describes his horrific plight. Job loses everything in a matter of moments: his children, his servants, his home, his flocks, and his health. His wife rejects him, most of his friends desert him, and now he sits, with boils covering his body, considering his situation. He has suffered perhaps the most intense losses we can imagine, yet he says, “I know that my Redeemer lives, and that in the end he will stand upon the earth. And after my skin has been destroyed, yet in my flesh I will see God” (Job 19:25–26). After building up Job’s condition and quoting his amazing words, the application could be something like this:

The doctor says my cancer is terminal, but I know my Redeemer lives! I have lost my job and have no means to pay the bills, yet I know my Redeemer lives! I just lost my husband. I know my Redeemer lives! Life does not make sense, and I do not know what to do next, but I know my Redeemer lives!

The application presents real-life situations that help people recognize how the point being made really does apply to their lives. People may see how amazing it was for Job to say these words, but they fail to relate his words to their own experiences. By identifying possible life situations and responding to those situations with Job’s words, the congregation is suddenly thrust into the text and challenged with the idea that they can praise God, even in the midst of suffering, because He is victorious. Often, a person will both hear and agree with the point but not truly embrace it or have an “A-ha” moment until the application. Ask God to direct you and help you give applications that will be specific enough to directly speak to people about their present life situations.

Conclusion

When the sermon is finished, it is important to have a solid conclusion that will tie together all the points and make a final attempt to drive the message home. A sermon without a conclusion makes an abrupt stop, leaves people hanging, and diminishes the power of the sermon. As the sermon draws to an end, recap the proposition and main points, and end with one final illustration and / or application that will reverberate in the listener’s heart and mind. The conclusion should be as well thought out as the rest of the sermon. You should know where you want the sermon to go and stop when you get there. Use the conclusion to clarify the message, wrap things up, and make sure the congregation lands where you have desired they land.

The goal of preaching is life change. The entire sermon works toward this goal, and the conclusion is where life change is addressed. Those who were curious and skeptical during your introduction should hopefully embrace your proposition by the conclusion. Now they understand that life’s purposes can be accomplished through detours, and they want time to process this new understanding in light of their own circumstances.

If you give people a list of things to remember, they will most likely remember the first and last items. The same is true of sermons. Even if the audience cannot recall all of the main points, they will remember the beginning and the end. It is important that in the introduction and conclusion, you expend all you can in helping people remember the central thrust of the message.

Indicate how attention to sermon organization aids in the presentation and reception of the message.

A neat, organized, and well-planned sermon will help the audience hear, understand, and remember what is said. Sermons that jump around, are jumbled, and have no clear direction make it difficult for the audience to catch meaningful thoughts to help their Christian life. Think about memorable sermons in your own life. They were clear, easy to follow, and catching, right? The elements that make a sermon easy to follow are the elements your audience will look for as well. This kind of sermon does not just happen.

Clear, powerful sermons require time and work. A preacher committed to life change will spend the time and energy needed to create a meaningful sermon. It may be tempting to put off the sermon until Saturday afternoon and hope God’s anointing will bring it all together when you step to the pulpit Sunday morning. However, this kind of procrastination will show and will ultimately decrease your effectiveness in preaching. The hard work of struggling for a catchy introduction; a clear, correct central proposition; well-worded main points; helpful illustrations and applications; and a fitting conclusion will be worth the effort when you see lives changed for eternity.

Define deductive preaching and inductive preaching .

Deductive and Inductive Preaching

The clarity of a message comes from its structure. The components of a sermon (introduction, proposition, main points, illustrations / applications, and conclusion) will not change. However, how they are structured can be changed to increase the sermon’s effectiveness. The two ways to format a sermon are deductively and inductively. Both types of sermons can be effective, but it is imperative to know your audience to determine which best suits them.

Deductive preaching begins with a declaration and then lays out the support for this declaration. This is most helpful for modern audiences who want to hear a truth and then see its proofs, although it can be effectively used with postmodernists when it is the best way to present the biblical text. This type of preaching “works from the whole (this is what I’m telling you today) to the particulars (this is why), and from the known to the unknown” (Johnston 2001, 151). If our Exodus sermon is preached deductively, the proposition will be stated up front and the proofs will follow. From the beginning, the sermon states that detours can be used to accomplish life’s purpose, and the main points serve to support this.

Inductive preaching reverses the order. The sermon begins by stating a need, presents answers or solutions for meeting this need, and ends by allowing the hearer to come to the same conclusion as the preacher. Rather than stating up front what the preacher wants the audience to believe, the preacher guides people into weighing all the evidence and arriving at the same conclusion. If the Exodus sermon is preached inductively, the minister would begin with the need—everyone experiences detours in life. Why do these detours happen? The main points would present the world’s answers (Murphy’s Law, things just happen, there is no reason to life, etc.), other religious answers (karma, fate, evil, etc.), and biblical answers (God’s desire to prepare, guide, and build trust). By the end of the illustrations and the evidence, the preacher and the audience arrive at the same conclusion: life’s purpose can be accomplished through journeys off the beaten trail because God is in control and has the bigger picture in mind. He sees our lives, has purposes for our lives, and can use all situations to bring about His purposes for us.

Strengths and Weaknesses

The form of preaching used depends largely on the audience and the text. Modernists want to hear truths up front, followed by supporting evidence. Deductive sermons excel at this. Placing the central proposition first evokes questions from the audience: “What does this mean?” “Can this be true?” “How do I do this?” The goal is to present powerful evidence that supports the proposition and ultimately convinces people of its truth. While this approach can be helpful for modernists, the postmodern members of your audience might be offended by the assertion of truth at the beginning. Postmodernists do not want to be told what to believe. They want to journey, discover, and embrace a position once all the evidence has been examined. A deductive sermon can be viewed as being closed-minded and cold, causing this audience to be hostile from the beginning.

The deductive approach is also ineffective if it places the climax of the sermon in the introduction and leaves the rest of the sermon in its wake. If the sermon can state an attention-grabbing statement that hooks listeners and then list supporting proofs, it is powerful. If the central proposition “steals its own thunder,” the deductive sermon fails miserably.

In the deductive Exodus example, the proposition does not present the climax. If it had stated that God uses detours to prepare us for His purposes, the audience would have received the entire sermon in one sentence and had nothing else to look forward to. Our proposition, however, states a rather shocking truth: hard times can be for good reason, but it does not resolve the mystery. Each sermon point reveals more supporting evidence and builds momentum through the conclusion. A deductive sermon that keeps the audience hooked from start to finish is a great success.

Inductive sermons are most helpful for postmodernists. Truths are presented in ways that allow more people to embrace them. Since postmodernists want to journey, the inductive sermon presents a variety of solutions to the problem. This type of sermon engages listeners, draws them into the sermon process, and allows them to discover scriptural truths. The listeners can hear the evidence, think through all aspects of the issue, and decide for themselves that the godly answer is the best answer.

Modernists are not as excited with this form of preaching, for inductive sermons will not present answers only from the Bible. While the purpose is to show how worldly answers fall short of God’s answers, some in the audience will object to anything being presented from the pulpit that does not come straight from Scripture. The minister must clearly present non-biblical answers as being answers the world gives. Evidence in the sermon must be set up in such a way as to clearly demonstrate that God’s way is best. Inductive sermons guide people to the proposition. However, if the sermon cannot present a clear message with the proposition at the end, the deductive method should be used instead.

Justify the relevance of inductive preaching in a postmodern world.

Effective Preaching in a Postmodern World

In today’s cultural landscape, many people are not looking to be told what to believe—they are looking to discover truth for themselves. In a world shaped by skepticism, personal experience, and competing truth claims, listeners often resist conclusions that feel imposed upon them. Instead, they are more open to a journey that allows them to wrestle with questions and arrive at truth with a sense of ownership. For this reason, the inductive method of preaching remains especially effective. It invites listeners into a process of discovery rather than demanding immediate agreement.

The ministry of Jesus provides a compelling model of this approach. In John 4, Jesus does not begin with a direct declaration of His identity. Rather than opening with, “I am the Messiah,” He begins with a simple, relational request: “Will you give me a drink?” This starting point engages the woman within her everyday reality. As the conversation unfolds, Jesus gently exposes deeper layers of her life—her isolation, her broken relationships, and her spiritual confusion. He does not force a conclusion upon her; instead, He leads her step by step toward recognizing her own need. Only after she arrives at that realization does Jesus reveal himself as the Messiah. Because she participates in the discovery, the truth is not resisted; it is embraced.

This approach speaks directly to our present context. Modern listeners, shaped by social media, cultural pluralism, and distrust of authority, often react defensively to bold theological assertions made without relational or intellectual engagement. A statement such as “God is holy,” while true, may initially be perceived as abstract, irrelevant, or even confrontational. However, when a preacher begins with shared human experience—our longing for justice, our outrage at evil, our desire for things to be made right, listeners begin to recognize a deeper moral reality already at work within them. From there, the truth of God’s holiness emerges not as an imposed idea, but as the most reasonable and compelling conclusion.

Effective preaching today requires both theological faithfulness and cultural awareness. The preacher must carefully study the biblical text while also understanding the questions, doubts, and longings of the contemporary world. This dual attentiveness allows sermons to bridge the gap between Scripture and lived experience. As Graham Johnston (2001, 153) suggests, when listeners are guided through the weight of evidence, truths that might have initially been rejected are often received with openness.

Ultimately, the task of preaching is not simply to declare truth, but to guide people into it. This requires dependence on the Holy Spirit, disciplined preparation, and a commitment to clarity. As preachers engage both God’s Word and the world it addresses, they participate in the ongoing work of leading people into the knowledge of God. For this reason, it remains essential to faithfully and accurately handle Scripture, “rightly dividing the word of truth,” so that lives may be transformed not just momentarily, but for eternity.

**Q:** How does a strong introduction help a sermon?

**A:** The introduction gives the first impression of the preacher and the sermon. If an audience is bored, offended, or confused by the introduction, they will not likely tune in for the rest of the sermon. A good introduction will hook the audience, build confidence in the preacher, and keep people listening all the way to the end.

**Q:** What is the purpose of illustrations?

**A:** Illustrations give a more detailed explanation of the point through an example, catchy quote, or statistics that will increase the audience’s ability to understand what is being said. It is often a place where the biblical text meets real-life situations and helps the audience identify with what is being said.

**Q:** What is the role of a conclusion in a sermon?

**A:** The conclusion wraps up the sermon, gives it a feeling of completion, and brings the entire sermon to a place of decision. In the conclusion, the audience and preacher are now on the same page and the listeners are brought to a point of decision. The conclusion recaps the message and makes one final push to highlight the main proposition.

**Q:** Are you more receptive to deductive or inductive preaching? Why?

**A:** Answers will vary.

## 6 - Speaker / Audience Dynamics Part 1: Know Your Audience

### The Non-Christian Church

#### 6.1.1 — Explain how postmodernism has affected the makeup of the church.

Postmodernism’s Effect on the Church

If you could travel back in time to the 1940s, you would most likely find a rather “Christianized” society. Most would be familiar with the God of the Bible, the Bible’s content, and Christian lingo. They would easily follow a sermon that assumed Christian understanding. Preachers would not take the time to establish the Bible’s credibility or feel they needed to explain that the God of the Bible is different from other gods in the world. These concepts would remain unspoken because everyone shared a similar worldview and standard of belief. In fact, in the 1940s, prayer, God, and the Bible were all part of public education. Society held a standard of morality that mirrored that of the Bible. Although not everyone was “saved,” most lived rather moral “Christian” lives.

Explain how postmodernism has affected the makeup of the church.

The audience preachers face today is very different from the audience of the 1940s. The move toward postmodernism requires ministers to rethink both how to do ministry and how to communicate the gospel. The postmodern influence in the church requires preachers to explain everything and assume nothing. Postmodernists do not want to be told what to believe; they need to understand the why of what is preached and then have the time and space to journey toward their own acceptance of faith.

Understanding that modern and postmodern people share many similarities is important. The “essential qualities that define humanity have not changed and presumably will not change” (Arthurs 2004, 177). We are created beings who have fallen short of God’s plan and are in need of salvation. Arthurs (2004, 178) observes, “Our real needs have not changed, nor has the gospel.” Jesus is the same, “yesterday and today and forever” (Hebrews 13:8). His message is true, powerful, and able to transform people of all eras. The message will not change; however, the way it is presented needs to change with the audience. Since modern people receive truth differently than postmodernists, it is important to understand how postmodernism has affected the church.

Reaching Postmodernists

In the modern world, preaching served as a primary way of confronting people with truth. Preachers made declarations like, “The Bible says you should do X ,” and assumed it was embraced because the audience believed the Bible to be an ultimate authority as both sacred and religious. Sermons boldly confronted people with truth claims and asked for an immediate response. People heard the biblical claims and quickly decided, “Yes, I believe,” or “No, I do not.”

The postmodern shift has changed this. Preachers cannot begin with “The Bible says you should do X ” because many people do not know what is in the Bible and will not automatically believe it is a sacred authority. It is a religious book, but only one among many. How can one know it is ultimate truth? Because of this shift in thinking, the church does not look like it used to. A postmodern world means effective churches have a large percentage of seekers on a journey of faith. In fact, Jeffrey Arthurs (2004, 191) says, “‘As a result of the pluralism of our society, the preacher may never assume that the congregation has already been converted.’ We should not assume that our listeners, even if they are baptized church members, fully understand and embrace the Christian [story].”

Postmodernists do not accept anything without examination, experimentation, and understanding. Churches should be safe places for journey, and this requires flexibility, patience, time, involvement, and commitment on the part of preachers. Clear preaching in today’s world might not bring the quick response of faith acceptance that characterized the modern church, yet once the journey takes place, the postmodernist willing to embrace faith is more likely to do so with great zeal. Preachers should do everything to “skillfully illustrate what they mean and patiently demonstrate how they have reached their conclusions” (Arthurs 2004, 196). Then they should watch the exciting journey of discovery and belief that takes place in the postmodern hearer. Church is no longer a gathering of just Christians; the church is now a place of discovery for those looking for truth.

Describe ways preachers can help the post-Christian audience embrace God.

Preaching to an Unbelieving Audience

Presenting God to an unbelieving audience requires boldness, Holy Spirit creativity, and trust in God’s power. In the past, a preacher easily presented biblical arguments because the Bible was commonly accepted as a trusted, religious reference book. Preachers argued for the evidence of the Bible’s truthfulness and believed everyone would accept it as God’s Word even before they read it. Today, a preacher must have the same trust in the Bible’s authority but be willing to let people approach the Bible with skepticism, questions, and the need to examine the Bible to see if it is true (Johnston 2001, 88–89). The kinds of questions postmodernists bring to the table may seem threatening, sacrilegious, and unsettling. Helping someone to journey requires honesty, flexibility, patience, and trust.

Reaching Unbelievers Requires:

Honesty

Preachers must show how they arrive at their conclusions and be willing to say, “I don’t know” when the postmodernist asks a question he or she has not yet figured out.
