Postmodernists want to know your story and understand how and why you believe. You will not know everything and might not have thought through all the questions the seeker will bring up. Honesty about your own journey and the willingness to journey with the seeker for answers to the questions neither of you know will bring clarity to your message, your beliefs, and your presentation.

Flexibility and Patience

Preachers need flexibility and patience during the journey. Seekers may seem to take too long to examine the faith. Watching them experiment and examine multiple religions as they search for truth is difficult. Patience for the long journey and flexibility during the journey are qualities that can speak volumes to the seeker and help him or her toward a decision. Johnston (2001, 90) observes, “If we as preachers are committed to the process of allowing our listeners to search, then people need to be given reassurances, both verbally and nonverbally, that they can suspend judgment on belief while being free to investigate the Bible for themselves.”

Trust

Preachers also need trust in God and His power to save. The journey of the seeker will take him or her through times of doubt and questioning. Faith in Christ will not be embraced without comparing who He is and claims to be with other religious teachings. The questions and the journey require preachers to trust that God is able to save, reveal himself in a variety of places, and lead the seeker to seeing, embracing, and knowing Christ.

**Q:** Describe how society viewed Christianity in the early to mid-1900s.

**A:** Most people accepted that the Bible was a religious authority that gave clear guidelines as to how people should live. Even though not everyone was saved, most lived moral “Christian” lives and openly embraced statements from the Bible as true.

**Q:** Where should preachers begin when addressing a post-Christian audience?

**A:** Preachers should begin with human needs and problems, show all of the possible solutions to the needs / problems, and then show how Christ makes sense and is the best solution. Preachers cannot assume the audience has any Christian background or knowledge. They must explain everything and assume nothing.

**Q:** In a postmodern world, how might people approach the Bible?

**A:** Postmodernists will not embrace the Bible without journey and comparison. They will approach it with a lot of questions and will at times be skeptical of its message and truthfulness.

### Church Language

#### 6.2.1 — Indicate the importance of avoiding Christian jargon in sermons.

Common Words in Preaching

Christians have lingo that can be confusing for non-Christians. Words like justification , sanctification , propitiation , and substitutionary atonement can perfectly say what the preacher wants to say, but might leave most of the audience in confusion as to just what was said. Using Christian jargon in sermons creates barriers that not only leave people wondering what was said, but can also leave some people feeling left out. Even words like saved , heathen , lost , and Christian can be difficult to overcome. The listener might wonder why people who look, act, and talk like him, are considered “saved” when he is not. Another hearer will be greatly offended to be called a “heathen.” After all, she knows some of the people in the church and they do not act much differently than she does. “I’m not lost,” another might say. “I know exactly where I am.” Christian is even difficult because many people in the world equate Christian with narrowminded, uptight, easily offended, ultra-conservative people who persecute those of other faiths. If the preacher urges people to become Christians, those who view Christians in this light will resist with everything in them.

Indicate the importance of avoiding Christian jargon in sermons.

Instead of pushing “Christian” lingo, the preacher may use words common to everyone—words that lack baggage—and speak in simpler, easier to understand terms. Instead of merely using the term Christian , the preacher might talk about following Christ. Instead of lost or saved , talk about those who search for truth and those who have embraced Christ. Depending on the audience, consider replacing theological terms with everyday, ordinary ones. This will make the sermon clearer for the audience and more likely bring about the desired results.

Explain how the preacher should incorporate Greek and Hebrew into a sermon.

Using Greek and Hebrew in Sermons

Part of the process of rethinking Christian jargon is also learning when and how to incorporate Greek and Hebrew in the actual sermon. Studying Greek and Hebrew will help the preacher gain insight into the biblical author’s purpose and meaning and will help the preacher prepare a sermon that is of excellent scholarship. While it is important to use the biblical languages in the course of sermon preparation, it is not always necessary to use the Greek and Hebrew in the sermon itself.

A good preacher will use the original texts to gain insight but will present his or her discovery in common, ordinary language. Rarely should the Greek or Hebrew be mentioned specifically in the sermon. Intricate word studies can show off a preacher’s skill but leave the congregation confused and unenlightened. The best way to preach is to use common, everyday language that clearly communicates. During sermon preparation, the preacher should ask himself or herself whether it is necessary to include a language study for the audience to fully understand. If there are other ways to express the meaning without interjecting Greek and Hebrew, those should be used. Only refer to the original language if absolutely necessary, and then use common language to explain the Greek or Hebrew study.

For instance, if you were preaching from Isaiah 53, your study of the Hebrew language would reveal several things. In verse four (NKJV), the Hebrew includes the words griefs and sorrows . If the preacher ignored the Hebrew, he or she would define these terms as they are often understood in English, and think of them as simply referring to mild sadness and heartache. Knowing the Hebrew language would help the preacher understand that the griefs and sorrows are more than casual things. Griefs refers to sickness and torments. Sorrows refer to anguish, pain, and intense suffering. The Hebrew language shows a more vivid picture than most English translations and reveals a powerful thought to the preacher during the sermon preparation. However, when presenting this, it is unnecessary to say, “The Hebrew word châlâh (griefs) means sickness and torments.”

The better way to include this word study might be to say, “When we read these words griefs and sorrows , our minds tend to tidy up the picture and think of times of rather minute sadness, but get your mind around what Christ really took on himself. He did not just take our sniffles and tears. He took our anguish, torments, pain, and suffering. The loss a mother feels at her son’s casket. The anguish and heartache at a body destroyed by disease and pain. The brokenness and burden from betrayal, theft, and violation. Christ took all this when He bore our griefs and our sorrows.” This kind of explanation lets the audience in on the word study without the preacher’s mentioning a word of Hebrew and without the audiences realizing they have just had a Hebrew lesson.

**Q:** What kind of Christian words do you think might be hard for a non-Christian to understand?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Why do you think Greek and Hebrew are important for sermons?

**A:** Answers will vary.

### Background Checks

#### 6.3.1 — Explain why knowing the audience’s background is essential for clear communication.

Preaching Appropriately for the Audience

It is important to word the sermon in a way that is clear and easily understood. It is also important to present a sermon that is appropriate for the audience. What a bad feeling it is for the preacher to be in the middle of a sermon and realize that the audience is in a different world. Delivering a sermon without audience connection can make the speaker feel like he or she is talking to the wind. One way for the preacher to avoid this disconnect is to know who the audience is before he or she steps to the pulpit.

Explain why knowing the audience’s background is essential for clear communication.

Know the Audience’s Spiritual Level

Knowing the audience’s spiritual level will help in sermon preparation. It would be foolish for a Spanish teacher to walk into a Spanish class and expect the students to instantly comprehend an entire lesson in Spanish. The teacher should first know who is in the class (age and intellect will affect where the teacher begins); how knowledgeable the class is of the language (the class could be beginner, intermediate, or advanced); and why the students in this class want to know Spanish (those needing to become fluent will have a different purpose than a group of tourists). A preacher would be just as foolish to preach a sermon with a deep theological subject without finding out whether the audience would be able to understand. For Bible school students, a deep theological sermon would be very appropriate. For a local congregation that is a mix of ages, educational backgrounds, and beliefs, this type of sermon would likely be inappropriate. For beginners, start at the beginning and move them toward deeper knowledge. For “advanced” Christians, start a bit deeper and challenge them to press further.

Know the Audience’s Interests and Backgrounds

Knowing your audience’s interests and backgrounds can help you build in illustrations and examples that will engage them in the sermon from the beginning and help explain the main points. As Johnston (2001, 73) aptly points out, “To speak of one’s own passion for rock collecting may well touch deeply within the speaker’s sphere but remains outside the sphere of the average listener.” Look for common experiences (high school, dating, pets, trips, sickness, embarrassing moments, etc.), local interests (the town’s annual apple fest, a sports team, a local legend, etc.), or a specific experience of the audience (a favorite song, a funny story of the church’s missions trip, the annoying water fountain in the lobby that sprays people, etc.). These types of experiences unify the group and give the audience an instant identification point that makes them a part of the sermon. They will identify with the preacher because the illustration is something they directly understand. Once this connection is made, the preacher can lead the audience to better understand the point being made. This is an important step in sermon clarity that will help an audience latch on to the message and move from their perspective to God’s (Johnston 2001, 72).

Know What the Audience Is Experiencing

Knowing what your listeners are going through can help you develop sermons that are relevant and that speak to pressing issues. A congregation that is experiencing loss, reconciliation, turmoil, job layoffs, testing, or triumph will need you to speak to these issues. At times you will identify with the congregation. For instance, a local tragedy occurs that is difficult for people to understand. You grieve with the rest of your congregation and openly share your feelings from the pulpit. By sharing in the grief, you have a platform to speak to the hurt and confusion and bring words of comfort, peace, and hope. At other times, you will disagree with the congregation and provide a word that brings change. For example, you recognize that the congregation has become hostile toward another local congregation. Knowing this to be a great error, you prepare a sermon that will encourage unity in the Body and love for those considered enemies. By knowing the listeners, a preacher is better able to speak timely words from God.

The congregation will not listen to the sermon if they believe the preacher to be indifferent, disconnected, harsh, or uncaring. A good preacher will know the listeners—what makes them tick, what angers them, what impassions them, what gives them life, what causes them grief—and use these points of response to involve the congregation in the sermon delivery. The prophet Nathan did this with king David (2 Samuel 12:1–13). Nathan took David’s shepherding background and developed a parable of a poor shepherd whose only beloved lamb was stolen by a rich man and sacrificed for the rich man’s guest. The story of a shepherd brought David into immediate identification with Nathan’s words. No other story or accusation could have accomplished what the story of a shepherd did. Nathan knew David’s life and found a way to present David’s sin with as much clarity and power as possible. For David, the story immediately cut him to the heart and brought complete repentance. A good preacher will know the congregation and tailor the sermon to connect and challenge them toward life change.

List ways the preacher can identify with the audience.

Identifying with the Audience

Identifying with the audience does not just happen; it requires work and deliberate attention to what is going on within the hearers’ lives. As a preacher learns about the audience, he or she will begin to understand the issues that are important for them and will be able to intentionally address these issues in the sermon. Loscalzo (1992, 108) points out that some of the questions people ask will be general: What does it mean to be a disciple of Christ? How can faith in Christ be transformed into practice in the marketplace? If God is a loving God, why is there evil in the world? Other questions, however, will be more specific: How do I deal with my dishonest boss? What does God say about being a single parent? How do I deal with the death of my spouse? Which job should I choose? Knowing these dilemmas can help the preacher develop a sermon that will specifically answer these questions. The preacher might read a biblical text and ask, “How do the issues here relate to the issues faced by the congregation?” The preacher might also come to the Bible with a specific issue and look for the biblical solution. However the preacher approaches the topic, it is imperative that he or she pay attention to specific congregant issues as well as local and world issues in order to best identify with those in the pew. There are many specific things a preacher can do to increase audience connection.

Increase Audience Connection

1. Listen to the audience. Take time to listen to the congregation to find out what people are dealing with, concerned about, and struggling through. One practical way to do this is by regularly reviewing prayer requests, which often reveal what people are truly thinking about and wrestling with beneath the surface. Sermons that remain aloof and fail to get specific will rarely connect with people in significant ways. The best way to bring life change is to address real and specific life issues.

2. Study current events. People will sometimes try to separate the spiritual from the rest of their lives. There are “religious” things and “secular” things, and the two rarely meet. The problem is that as much as people try to live segregated lives, it is impossible for current events to be left at the door of the church. People see world conflicts, read about unemployment, fear high gas prices, wonder about global warming, and worry about what kind of world their children will live in. Sermons are a great way to address these issues and show God’s way in a mixed-up world. To ignore these events would be to ignore the biggest concerns in peoples’ lives and leave them thinking God is unconcerned and unable to deal with these issues.

3. Work on creativity. In the movies, preachers are often portrayed as droning on and on while the congregation sits expressionless and bored. Preaching does not have to be a monotonous “speech” that people endure once a week. Preaching that connects will be fun, engaging, and exciting. God does not live in a box, and neither should preaching. Study current trends and interests, and spend time on the delivery and illustration side of the sermons as well as on the exegetical side.

4. Tell your story. People are much more apt to listen to a person who is believable. At times, preachers are placed on an unrealistic pedestal—preachers do not struggle with the same issues or sin, do not have the same interests, and are unable to identify fully with the congregation. The “super-spiritual” label given to preachers is sometimes hard to overcome. People will see the pastor in a high view. Though they respect the pastor, they will not always believe he or she truly understands the “common” person. One of the best ways to get people to hear the sermon is to tell your story. Talk about your struggles, your questions, your blunders, and your interests. Let people know that you really are just like them. Sharing your story with the congregation will increase their ability to hear, believe, and apply the message.

5. Practice and review the sermon well before preaching it. Allowing time between writing and preaching gives the message an opportunity to mature. As you revisit the sermon, ask whether it still speaks clearly to your audience. Consider whether recent events, changes in your congregation, or your own ministry experiences have provided fresh illustrations or applications that make the message more relevant. Read the sermon aloud and refine the wording so it sounds natural rather than scripted. Effective preachers continually evaluate, revise, and improve their messages so that God’s Word is communicated clearly and meaningfully.

**Q:** What kind of background information is important for understanding the audience?

**A:** Answers will vary but should include things like age, education level, spiritual level, audience experience, and local issues.

**Q:** Describe a situation from your local church that would be helpful for a preacher to consider before preparing a sermon.

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 6.3.2 — List ways the preacher can identify with the audience.

Preaching Appropriately for the Audience

It is important to word the sermon in a way that is clear and easily understood. It is also important to present a sermon that is appropriate for the audience. What a bad feeling it is for the preacher to be in the middle of a sermon and realize that the audience is in a different world. Delivering a sermon without audience connection can make the speaker feel like he or she is talking to the wind. One way for the preacher to avoid this disconnect is to know who the audience is before he or she steps to the pulpit.

Explain why knowing the audience’s background is essential for clear communication.

Know the Audience’s Spiritual Level

Knowing the audience’s spiritual level will help in sermon preparation. It would be foolish for a Spanish teacher to walk into a Spanish class and expect the students to instantly comprehend an entire lesson in Spanish. The teacher should first know who is in the class (age and intellect will affect where the teacher begins); how knowledgeable the class is of the language (the class could be beginner, intermediate, or advanced); and why the students in this class want to know Spanish (those needing to become fluent will have a different purpose than a group of tourists). A preacher would be just as foolish to preach a sermon with a deep theological subject without finding out whether the audience would be able to understand. For Bible school students, a deep theological sermon would be very appropriate. For a local congregation that is a mix of ages, educational backgrounds, and beliefs, this type of sermon would likely be inappropriate. For beginners, start at the beginning and move them toward deeper knowledge. For “advanced” Christians, start a bit deeper and challenge them to press further.

Know the Audience’s Interests and Backgrounds

Knowing your audience’s interests and backgrounds can help you build in illustrations and examples that will engage them in the sermon from the beginning and help explain the main points. As Johnston (2001, 73) aptly points out, “To speak of one’s own passion for rock collecting may well touch deeply within the speaker’s sphere but remains outside the sphere of the average listener.” Look for common experiences (high school, dating, pets, trips, sickness, embarrassing moments, etc.), local interests (the town’s annual apple fest, a sports team, a local legend, etc.), or a specific experience of the audience (a favorite song, a funny story of the church’s missions trip, the annoying water fountain in the lobby that sprays people, etc.). These types of experiences unify the group and give the audience an instant identification point that makes them a part of the sermon. They will identify with the preacher because the illustration is something they directly understand. Once this connection is made, the preacher can lead the audience to better understand the point being made. This is an important step in sermon clarity that will help an audience latch on to the message and move from their perspective to God’s (Johnston 2001, 72).

Know What the Audience Is Experiencing

Knowing what your listeners are going through can help you develop sermons that are relevant and that speak to pressing issues. A congregation that is experiencing loss, reconciliation, turmoil, job layoffs, testing, or triumph will need you to speak to these issues. At times you will identify with the congregation. For instance, a local tragedy occurs that is difficult for people to understand. You grieve with the rest of your congregation and openly share your feelings from the pulpit. By sharing in the grief, you have a platform to speak to the hurt and confusion and bring words of comfort, peace, and hope. At other times, you will disagree with the congregation and provide a word that brings change. For example, you recognize that the congregation has become hostile toward another local congregation. Knowing this to be a great error, you prepare a sermon that will encourage unity in the Body and love for those considered enemies. By knowing the listeners, a preacher is better able to speak timely words from God.

The congregation will not listen to the sermon if they believe the preacher to be indifferent, disconnected, harsh, or uncaring. A good preacher will know the listeners—what makes them tick, what angers them, what impassions them, what gives them life, what causes them grief—and use these points of response to involve the congregation in the sermon delivery. The prophet Nathan did this with king David (2 Samuel 12:1–13). Nathan took David’s shepherding background and developed a parable of a poor shepherd whose only beloved lamb was stolen by a rich man and sacrificed for the rich man’s guest. The story of a shepherd brought David into immediate identification with Nathan’s words. No other story or accusation could have accomplished what the story of a shepherd did. Nathan knew David’s life and found a way to present David’s sin with as much clarity and power as possible. For David, the story immediately cut him to the heart and brought complete repentance. A good preacher will know the congregation and tailor the sermon to connect and challenge them toward life change.

List ways the preacher can identify with the audience.

Identifying with the Audience

Identifying with the audience does not just happen; it requires work and deliberate attention to what is going on within the hearers’ lives. As a preacher learns about the audience, he or she will begin to understand the issues that are important for them and will be able to intentionally address these issues in the sermon. Loscalzo (1992, 108) points out that some of the questions people ask will be general: What does it mean to be a disciple of Christ? However the preacher approaches the topic, it is imperative that he or she pay attention to specific congregant issues as well as local and world issues in order to best identify with those in the pew. There are many specific things a preacher can do to increase audience connection.

Increase Audience Connection

Sermons that remain aloof and fail to get specific will rarely connect with people in significant ways. The best way to bring life change is to address real and specific life issues.

People see world conflicts, read about unemployment, fear high gas prices, wonder about global warming, and worry about what kind of world their children will live in. Sermons are a great way to address these issues and show God’s way in a mixed-up world. To ignore these events would be to ignore the biggest concerns in peoples’ lives and leave them thinking God is unconcerned and unable to deal with these issues.

3. Work on creativity. In the movies, preachers are often portrayed as droning on and on while the congregation sits expressionless and bored. Preaching does not have to be a monotonous “speech” that people endure once a week. Preaching that connects will be fun, engaging, and exciting. God does not live in a box, and neither should preaching. Study current trends and interests, and spend time on the delivery and illustration side of the sermons as well as on the exegetical side.

4. Tell your story. People are much more apt to listen to a person who is believable. At times, preachers are placed on an unrealistic pedestal—preachers do not struggle with the same issues or sin, do not have the same interests, and are unable to identify fully with the congregation. The “super-spiritual” label given to preachers is sometimes hard to overcome. People will see the pastor in a high view. Though they respect the pastor, they will not always believe he or she truly understands the “common” person. One of the best ways to get people to hear the sermon is to tell your story. Talk about your struggles, your questions, your blunders, and your interests. Let people know that you really are just like them. Sharing your story with the congregation will increase their ability to hear, believe, and apply the message.

5. Practice and review the sermon well before preaching it. Allowing time between writing and preaching gives the message an opportunity to mature. As you revisit the sermon, ask whether it still speaks clearly to your audience. **Q:** What kind of background information is important for understanding the audience?

**A:** Answers will vary but should include things like age, education level, spiritual level, audience experience, and local issues.

**Q:** Describe a situation from your local church that would be helpful for a preacher to consider before preparing a sermon.

**A:** Answers will vary.

### The Balancing Act

#### 6.4.1 — Describe the challenge preachers face in preaching to both Christian and non-Christian audiences.

Preaching to a Blended Audience

A blended audience is a wonderful thing because it brings great depth and diversity to the church. Having people at various points on the Christian spectrum is exciting because it means people are searching for, embracing, and growing in faith. A church with this kind of makeup is full of life. The challenge for preachers comes in knowing what to preach and how to preach it. Should the sermon focus on the seekers or the Christ-followers? Churches take a variety of approaches: from alternating sermon focuses to designating days for specific kinds of sermons (e.g., Sundays for evangelism and other midweek services for discipleship). No single answer is right for all churches. This will come through prayer, thought, and design. Even so, the preacher should be aware of who is in the audience and address as many groups as possible in the sermon.

Describe the challenge preachers face in preaching to both Christian and non-Christian audiences.

Preaching often takes place in a blended audience, where people are at different stages in their faith journey. Some are mature believers, others are new to faith, and some may not yet know Christ. Because of this reality, the preacher must communicate in a way that speaks to everyone in the room without losing clarity or focus. Regardless of who is present, the gospel should always be clearly presented. Even when it seems that everyone in the room is a believer, the preacher should remain faithful to proclaim the gospel, trusting that God will bring those who need to hear it. Faithful preaching creates space for God to draw unbelievers, and the consistent proclamation of the gospel ensures that when they come, they encounter the truth that leads to life.

Growing the congregation into deeper levels of faith is important and sermons should challenge people to be more like Christ. Remember, the goal is life change. Deeper issues are invaluable to creating a healthy, maturing body of believers. At the same time, a church that focuses only on believer issues is more likely to stagnate and exclude seekers. It is important to focus on “beginner” issues because even the most advanced Christian will need reminders of God’s love, the value of grace and mercy, and Christ’s work on the cross. These foundational teachings should be brought up frequently as reminders of God’s greatness and our dependence on Him. Aside from helping mature believers, the Christian basics are important for allowing seekers to have a place to learn about God, journey in faith, and eventually embrace faith. Both levels of sermons are needed, and preachers should work to incorporate basic and more advanced concepts into the sermon.

It can be difficult at times to know just how effective a sermon has been or whether a healthy beginner / advanced balance has been achieved. The change in people’s lives and the impact that sermons make cannot always be seen instantly. A preacher could become discouraged if immediate results are not seen. However, one must remember that the results are ultimately not up to the preacher. If the preacher bathes the sermon in prayer, works diligently to prepare a biblically sound sermon, and then gives equal effort toward creativity and clarity, he or she can entrust the sermon’s impact to the Holy Spirit and know He will ultimately bring the message to bear in the hearer’s heart. God’s Word is powerful, and the revelation and illumination of the Spirit will work to soften hard hearts and make the message alive to people. The Spirit will be the One who walks with people moment by moment and reminds them of the message they heard and how it applies in the present circumstance.

You might see an immediate result from your preaching, but more often it will be a gradual change that you witness over time. In fact, you might even be surprised years down the road when someone tells of the impact a particular sermon had on his or her life. Do all you can to be biblically sound and clear in your sermons. In your preparation, trust the Holy Spirit to guide you. Learn to hear the Spirit’s voice as you deliver the sermon, and trust Him to anoint and inspire you to present a message that is powerful and memorable. It will be amazing to see all He does in your hearers’ lives.

**Q:** List several positive things about having a blended (Christian and non-Christian) church.

**A:** Answers will vary. Some things could include a rich perspective on life and God’s working in it, depth and diversity, excitement, a realization that the church is growing, signs that the church is alive and making a difference.

## 7 - Speaker/Audience Dynamics Part 2: Know Yourself

### The Preacher and Sermon Presentation

#### 7.1.1 — Explain the importance of being “real.”

The Authentic Preacher

Our postmodern world longs for authenticity. The advancement of today’s technology causes people to constantly ask, “Is that real?” People watch television, search the internet, look at photographs, and never know the genuineness of what they see because alterations in these fields are so easy.

Explain the importance of being “real.”

The ability to manipulate pictures and films creates doubt and leads people to be skeptical toward what they see. In the same way, people are skeptical when what they see does not match what they hear. Many leaders from all areas of life have lived inconsistent lives. Politicians, preachers, CEOs, and public servants have said one thing while doing another. People want something that is true, consistent, and real. When it comes to preaching, people will be skeptical of sermons if the sermons come from preachers who are “unreal.”

When a preacher steps to the pulpit, people want to see a real, “just like me” type of person. The congregation needs someone who is transparent, fallible, and genuine. People will shut out a preacher who changes personality in the pulpit, professes self-perfection, makes comments that are inconsistent or impossible to follow, and appears plastic, fake, and distant. People need a preacher who is like them and can show them the way.

Transparency

First, people want a person who is transparent. For too long, ministers have been told to distance themselves from the congregation. The perception has been that preachers should be holy people who minister in times of crisis; they should be known for their strength, their immovability, their example, and their emotional grounding. The problem with this kind of perception is that preachers are not immovable towers of strength who are always emotionally grounded. True, preachers should be strong people who help others through life’s difficulties. However, preachers are not always strong or emotionally stable. Preachers are people who feel, struggle, and are moved just like everyone else. Modern people want the tower of strength, but postmodern people want the “real” person. Transparent preachers will show their vulnerabilities, emotions, fears, worries, joys, and questions. They will admit when they do not have all the answers, and will be honest about their own questions.

Postmodernists are on a journey, and they need a preacher who can be honest enough to admit he or she is on a journey too. If preachers portray themselves as always having it together, never bothered by life or the curve balls that life throws at them, postmodernists will fear that embracing Christ will turn them into someone who cannot be truthful about life and how it affects them. However, when preachers are transparent about their humanness, postmodernists will see that embracing Christ does not mean giving up the very things that make humans human. People need “real” preachers, and transparency shows them that preachers are “just like them.”

Fallibility

Second, people need to see a preacher who is fallible. The pulpit is not a confessional and should never be the place where the minister divulges his or her every sin. Nor should the preacher boast in mistakes and constantly profess his or her fallen state. No one should glory in sin; however, the pulpit is not a stage where a minister presents himself or herself as a perfect being. Being fallible shows people you are real, and not a stiff, uncompassionate, unrealistic theologian living in isolation from the rest of the world. Preachers are rarely unseen. Even when it seems no one is watching, preachers can be assured that how they react, what they say, or where they go will be known by at least one person in the congregation. People will know the times you mess up, fail in parenting, dishonor your spouse, lose your temper, or stretch the truth. They know you are not perfect, but they need to hear you say it too.

If you step to the pulpit in the “preacher mode” and hold yourself up as an example of godliness—one who does not struggle as others do—your words will never be heard. No one is perfect. Sometimes you will get it right, and through your example, others will too. But only presenting your victories and never showing your disastrous blunders will make your message more difficult to swallow. Sometimes the best explanation will come through telling your mistakes: “I struggle with always honoring my wife.” “I struggle with impure thoughts.” “I lost my temper this week and it wasn’t pretty.” These types of personal, revealing illustrations admit your humanity and help people to identify with what you are saying. The words you speak are heard more clearly when it is apparent that you are preaching as much to yourself as to others.

People need to know you are just like them and that God’s grace can be as helpful in their lives as His grace has been in yours. Today’s people do not want harsh condemnation from a person who has it all together. They need identification with the speaker and a message of, “This has worked for me, and look how badly I messed up.” Your story—good and bad—will create identification with the audience and make your words heard more clearly.

Genuineness

Third, people need to see a person who is genuine. Our world struggles with being genuine. Consider, for instance, the words I’m sorry . These words have lost much of their punch because they are seriously overused. Whether or not a person truly feels sorry, the words pop out. A banker makes a customer angry by refusing a transaction and says, “I’m sorry, you don’t have money right now.” A cashier smashes your bread with the milk jug and says casually, “I’m sorry.” You bump someone in line and without looking up you say, “I’m sorry.” People say, “I’m sorry you don’t feel well” with about as much meaning as, “I’m sorry, but I don’t understand what you just said.” What does “I’m sorry” mean anymore? People are not always authentic with one another. “I’m sorry” does not express true empathy any more than the question, “How are you?” invites you to tell how you are really doing.

The result of this loss of authenticity is that true, genuine concern for others is greatly lacking. When you step to the pulpit, people do not want to hear trite words. They want real words from a real person who really feels them. A compassionless pastor who speaks about compassion and an aloof preacher who preaches on bearing each other’s burdens will not be clearly heard. People want a preacher who is genuine through and through. The way you present your message will determine how genuinely you will be perceived. Even if you are a compassionate person, people will not automatically perceive this about you if you talk with a scowl on your face or a growl in your throat. Be who you are and present that with as much care as possible. If you are happy, show it. If you are sad, show it. Masking your true nature and trying to present something else will turn you into an actor. Without the traits of authenticity, a sermon will rarely do more than occupy a few minutes of someone’s Sunday morning. Make your presentation heard by being real in the pulpit.

The Preacher’s Personality

Part of being “real” in the pulpit is allowing your genuine personality to come through. Authenticity matters because people already know who you are outside of the sermon. They interact with you, observe your life, and form trust based on consistency. When a preacher adopts a different persona in the pulpit—one that feels exaggerated, forced, or disconnected—listeners are often distracted by the inconsistency rather than drawn into the message. Instead of focusing on the truth being communicated, they begin to question its sincerity.

Effective preaching does not require imitating someone else’s style or personality. There is no need to perform or adopt the voice, mannerisms, or delivery of another preacher. God has uniquely shaped each communicator, and that uniqueness is not a weakness—it is a strength. Faithful and impactful preaching flows from authenticity, where the preacher’s true self aligns with the message being proclaimed. When preachers embrace who God created them to be, their words carry greater credibility, clarity, and connection.

Personality creates diversity and excitement, and each person’s personality will build God’s kingdom through ways that other personalities cannot. It is important to be creative in sermon presentation and at times work beyond your comfort level. However, always remain true to who you are. People know you , and they want to see you in the pulpit, not some other.

**Q:** How important is transparency to you?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What does being genuine mean to you?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** Describe how your personality is a strength in your preaching.

**A:** Answers will vary.

### The Preacher’s Life Presentation

#### 7.2.1 — Explain the importance of living the Word you preach.

It was said earlier that an effective preacher would practice what he or she preaches. This cannot be overstated. Who you are speaks louder than what you say. James speaks of this plainly throughout his book: faith without works is dead (James 2:17). In chapter 2, James gives an illustration of a person in need of clothing and food coming to the door of a Christian for help. The Christian blesses the person, saying, “I wish you well; keep warm and well fed” (v. 16), and then sends the destitute person away without a supply of food or clothes. James says that a “Christian” like this has no faith because true faith will show in one’s actions. It is often said, “Do what I say and not what I do.” However, this is impossible to separate in preaching. As much as a preacher would like for people to do as the sermon says and not as he or she does, it will never be so.

Explain the importance of living the Word you preach.

In 1 Timothy 4:10–12, Paul says,

(And for this we labor and strive), that we have put our hope in the living God, who is the Savior of all men, and especially of those who believe. Command and teach these things. Don’t let anyone look down on you because you are young, but set an example for the believers in speech, in life, in love, in faith and in purity.

Timothy’s greatest sermon was not in his words; Timothy’s greatest sermon came through his actions. People watch a preacher and gauge their own behavior by the preacher’s. All the words in the world cannot make up for a lack of true godliness in everyday life. Though many things form the character list for a pastor, the following two items cannot be compromised in any way.

The Preacher’s Character

Preachers must have integrity and must follow through on their promises. They must refrain from meaningless comments like, “I’m praying for you,” unless such words are true reflections of what the preachers will do. It is important to be someone people can count on and someone who handles mistakes in a humble, godly fashion.

Integrity does not mean being perfect; that is beyond anyone. Integrity means living as pure a life as possible and being open, honest, up front, humble, and loving. Preachers must avoid exaggeration and “white lies.” They should also avoid gossip, slander, and questionable jokes. Living a life of purity in thought and action requires living what one preaches before it is preached.

How would people respond to a sermon if the speaker preaches on honesty but is known to tell white lies, to boast about breaking speed limits, or to never follow through with promises? How could people receive a preacher’s message on loving others, when he or she constantly speaks to his or her spouse with spiteful words, yells at the kids, or never wears a smile? The greatest sermon is preached through one’s actions. Preachers must therefore live in dependence on God and show others how to do what is preached.

Equally significant to preachers maintaining good character is their commitment to personal, spiritual enrichment. A preacher can use the strongest words and illustrations to persuade the congregation that personal, spiritual commitment is vital, but if he or she is not personally committed to prayer and Bible study, the congregation will not be either. Of greatest importance for any minister is his or her own spiritual life.

The demands people place on preachers today are incredible. Preachers often face a job description that expects them to be visionaries with the ability to lead people; organized, charismatic, influential, motivating, and nurturing; and compassionate counselors and mentors. The ideal candidate will have exceptional oratory skills with the ability to preach and teach clearly, yet still be available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week. Those unwilling or unable to meet these requirements need not apply.

These demands are superhuman and cause preachers to overextend and burn out. Even with these kinds of demands out there, however, preachers cannot allow pressures, or the spiritual work of ministry to take the place of cultivating a personal relationship with God. True preaching flows out of a heart that is in relationship with God. This does not mean that all sermons will be easy to prepare or deliver. It does mean that the heart and life message put into sermons will be from a true, active, growing relationship with God.

Paul stresses this to Timothy. In 1 Timothy 4:7–8, Paul advises,

Have nothing to do with godless myths and old wives’ tales; rather, train yourself to be godly. For physical training is of some value, but godliness has value for all things, holding promise for both the present life and the life to come.

For Paul, spiritual commitment was the highest commitment Timothy could make. More than anything else, Timothy should devote himself to godliness. After this, he was to preach, teach, train, and counsel. The genuine, clearly heard preacher is not perfect, but is sincere in his or her commitment to living rightly before God.

Describe the balance a preacher should have between confidence and humility.

Confidence and Humility

Being a “real” person in the pulpit and allowing actions to speak louder than words can cause some misgivings in the preacher. If the preacher is not perfect, can he or she rightly stand before the congregation and confidently declare, “Thus says the Lord”? Yes, a preacher can have full faith, trust, and confidence in the Word of God; the sermon that presents this message can also be presented with great confidence. True, it is very intimidating to know that one’s life is of as much importance as the message. However, this does not mean the preacher should shrink from the high calling of presenting God’s Word. Stepping to the pulpit is a weighty task that requires a life commitment by the preacher. A preacher who lacks confidence in the message will convey this lack of confidence to the hearers. The one who evaluates the cost and is willing to both live and speak life-changing sermons can be confident that God’s Word is powerful and, through the work of the Holy Spirit, will eternally change people’s lives.

However, a caution must be given. Confidence should never give way to arrogance. Arrogance must never be part of the minister’s life. An arrogant preacher presents an arrogant message that is unattractive to people. The message that is spoken might be true, but the arrogance with which it is presented will distort the words and cause people to resist God. Preachers stand as representatives of God. If they are arrogant and condescending, people will think God is also arrogant and condescending.

Even though the preacher might have biblical education, years of experience, and a multitude of skills, it is imperative that he or she learn to walk in humility and present sermons in humility. The preacher is a vessel God uses to present His Word; he or she should preach with a personal humility that says, “God’s grace is for me too.” A real, approachable, genuine minister will exhibit a humble life that sees the good in others, praises the triumphs of others, and glories in the grace and love of God that have been poured out on everyone alike. Preachers are servants who pour out their lives through sermons and submit themselves to others “out of reverence for Christ” (Ephesians 5:21).

Paul spoke with great authority and confidence. He was even so bold as to instruct the Corinthians to follow him as he followed Christ (1 Corinthians 11:1); yet in his boldness, Paul was not arrogant. He knew he was a sinner who had been saved by God’s mercy. He remembered the mess he was in without Christ, and he knew that even now, he was not perfect. In Philippians 3:13, he admits he has not fully attained all he should be, and in several places, he admits he is the worst sinner of all (1 Timothy 1:15–16). Paul was confident in God and in his role as God’s servant. He boldly preached the gospel message, but never with arrogance. Paul submitted himself to God and to the church, and his example of the balance between confidence and humility is one that preachers should follow today.

**Q:** Think about someone you consider to be a good preacher. How does this person’s life compare with the message he or she presents?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How should a preacher number his or her priorities?

**A:** Though some answers will vary, the list should place his or her personal relationship with God as number one, family as number two, and ministry as number three.

**Q:** Was Paul arrogant, confident, or humble?

**A:** Answers will vary; however, answers should show Paul to be a balance of confident and humble. Paul was not arrogant. He lived a life of great humility, but at the same time was confident in God and His Word.

#### 7.2.2 — Describe the balance a preacher should have between confidence and humility.

It was said earlier that an effective preacher would practice what he or she preaches. This cannot be overstated. Who you are speaks louder than what you say. James speaks of this plainly throughout his book: faith without works is dead (James 2:17). In chapter 2, James gives an illustration of a person in need of clothing and food coming to the door of a Christian for help. The Christian blesses the person, saying, “I wish you well; keep warm and well fed” (v. 16), and then sends the destitute person away without a supply of food or clothes. James says that a “Christian” like this has no faith because true faith will show in one’s actions. It is often said, “Do what I say and not what I do.” However, this is impossible to separate in preaching. As much as a preacher would like for people to do as the sermon says and not as he or she does, it will never be so.

Explain the importance of living the Word you preach.

In 1 Timothy 4:10–12, Paul says,

(And for this we labor and strive), that we have put our hope in the living God, who is the Savior of all men, and especially of those who believe. Command and teach these things. Don’t let anyone look down on you because you are young, but set an example for the believers in speech, in life, in love, in faith and in purity.

Timothy’s greatest sermon was not in his words; Timothy’s greatest sermon came through his actions. People watch a preacher and gauge their own behavior by the preacher’s. All the words in the world cannot make up for a lack of true godliness in everyday life. Though many things form the character list for a pastor, the following two items cannot be compromised in any way.

The Preacher’s Character

Preachers must have integrity and must follow through on their promises. They must refrain from meaningless comments like, “I’m praying for you,” unless such words are true reflections of what the preachers will do. It is important to be someone people can count on and someone who handles mistakes in a humble, godly fashion.

Integrity does not mean being perfect; that is beyond anyone. Integrity means living as pure a life as possible and being open, honest, up front, humble, and loving. Preachers must avoid exaggeration and “white lies.” They should also avoid gossip, slander, and questionable jokes. Living a life of purity in thought and action requires living what one preaches before it is preached.

How would people respond to a sermon if the speaker preaches on honesty but is known to tell white lies, to boast about breaking speed limits, or to never follow through with promises? How could people receive a preacher’s message on loving others, when he or she constantly speaks to his or her spouse with spiteful words, yells at the kids, or never wears a smile? The greatest sermon is preached through one’s actions. Preachers must therefore live in dependence on God and show others how to do what is preached.

Equally significant to preachers maintaining good character is their commitment to personal, spiritual enrichment. A preacher can use the strongest words and illustrations to persuade the congregation that personal, spiritual commitment is vital, but if he or she is not personally committed to prayer and Bible study, the congregation will not be either. Of greatest importance for any minister is his or her own spiritual life.

The demands people place on preachers today are incredible. Preachers often face a job description that expects them to be visionaries with the ability to lead people; organized, charismatic, influential, motivating, and nurturing; and compassionate counselors and mentors. The ideal candidate will have exceptional oratory skills with the ability to preach and teach clearly, yet still be available twenty-four hours a day, seven days a week. Those unwilling or unable to meet these requirements need not apply.

These demands are superhuman and cause preachers to overextend and burn out. Even with these kinds of demands out there, however, preachers cannot allow pressures, or the spiritual work of ministry to take the place of cultivating a personal relationship with God. True preaching flows out of a heart that is in relationship with God. This does not mean that all sermons will be easy to prepare or deliver. It does mean that the heart and life message put into sermons will be from a true, active, growing relationship with God.

Paul stresses this to Timothy. In 1 Timothy 4:7–8, Paul advises,

Have nothing to do with godless myths and old wives’ tales; rather, train yourself to be godly. For physical training is of some value, but godliness has value for all things, holding promise for both the present life and the life to come.

For Paul, spiritual commitment was the highest commitment Timothy could make. More than anything else, Timothy should devote himself to godliness. After this, he was to preach, teach, train, and counsel. The genuine, clearly heard preacher is not perfect, but is sincere in his or her commitment to living rightly before God.

Describe the balance a preacher should have between confidence and humility.

Confidence and Humility

Being a “real” person in the pulpit and allowing actions to speak louder than words can cause some misgivings in the preacher. If the preacher is not perfect, can he or she rightly stand before the congregation and confidently declare, “Thus says the Lord”? Yes, a preacher can have full faith, trust, and confidence in the Word of God; the sermon that presents this message can also be presented with great confidence. True, it is very intimidating to know that one’s life is of as much importance as the message. However, this does not mean the preacher should shrink from the high calling of presenting God’s Word. Stepping to the pulpit is a weighty task that requires a life commitment by the preacher. A preacher who lacks confidence in the message will convey this lack of confidence to the hearers. The one who evaluates the cost and is willing to both live and speak life-changing sermons can be confident that God’s Word is powerful and, through the work of the Holy Spirit, will eternally change people’s lives.

However, a caution must be given. Confidence should never give way to arrogance. Arrogance must never be part of the minister’s life. An arrogant preacher presents an arrogant message that is unattractive to people. The message that is spoken might be true, but the arrogance with which it is presented will distort the words and cause people to resist God. Preachers stand as representatives of God. If they are arrogant and condescending, people will think God is also arrogant and condescending.

Even though the preacher might have biblical education, years of experience, and a multitude of skills, it is imperative that he or she learn to walk in humility and present sermons in humility. The preacher is a vessel God uses to present His Word; he or she should preach with a personal humility that says, “God’s grace is for me too.” A real, approachable, genuine minister will exhibit a humble life that sees the good in others, praises the triumphs of others, and glories in the grace and love of God that have been poured out on everyone alike. Preachers are servants who pour out their lives through sermons and submit themselves to others “out of reverence for Christ” (Ephesians 5:21).

He was even so bold as to instruct the Corinthians to follow him as he followed Christ (1 Corinthians 11:1); yet in his boldness, Paul was not arrogant. Paul submitted himself to God and to the church, and his example of the balance between confidence and humility is one that preachers should follow today.

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How should a preacher number his or her priorities?

**A:** Though some answers will vary, the list should place his or her personal relationship with God as number one, family as number two, and ministry as number three.

**Q:** Was Paul arrogant, confident, or humble?

**A:** Answers will vary; however, answers should show Paul to be a balance of confident and humble. Paul was not arrogant. He lived a life of great humility, but at the same time was confident in God and His Word.

## 8 - Creating Engaging Worship Experiences

### Preaching in Light of the Worship Experience

#### 8.1.1 — Describe the contemporary preacher’s goal and responsibility.

The Preacher’s Goal and Responsibility

The word hearing that Paul uses in Romans 10:17 connotes the idea of “paying attention to” or “responding to.” Like travelers guilty of not listening to the flight attendants’ pre-takeoff announcements, people in America today are tuning out the church and contemporary preachers. The Bible has the greatest story ever told, yet too few people are listening. This tragedy prompts Paul to echo the prophet Isaiah’s lament, “Who has believed our message?” (Romans 10:16).

Describe the contemporary preacher’s goal and responsibility.

With church attendance in North America continuing its decline, many pastors mourn with Paul and Isaiah (Isaiah 53:1) as people fail to listen to the message and believe in Christ. Even Jesus, the greatest preacher ever, shared this frustration with an unresponsive audience as He cried, “He who has ears, let him hear” (Matthew 11:15; 13:9, 43).

Nevertheless, although today’s preacher is not held responsible to make the audience believe in Christ, the preacher is called to speak in such a way as to make the audience at least listen. Contemporary preachers are called to speak the gospel in the language of the culture to which they are ministering. The gospel has an attractive, magnetic power contained within itself. In fact, when preached in the language of the culture, the gospel itself promises to yield life-changing results in that it will not return void (Isaiah 55:11).

In order for the gospel to impact the life of the hearer, it must be effectively communicated. Of course, the basic components of communication involve a sender, a receiver, and a message. However, God creatively wired individuals to communicate with more than just words. In fact, sociologists and psychologists agree that people send, receive, and process information through an integrated use of multiple sensory receptors. For example, some individuals process information more readily through sight rather than sound, while others, due to their genetic design or based on their past learning experiences, receive and process information more effectively through touch, taste, or smell. Ultimately, God has provided various multisensory receptors in an effort to aid the communication process.

Indicate how Holy Communion engages multisensory worship.

Holy Communion: A Multisensory Approach

For centuries, the message of the faith has been passed down through creative, multidimensional communication techniques that engage all the senses. One area this multisensory approach can be found is in the ordinance of Holy Communion. According to the Scriptures, Christians are called to remember the sacrificial act of God’s love through Christ’s death on the cross. God foretold this act of love when He delivered Israel from four hundred years of Egyptian captivity.

This deliverance came through a unique and unusual event when God asked Israel to take the blood from the slaughtered lamb and place it on top of door frames of the houses where they ate the lamb. This one-time event would be a sign for the Lord to pass over impending judgment on the house of Israel, thus saving them from death and destruction. The Lord said, “This is a day you are to commemorate; for the generations to come you shall celebrate it as a festival to the Lord—a lasting ordinance” (Exodus 12:14). While God never instructed Israel to slaughter a lamb and place the blood on the door frames again, they were commanded to commemorate this day of deliverance by holding an annual festival that included a meal, retelling the story, and prayers of thanksgiving and remembrance.

Fourteen hundred years later, on the night of His betrayal, Jesus gathered His disciples to celebrate this ancient tradition. However, the Lord added a new twist to the age-old ceremony. The wine representing the blood of the lamb that was placed over the door frames of ancient Israel would now symbolize His blood that would be poured out on the cross for all humanity’s sins. In the same way, the unleavened bread would no longer represent the bread they ate with the meal on the first Passover. Instead, it would now symbolize Jesus’ body that was broken and beaten when the iniquity of humanity was laid upon Him. Jesus concluded the new covenant ceremony by saying to His disciples that whenever they eat the bread and drink the wine in remembrance, they “proclaim the Lord’s death until he comes” (1 Corinthians 11:26).

The remembrance and proclamation of Jesus’ death and resurrection is a prime example of God’s desire to employ a multisensory approach when sharing the message of redemption and salvation. God authored creative, multisensory communication by taking Holy Communion—perhaps the most significant spiritual event to be remembered and proclaimed to future generations—and utilizing all the senses to help listeners and followers of the faith to never forget it.

The audio and visual senses are engaged as those receiving Communion look and listen to the pastor retelling the story of Christ’s death and resurrection. Next, taste and tactile senses are aroused when the listener holds and ingests the bread and contents of the cup. In addition, a normal practice in the Communion celebration, especially in orthodox Christianity, included the burning of incense or candles to engage the olfactory senses. The fragrance represents the prayers of thanksgiving wafting heavenward throughout the time of remembrance. The message of God’s redemption through Holy Communion is communicated in a multisensory manner to help the observers understand and remember the faith.

Describe biblical accounts from Early Church history that implemented multisensory worship experiences.

Implementing Multisensory Worship Experiences

The use of a multisensory communication approach to creating engaging worship experiences is not new to the church. As we have just seen, God himself began it when He spoke to Israel for the first time as a nation upon their deliverance from Egypt. Prophets carried the creative torch in the Old Testament as some of the most innovative communicators of the faith to ever walk the planet. The writer of Hebrews said, “In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets at many times and in various ways” (Hebrews 1:1). These prophets were divinely inspired spokesmen for God who used a variety of communication methods to deliver the divine revelation. The revelation was presented as law, history, poetry, and prophecy. Sometimes it was written, while other times it was spoken. Sometimes it was communicated through dreams, visions, symbols, or even pantomime. But whatever the method used, the revelation of God to the Jews was multisensory and diverse.

John explained, in no uncertain terms, that the Word of God was to be communicated in multidimensional living color when He said, “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God . . . The Word became flesh and made his dwelling among us” (John 1:1, 14). The incarnation of Jesus, the Word of God wrapped in human skin, was differentiated from the written Word. The Word of God, according to the Gospel of John, is eternal, which means it is not restricted to a particular communication style. Thus, no single communication system is more holy than another.

Jesus’ preaching ministry reinforced creative multisensory communication as He often told stories and used object lessons from contemporary culture to get His point across. At times, He used a fictional narrative to communicate with the audience, while in other settings, Jesus used appropriate humor, visual aids (mustard seed, coin, fish, etc.), and even current events to connect with the hearer. Rarely do we see Jesus using a didactic, propositional, argument-driven communication style when speaking of His heavenly Father. Typically, Jesus preferred a more casual, conversational style of communication that drew the listeners into a story and compelled them to deal with uncomfortable issues of life and faith.

Moreover, many church leaders throughout the ages continued this creative tradition. For instance, the artists in the Middle Ages who introduced the world to the iconoclast art form used stained glass in their church architecture to communicate the faith. Upon entering the church, enormous stained glass windows on either side of the sanctuary surrounded the worshiper. Each handcrafted stained glass image told a story from Scripture. These images often included Jesus seeking the one escaped lamb from the sheepfold or the impartation of the Holy Spirit on the day of Christ’s baptism. The Early Church also used icons and religious art to communicate the story in a multisensory fashion. Historically, the church has used artistic advancements and modern technology to make the time of worship more engaging and memorable.

Today’s preachers are called to communicate with the same creativity and clarity imbedded in the celebration of the Passover, the Old Testament prophets, Jesus’ ministry, and other examples throughout church history and Scripture. Communicating with more than words allows the listener to engage the message at multiple entry points. Today’s preacher must utilize modern technology and creative styles of communication to connect with the audience. God designed it, Jesus modeled it, the Holy Spirit empowered it, and the Early Church practiced it.

Identify practical effects of creating worship experiences.

Practical Effects of the Worship Experience

When ministers create a unified worship experience that integrates a variety of communication techniques, they will discover practical benefits to their preaching ministry. First, implementing an integrative approach to sermon preparation will force the minister to plan well in advance. While creative moments of inspiration happen spontaneously, they take time and a team of people working together to bring those creative elements into reality.

For example, you might be planning a sermon on forgiveness. You will want to consult with your music team and worship leader so they can choose worship songs and perhaps a special solo that will communicate through music what you are trying to say with words. Also, if you use video clips as sermon illustrations, you will want to discuss the theme of your message with the multimedia team well in advance of the actual delivery date so they have enough lead time to find the right video to not only complement your message, but actually speak the Word through the video. You might also discuss with your staff or church leadership team the sermon theme and investigate personal testimonies within the congregation that relate to the theme. You will need time to contact the individuals and help them prepare their testimonies and practice delivering them prior to the day of the message. Of course, to coordinate these activities, you and your team will need to plan ahead.

Another benefit of an integrated approach is that more people are involved in creating the worship experience. Each team member brings a set of abilities, skills, and creative experiences to the table. This collaborative effort enhances the overall richness of the worship experience while also generating genuine camaraderie in the creative planning process. Shared ministry brings people together and makes ministry more enjoyable.

In addition to generating more creativity, advance planning lowers stress levels for pastors. Most preachers live in a week-to-week mindset when it comes to sermon preparation. Sunday is always looming on the horizon, and each day of the week, they grow more aware of that deadline. This ministry mindset creates a great deal of stress and frustration in the life of a minister. While the process of moving from a week-to-week mindset is difficult, the payoff is well worth the effort.

**Q:** What is the responsibility of today’s preacher?

**A:** To communicate the gospel in a way that compels a contemporary audience to listen to the message

**Q:** How does Holy Communion model a multisensory ministry approach?

**A:** Eyes—see the bread and cup. Ears—hear the prayers and the reading of Scripture. Nose—smell the incense (in some church traditions). Mouth—eat the bread and drink the wine. Hands—hold the elements in our hands.

**Q:** How did Jesus’ ministry reflect multisensory communication?

**A:** Jesus often communicated timeless truth through storytelling known as parables. The parables were often accompanied by visual object lessons.

**Q:** What benefit is there to advanced planning in sermon preparation?

**A:** It allows the minister and ministry teams to work together and be more creative. It also significantly reduces the level of stress and anxiety a minister experiences in a week-to-week ministry mindset.

The Preacher’s Goal and Responsibility
