**A:** Jesus often communicated timeless truth through storytelling known as parables. The parables were often accompanied by visual object lessons.

**Q:** What benefit is there to advanced planning in sermon preparation?

**A:** It allows the minister and ministry teams to work together and be more creative. It also significantly reduces the level of stress and anxiety a minister experiences in a week-to-week ministry mindset.

#### 8.1.3 — Describe biblical accounts from Early Church history that implemented multisensory worship experiences.

The Preacher’s Goal and Responsibility

The word hearing that Paul uses in Romans 10:17 connotes the idea of “paying attention to” or “responding to.” Like travelers guilty of not listening to the flight attendants’ pre-takeoff announcements, people in America today are tuning out the church and contemporary preachers. The Bible has the greatest story ever told, yet too few people are listening. This tragedy prompts Paul to echo the prophet Isaiah’s lament, “Who has believed our message?” (Romans 10:16).

Describe the contemporary preacher’s goal and responsibility.

With church attendance in North America continuing its decline, many pastors mourn with Paul and Isaiah (Isaiah 53:1) as people fail to listen to the message and believe in Christ. Even Jesus, the greatest preacher ever, shared this frustration with an unresponsive audience as He cried, “He who has ears, let him hear” (Matthew 11:15; 13:9, 43).

Nevertheless, although today’s preacher is not held responsible to make the audience believe in Christ, the preacher is called to speak in such a way as to make the audience at least listen. Contemporary preachers are called to speak the gospel in the language of the culture to which they are ministering. The gospel has an attractive, magnetic power contained within itself. In fact, when preached in the language of the culture, the gospel itself promises to yield life-changing results in that it will not return void (Isaiah 55:11).

In order for the gospel to impact the life of the hearer, it must be effectively communicated. Of course, the basic components of communication involve a sender, a receiver, and a message. However, God creatively wired individuals to communicate with more than just words. In fact, sociologists and psychologists agree that people send, receive, and process information through an integrated use of multiple sensory receptors. For example, some individuals process information more readily through sight rather than sound, while others, due to their genetic design or based on their past learning experiences, receive and process information more effectively through touch, taste, or smell. Ultimately, God has provided various multisensory receptors in an effort to aid the communication process.

Indicate how Holy Communion engages multisensory worship.

Holy Communion: A Multisensory Approach

For centuries, the message of the faith has been passed down through creative, multidimensional communication techniques that engage all the senses. One area this multisensory approach can be found is in the ordinance of Holy Communion. According to the Scriptures, Christians are called to remember the sacrificial act of God’s love through Christ’s death on the cross. God foretold this act of love when He delivered Israel from four hundred years of Egyptian captivity.

This deliverance came through a unique and unusual event when God asked Israel to take the blood from the slaughtered lamb and place it on top of door frames of the houses where they ate the lamb. This one-time event would be a sign for the Lord to pass over impending judgment on the house of Israel, thus saving them from death and destruction. The Lord said, “This is a day you are to commemorate; for the generations to come you shall celebrate it as a festival to the Lord—a lasting ordinance” (Exodus 12:14). While God never instructed Israel to slaughter a lamb and place the blood on the door frames again, they were commanded to commemorate this day of deliverance by holding an annual festival that included a meal, retelling the story, and prayers of thanksgiving and remembrance.

Fourteen hundred years later, on the night of His betrayal, Jesus gathered His disciples to celebrate this ancient tradition. However, the Lord added a new twist to the age-old ceremony. The wine representing the blood of the lamb that was placed over the door frames of ancient Israel would now symbolize His blood that would be poured out on the cross for all humanity’s sins. In the same way, the unleavened bread would no longer represent the bread they ate with the meal on the first Passover. Instead, it would now symbolize Jesus’ body that was broken and beaten when the iniquity of humanity was laid upon Him. Jesus concluded the new covenant ceremony by saying to His disciples that whenever they eat the bread and drink the wine in remembrance, they “proclaim the Lord’s death until he comes” (1 Corinthians 11:26).

The remembrance and proclamation of Jesus’ death and resurrection is a prime example of God’s desire to employ a multisensory approach when sharing the message of redemption and salvation. God authored creative, multisensory communication by taking Holy Communion—perhaps the most significant spiritual event to be remembered and proclaimed to future generations—and utilizing all the senses to help listeners and followers of the faith to never forget it.

The audio and visual senses are engaged as those receiving Communion look and listen to the pastor retelling the story of Christ’s death and resurrection. Next, taste and tactile senses are aroused when the listener holds and ingests the bread and contents of the cup. In addition, a normal practice in the Communion celebration, especially in orthodox Christianity, included the burning of incense or candles to engage the olfactory senses. The fragrance represents the prayers of thanksgiving wafting heavenward throughout the time of remembrance. The message of God’s redemption through Holy Communion is communicated in a multisensory manner to help the observers understand and remember the faith.

Describe biblical accounts from Early Church history that implemented multisensory worship experiences.

Implementing Multisensory Worship Experiences

The use of a multisensory communication approach to creating engaging worship experiences is not new to the church. As we have just seen, God himself began it when He spoke to Israel for the first time as a nation upon their deliverance from Egypt. Prophets carried the creative torch in the Old Testament as some of the most innovative communicators of the faith to ever walk the planet. The writer of Hebrews said, “In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets at many times and in various ways” (Hebrews 1:1). These prophets were divinely inspired spokesmen for God who used a variety of communication methods to deliver the divine revelation. The revelation was presented as law, history, poetry, and prophecy. Sometimes it was written, while other times it was spoken. Sometimes it was communicated through dreams, visions, symbols, or even pantomime. But whatever the method used, the revelation of God to the Jews was multisensory and diverse.

John explained, in no uncertain terms, that the Word of God was to be communicated in multidimensional living color when He said, “In the beginning was the Word, and the Word was with God, and the Word was God . . . The Word became flesh and made his dwelling among us” (John 1:1, 14). The incarnation of Jesus, the Word of God wrapped in human skin, was differentiated from the written Word. The Word of God, according to the Gospel of John, is eternal, which means it is not restricted to a particular communication style. Thus, no single communication system is more holy than another.

Jesus’ preaching ministry reinforced creative multisensory communication as He often told stories and used object lessons from contemporary culture to get His point across. At times, He used a fictional narrative to communicate with the audience, while in other settings, Jesus used appropriate humor, visual aids (mustard seed, coin, fish, etc.), and even current events to connect with the hearer. Rarely do we see Jesus using a didactic, propositional, argument-driven communication style when speaking of His heavenly Father. Typically, Jesus preferred a more casual, conversational style of communication that drew the listeners into a story and compelled them to deal with uncomfortable issues of life and faith.

Moreover, many church leaders throughout the ages continued this creative tradition. For instance, the artists in the Middle Ages who introduced the world to the iconoclast art form used stained glass in their church architecture to communicate the faith. Upon entering the church, enormous stained glass windows on either side of the sanctuary surrounded the worshiper. Each handcrafted stained glass image told a story from Scripture. These images often included Jesus seeking the one escaped lamb from the sheepfold or the impartation of the Holy Spirit on the day of Christ’s baptism. The Early Church also used icons and religious art to communicate the story in a multisensory fashion. Historically, the church has used artistic advancements and modern technology to make the time of worship more engaging and memorable.

Today’s preachers are called to communicate with the same creativity and clarity imbedded in the celebration of the Passover, the Old Testament prophets, Jesus’ ministry, and other examples throughout church history and Scripture. Communicating with more than words allows the listener to engage the message at multiple entry points. Today’s preacher must utilize modern technology and creative styles of communication to connect with the audience. God designed it, Jesus modeled it, the Holy Spirit empowered it, and the Early Church practiced it.

Identify practical effects of creating worship experiences.

Practical Effects of the Worship Experience

When ministers create a unified worship experience that integrates a variety of communication techniques, they will discover practical benefits to their preaching ministry. First, implementing an integrative approach to sermon preparation will force the minister to plan well in advance. While creative moments of inspiration happen spontaneously, they take time and a team of people working together to bring those creative elements into reality.

For example, you might be planning a sermon on forgiveness. You will want to consult with your music team and worship leader so they can choose worship songs and perhaps a special solo that will communicate through music what you are trying to say with words. Also, if you use video clips as sermon illustrations, you will want to discuss the theme of your message with the multimedia team well in advance of the actual delivery date so they have enough lead time to find the right video to not only complement your message, but actually speak the Word through the video. You might also discuss with your staff or church leadership team the sermon theme and investigate personal testimonies within the congregation that relate to the theme. You will need time to contact the individuals and help them prepare their testimonies and practice delivering them prior to the day of the message. Of course, to coordinate these activities, you and your team will need to plan ahead.

Another benefit of an integrated approach is that more people are involved in creating the worship experience. Each team member brings a set of abilities, skills, and creative experiences to the table. This collaborative effort enhances the overall richness of the worship experience while also generating genuine camaraderie in the creative planning process. Shared ministry brings people together and makes ministry more enjoyable.

In addition to generating more creativity, advance planning lowers stress levels for pastors. Most preachers live in a week-to-week mindset when it comes to sermon preparation. Sunday is always looming on the horizon, and each day of the week, they grow more aware of that deadline. This ministry mindset creates a great deal of stress and frustration in the life of a minister. While the process of moving from a week-to-week mindset is difficult, the payoff is well worth the effort.

**Q:** What is the responsibility of today’s preacher?

**A:** To communicate the gospel in a way that compels a contemporary audience to listen to the message

**Q:** How does Holy Communion model a multisensory ministry approach?

**A:** Eyes—see the bread and cup. Ears—hear the prayers and the reading of Scripture. Nose—smell the incense (in some church traditions). Mouth—eat the bread and drink the wine. Hands—hold the elements in our hands.

**Q:** How did Jesus’ ministry reflect multisensory communication?

**A:** Jesus often communicated timeless truth through storytelling known as parables. The parables were often accompanied by visual object lessons.

**Q:** What benefit is there to advanced planning in sermon preparation?

**A:** It allows the minister and ministry teams to work together and be more creative. It also significantly reduces the level of stress and anxiety a minister experiences in a week-to-week ministry mindset.

### Digital Age Worship

#### 8.2.1 — Identify ways to implement effective use of media in creating the worship experience.

Digital Technology in Preaching

The world isn’t just changing—it’s accelerating. New technologies aren’t trickling out anymore; they’re dropping daily, shaping how people think, communicate, and engage with truth. With every innovation, another wave of culture crashes up against the church. The most effective preachers and worship leaders today aren’t running from that reality—they’re learning how to use these tools for the kingdom of God. In the same way the Roman road system enabled the apostle Paul to carry the gospel across the first-century world, today’s digital landscape has the potential to carry the message of Christ to the literal ends of the earth in real time.

Identify ways to implement effective use of media in creating the worship experience.

The Gutenberg printing press was the defining communication breakthrough of its era. It revolutionized how ideas spread and reshaped preaching itself. As printed materials became central, preaching shifted from a more free-flowing, story-driven approach to a structured, information-heavy format—complete with organized outlines and clearly defined points. Worship services followed suit, emphasizing the spoken word, printed texts, and formal order. The typical worship posture became passive: sit, listen, observe. But that world is gone.

Today, print is no longer the dominant communication channel. We live in a digital-first culture—one that moves at the speed of a scroll, not the turn of a page. Attention is shaped by smartphones, social media, podcasts, streaming platforms, and constant connectivity. This is a culture measured in seconds, even milliseconds, not hours. And in the middle of that shift, the question for the church hasn’t changed, but the urgency has.

As Gibson (2004) puts it, “preachers have choices to make: engage the culture, ignore the culture, capitulate to the culture, or even challenge it.” That tension defines modern ministry.

Today’s preacher is not called to retreat from culture, nor to blindly mirror it, but to redeem what can be redeemed—leveraging the tools of the moment without losing the truth of the message. Paul said in 1 Corinthians 9:20–23,

To the Jews I became like a Jew, to win the Jews. To those under the law I became like one under the law (though I myself am not under the law), so as to win those under the law. To those not having the law I became like one not having the law (though I am not free from God’s law but am under Christ’s law), so as to win those not having the law . . . I have become all things to all men so that by all possible means I might save some. I do all this for the sake of the gospel, that I may share in its blessings.

The contemporary preacher must be filled with the same evangelistic passion as Paul and present the story in such a way that compels the audience to listen and respond. In order to communicate effectively, the preacher and message must morph once again and ride the shifting currents of cultural change.

In their book Growing Spiritual Redwoods , Bill Easum and Tom Bandy state, “If you say it all with words, you have missed the point” (Easum and Bandy, 1997). Visual imagery and the use of multimedia is the primary language of the day and is thus the most effective way to communicate to a visually oriented generation. High-tech worship is the “stained glass, and the cross, for the electronic media age . . . constantly transposing new imagery before us. Icons were the Bible for the illiterate, and the screen is the Bible for the post-literate” (Schultze 2004).

Following are three examples of how to implement effective high-tech worship elements.

Implementing High-Tech Worship

First, the preacher may use presentational technologies that allow the projection of still and moving images onto a large screen or multiple screens in the worship environment. These presentational technologies involve a computer, a video projector, and presentational software, such as PowerPoint, MediaShout, SongShow Plus, or one of many other applications on the market designed primarily for church use. The images can include but are not limited to song lyrics, pre- and post-service announcements, motion backgrounds, Scripture and sermon outlines, video clips, or even live video feeds projected on a screen. These visual images communicate truth and reinforce the spoken word throughout the service.

Second, a high-tech digital communication device includes digital signage and the church’s website. Although not typically used within the auditorium worship space, this is an effective high-tech tool available to the church today. Digital signage placed throughout the church or campus prepares the hearts and minds of worshipers prior to their entrance into the auditorium. In addition, high-tech churches use the church’s website to share vital congregational information through electronic newsletters; make sermon notes and small group curriculum available through digital downloads; create electronic forms for event registration; and develop spiritual growth resources, community blogs, sermon podcasts, online giving, first-time guest follow-up surveys, and other useful forms of connection. These media allow potential guests to get a look and feel of what your church and communication style is like before ever visiting a weekend service. The most effective churches attract return guests by ensuring that the website is updated regularly with fresh content.

A third and final high-tech worship element is more a technique than a tool. High-tech churches work very hard at helping listeners retain the information presented in the preaching event and worship experience through the use of metaphors . A metaphor is a communication device to help enhance the description of one object by comparing it with another object or to show a connection or association between two seemingly unrelated subjects. Communicators often find it very difficult to communicate abstract ideas, such as faith or trust , in concrete terms. The proper use and application of a metaphor simply takes the potentially abstract story or idea and updates it to a present-day tangible equivalent (Wilson and More 2002, 34).

The Bible is full of metaphors. The biblical writers use the metaphor of a parent to demonstrate God’s love (Deuteronomy 32:11; Matthew 6:9; 1 Corinthians 8:6). The body of Christ is one of Paul’s favorite metaphors of the Church (Romans 12:5; 1 Corinthians 12:12; Ephesians 3:6). And Jesus used metaphors in His own preaching when speaking about the kingdom of God. A famous example is His parable of the great banquet (Luke 14:15–24), which He uses to show His love for the marginalized.

Metaphors derived from contemporary culture are the most effective in that they constantly reinforce the communicator’s message when encountered by the listener in daily life. For example, computer operating systems might serve as an analogy for a series on the Ten Commandments. Computers need operating systems to function effectively, and all operating systems are built on a set of codes. These codes function as the foundation for the operating system. Likewise, God designed an operating system for our lives to help us function effectively. These ten simple codes known as the Ten Commandments, when applied to our lives, will make life work. This metaphor could be reinforced in the preaching event and worship experience in a variety of ways, including digital and print signage strategically placed outside the worship auditorium, the front cover of the weekly bulletin, sermon invitation cards for congregants to distribute to friends, connection cards, sermon note inserts, worship screens, and website communication graphics.

Describe potential pitfalls when using media elements to communicate the gospel.

Potential Pitfalls of Media Elements

One must be cautious of potential pitfalls when implementing digital media elements and metaphors in the worship service. Although this is not an exhaustive list, here are a few things to consider:

1. When implementing media elements, the preacher should always choose clarity over cuteness. In communication, nothing is more important than clarity.

2. Since less is more, the minister must be careful not to over-accessorize the message with too many worship elements. For instance, if using a video clip or drama to set up the sermon, the preacher must keep the length to a minimum.

3. Creative elements throughout the sermon series may be rotated. For example, in one sermon series, the preacher may use a sixty-second video roll-in to set the message up, while in the next sermon series, a drama, personal testimony, or song introduces the message (these methods must drive home the main point in the conclusion). When implementing creative elements, it is preferable to be consistently inconsistent.

4. Finally and more importantly, the preacher must not rely on creative elements or even a well-designed metaphor as a substitute for the sovereign work of the Holy Spirit. As pastors, we can only partner with the Holy Spirit to create engaging, relevant worship experiences that introduce people to the presence of God. But we are ultimately helpless unless the Holy Spirit enters the mix and convicts the sinner with compassion and grace, bringing him or her to repentance. Digital media and metaphors are merely tools, much like any other tool—pen and scroll, the pulpit, stained glass, or the printing press—used to communicate the gospel throughout history. Ministers must ultimately submit themselves and these tools to the working of the Holy Spirit.

**Q:** What are some of the most influential digital platforms that you can currently identify?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How can presentational technologies enhance contemporary worship?

**A:** The use of video projectors and screens can add the following creative worship elements: sermon outlines, video clips, pre- and post-service announcements, Scripture references, and song lyrics. Also, high-tech lighting and sound can communicate certain emotions and change the overall environment.

**Q:** List potential pitfalls of using media elements and metaphors in the worship service.

**A:** Too many metaphors or media elements can clutter up the message, thus reducing the overall effectiveness of communication. Also, there is the possibility that the preacher will give more time to the media elements than to studying the text and pulling out deep theological principles for everyday life.

### Cultivating Creativity in Communication

#### 8.3.1 — Discuss keys to developing creativity.

God is the author of creativity. The Bible begins with the statement, “In the beginning God created the heavens and the earth” (Genesis 1:1). Here God is first described as a creative being who brings forth life. To create means to bring into existence. Genesis describes God as the Creator of all things, who created ex nihilo , “out of nothing.” In other words, God created something out of nothing. He made the galaxies, universe, planet earth, all forms of life in the plant and animal world, out of an incredibly imaginative power. The crowning achievement of God’s creative genius was humanity, whom He made in His own image (Genesis 1:26).

Discuss keys to developing creativity.

If humanity is made in the imageo dei or “image of God,” then it stands to reason, according to the author of Genesis, that human beings are hardwired with the creative capacity of God. In the context of contemporary preaching, all communicators of the gospel, therefore, have God’s creative fingerprint on their lives and ministries. God has uniquely gifted His servants to function in creative capacities, to take that which has already been created and use it in new and imaginative ways. Every preacher is born with a creative component built into his or her genes by the hand of God.

Creativity, much like a muscle, needs to be exercised in order to operate at optimal levels of performance. The more one cultivates one’s God-given creative muscle, the stronger it will become. The converse is also true. The more one ignores it, the quicker it will atrophy.

Keys to Creativity

Following are a few keys designed to help the preacher cultivate a lifestyle of creativity in ministry. The minister who incorporates these principles into the ministry context will be amazed at the level of creativity he or she possesses.

1. First, be true to yourself; do not try to be someone else when you preach. Authenticity is the hallmark of any creative and effective preacher. God has uniquely gifted you with talents, skills, and abilities that He wants you to develop for His kingdom purposes. The sooner you understand that, the more fun you will have developing your preaching ministry. When you get to heaven and stand before God, He is not going to ask you, “Why weren’t you more like Moses or Joshua? Why didn’t you preach more like another pastor or leader?” He would be more interested in knowing why you were not more like yourself. It is okay to learn from others as long as you stay true to yourself. The key to developing your creativity is to grow more comfortable with who you are and begin to release your unique personality and gifts for God’s kingdom purposes in your sphere of influence. The more you are yourself, the better preacher you will be.

2. Second, learn to create in community. As we will see in a later chapter, creative teams are an effective and enjoyable way to develop innovative communication methodologies. When working with a creative team, you have the opportunity to address issues from a variety of angles and gain insight through the eyes and experiences of others. Creative teamwork brings fresh ideas to the table that rarely can be developed while working in isolation.

3. Third, study creative people. If you want to be more creative, you need to spend time with creative individuals. Buy their books, listen to their music, or attend their conferences. These are invaluable experiences that make you a more creative and effective communicator. If you cannot afford to travel and pay for a conference, then go online and learn in the digital world. You can also join coaching networks where you rub shoulders with other creative pastors who will feed your soul.

4. Fourth, take creative breaks. One of the most significant things you can do as a leader to develop your creativity is to take a day off or better yet, a month off. Over fifteen thousand pastors in America drop out of ministry each year. These high attrition rates are often a result of discouragement and lack of motivation, both of which stem from dangerously low levels of creativity. On the other hand, pastors who take regularly scheduled breaks tend to be more creative and passionate about ministry.

5. Last, creativity is a process. Note that the creation account covers a six-day time period. Clearly, God could have created the entire universe in an instant. Instead, He intentionally chose to bring the world into existence through a creative process. Creativity takes time to develop.

Asking the right questions will produce the right results. The preacher who wants to develop creativity and be effective must begin with the end in mind by asking the following key questions when crafting a sermon or weekend worship experience:

1. “What do I want people to know?” This question will help narrow the focus as it relates to content. After drilling deep into the biblical text and combing through the theological research, the minister must then summarize into one sentence the big idea of the message.

2. “What do I want people to do?” This is also known as the “so what” factor, and it positions the preacher to communicate how the information is intended to change the audience’s actions or attitudes.

**Q:** In what ways has God demonstrated creativity? What implications does that have on the modern preacher?

**A:** God created the heavens and earth and everything in it. He also created humanity in His image. Therefore, if God is creative, then we possess a God-given creative capacity as we are made in His image.

**Q:** List some ways you can be more creative in your preaching ministry.

**A:** Answers will vary.

## 9 - Communication that Connects

### Introduction to Narrative Preaching

#### 9.1.1 — Indicate how narrative preaching differs from propositional style preaching.

People are curious by nature. I believe that in every human heart resides the suspicion that there is more to this world than we can see and touch. The Bible supports this idea in that it states that God placed eternity in the hearts of all people (Ecclesiastes 3:11). In other words, there is something inside us all that was put there by God to lead us to believe there is more to life beyond the grave. Operating much like an eternal homing device or spiritual global positioning system, the God-given curiosity about this world and eternity is real.

Storytelling of the Scriptures has the ability to connect with this eternal curiosity at deep levels. Such narrative preaching goes far beyond fact and knowledge, reaching into the soul of individuals. In fact, that desire to know and answer the big questions of life, such as “Who am I?” “Where did I come from?” and “Where am I going after I die?” cannot be fully explained with mere facts. It takes more than a set of propositional statements to convince people in this culture that God loves them and holds the answers to the biggest questions.

Thankfully, God revealed himself to humanity primarily in the style of storytelling. God ultimately revealed himself through a man, Jesus Christ, and a book, the Bible. Both revelations go beyond facts and connect truth within the context of story. This story tells of God’s love for the lost and how He wants to redeem humanity. Think about this: Even before God gave Israel the law and principles to live by, He said, “I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of Egypt, out of the land of slavery” (Exodus 20:2). Immediately, God gave Israel a flashback in the form of a history lesson and reminded them through this snapshot of story that He first delivered them and accepted them into His family prior to giving them the facts of how to live life. Even something as seemingly dry as the writing of the law was framed and passed down to future generations through the compelling use of story. God knew that humans intuitively and innately understand stories in a manner different from the way they understand the propositions of logic (Wilson and More, 2002).

Indicate how narrative preaching differs from propositional style preaching.

The Essence of Narrative Preaching

The narrative preaching style uses the power of story to bind the entire sermon into a single plot or theme. It is much more than a collection of illustrations. Narrative preaching is properly defined as the ability to tell a story, supported by substories, that when woven together, form a cohesive theme.

Typically, this storytelling approach to preaching does not rely on the three-point propositional method of the deductive approach. Recall that the deductive approach focuses on a premise that is developed and supported by usually three claims or subpoints. The deductive style moves from a general truth to a particular application of the truth with the objective being to help listeners arrive at a logical conclusion, thus convincing them to change their minds about any given subject. In short, the desire of the propositional sermon is to change the mind of the listener by presenting facts.

Narrative preaching, on the other hand, employs an inductive or storytelling approach that invites the listener into a conversation. In an inductive sermon, the speaker begins with general life experiences (stories, events, occurrences) and moves the listener to a broader, biblical life principle at the conclusion.

Some might say there is an inherent danger in allowing the audience to arrive at their own conclusions, but today’s listeners prefer to draw their own conclusions through a thoughtful process rather than to have conclusions fed to them. Furthermore, this approach fits well with the essence of Pentecostal preaching, which relies on the work of the Holy Spirit to guide into all truth (John 16:13). Narrative preaching simply makes room for the Holy Spirit to bring listeners into the awareness of the truth. In other words, narrative preaching engages the audience through storytelling as it welcomes the listener into the conversation and draws conclusions based on the outcomes of the story.

Narrative preaching is a more intuitive approach, which focuses less on proving a point than on telling a story (Miller 2003). When it comes to narrative preaching, the story is the point and the point is the story. Calvin Miller explains the one-point approach with these words, “Here and there sub-plots, separate illustrations or precepts may punctuate or ornament the narrative, but the theme stays in force all the way through—from the “once-upon-a-time” until its “happily ever after” (Miller 2024, 94).

In essence, narrative preaching crafts a message within a story itself. Moreover, this form of preaching reflects the biblical text in that the Bible is largely a collection of stories. These stories, when woven together, form a spiritual tapestry that tells the one overarching story of God’s redemptive plan aimed at the restoration of relationship between the Creator and His creation.

Describe the historical roots of narrative preaching.

History of Narrative Preaching

Historically, we can trace narrative communication and preaching back to the Old and New Testaments. Many prominent biblical prophets and preachers relied a great deal on the narrative preaching style. Moses, the most prolific writer and preacher in the Old Testament, was trained in the finest schools Egypt had to offer and under the most educated Egyptian philosophers. Storytelling was the single most effective form of communication and education during those ancient days since the majority of people could not read or write. This is evident in the caves and tombs unearthed by archaeologists in Egypt that continue to tell the ancient stories written in hieroglyphics on the walls. These ancient ruins support the use of storytelling from early biblical days dating back to the great Exodus account.

Moses leveraged the education he received as he led and preached to the great nation of Israel following its deliverance from the Egyptian bondage. Old Testament storytelling, commonly known as oral tradition, enabled the first preachers of Israel to pass on the faith from generation to generation. John Holbert states,

The human narrators recognized that the story itself was their way to keep the relationship between God and themselves alive. But more than that, they recognized and affirmed that God, like themselves, found the clearest and most memorable expression of the eternal divine-human relationship in the telling and the hearing of stories. (Holbert 1991)

Moses’ legacy as a storyteller preacher played a key role in the ongoing lives of faith among the Hebrew people. Jesus’ preaching preference and the Gospels themselves are predominantly narrative. Almost one hundred years ago, the dean of homiletical theorists, H. Grady Davis, explained that while only one-tenth of the gospel is propositional, nine-tenths of it is narrative (Erickson and Heflin 1997).

Discuss postmodernists’ preference for narrative preaching.

Narrative Preaching for Postmodernists

A preaching paradigm shift is taking place once again among cutting-edge communicators. Many of today’s most successful communicators that are reaching the postmodern generation have developed a more narrative approach to preaching. Today’s effective communicators are challenging outmoded forms of communication with a fresh approach that is helping preaching make a comeback.

After twenty-five years of researching social change and the history of communication, Rex Miller designed the Millennium Matrix to help explain why storytelling and narrative preaching best connect with a contemporary audience. The Matrix is a compelling framework that enables us to view ourselves, our times, and the Church in a way that makes sense of the past, present, and future. Although we do not have the time or space to fully discuss the implications of Miller’s thoughts on culture, the Millennium Matrix helps us understand how our communication tools have changed over time: from spoken word to written word, broadcast word, and digital multimedia word. Miller contends that “with each change has come a new and different way of seeing the world” (Miller 2004, 15). This change in communication style radically altered how the Word of God was communicated in the church world and provides significant implications regarding why narrative preaching works best with today’s postmodern culture.

Millennium Matrix

Years

Communication Style

Preaching Method / Worship Style

4000 BC–AD 1500

1500–1950

1950–1999

2000 and beyond

Miller’s communication theory explains that from 4000 BC to about AD 1500—the preferred communication style was storytelling, also known as oral tradition. The church, in turn, practiced liturgical worship with storytelling as the primary preaching method. Gutenberg’s invention of the printing press and the mass production of books brought an end to the oral era and ushered in the print culture. Information was now widely available to the culture at large, which sped up the Enlightenment and facilitated Luther’s sixteenth-century Reformation.

From roughly 1500 to 1950, the church transitioned from a liturgical culture to a literature-based culture where preachers used logical argumentation as the primary method of communication. In turn, church services were orderly and structured with intentional design to emulate the unity of Christian thought through uniformity in worship (Easum and Brandy 1997). The entire church performed the same religious acts together, sang the same hymns together, read the same printed words together, and uniformly believed the same authoritative messages together.

The twentieth century brought yet another cascade of changes to communication style, and in turn, church worship services. With the invention of the broadcast radio and television technology, storytelling and the performing arts surfaced once again in culture and communication. It was about this time that narrative preaching began to pop up in pulpits again. The rediscovery of storytelling brought fresh new life to the church and expanded the horizons of cutting-edge preachers who were courageous enough to design more informal and celebratory church services in an attempt to reach today’s audience.

The invention of the computer and internet in the late twentieth century and the speed of technological advances already occurring in the twenty-first century have fueled further changes in communication. The shift from broadcast to digital technology creates new modes of communication in the digital world, including high-definition podcasts, blogs, and much more. Reflecting on this, Mark Miller comments, “This new communication mode is moving the church into a time of including and participation with the audience. Worship becomes collaboration, faith now becomes a journey, and the gospel is now communicating in multiple forms and media” (Miller 2003).

This new digital world creates a platform for the narrative preacher. Since information is now widely available, the primary concern is no longer the presentation of facts, figures, and logical argumentation. A clear example of this paradigm shift in communication is noticeable in the writing style and book titles of one of the twentieth century’s most prolific apologists, Josh McDowell. McDowell, incredibly gifted at reaching the postmodern generation, wrote a book entitled Evidence that Demands a Verdict . The premise of the book was that if you give teens the right facts about God, life, and adolescence (evidence), they will make wise choices based on your facts and supporting claims (verdict). In the late 1990s, McDowell realized that this communication style was no longer connecting with the postmodern generation. Something was missing. After studying contemporary culture, McDowell realized that the younger, postmodern mindset understands and accepts truth in connection with relationship. Therefore, his new book with Bob Hostetler, Beyond Belief to Convictions , which addresses many of the same issues as the former, became an instant bestseller because it communicated truth to the postmodern generation in the context of storytelling. Here, the focus was less on proving a theological point and more on telling a story. This is essentially why narrative preaching connects best to today’s postmodern culture.

**Q:** In your own words, explain the essence of narrative preaching.

**A:** Narrative preaching uses storytelling to communicate biblical principles. The overarching story of the sermon, known as the plot or theme, is often supported by substories.

**Q:** Why was storytelling the primary communication method in the ancient world?

**A:** Because very few people prior to the Enlightenment actually knew how to read or write, truth was communicated through the art of storytelling.

**Q:** Complete the following chart based on Rex Miller’s “Millennium Matrix,” which traces the history of communication and its implication for church worship practices:

**A:** Your chart should look something like this one:

In turn, church services were orderly and structured with intentional design to emulate the unity of Christian thought through uniformity in worship (Easum and Brandy 1997). The entire church performed the same religious acts together, sang the same hymns together, read the same printed words together, and uniformly believed the same authoritative messages together.

The twentieth century brought yet another cascade of changes to communication style, and in turn, church worship services. With the invention of the broadcast radio and television technology, storytelling and the performing arts surfaced once again in culture and communication. It was about this time that narrative preaching began to pop up in pulpits again. The rediscovery of storytelling brought fresh new life to the church and expanded the horizons of cutting-edge preachers who were courageous enough to design more informal and celebratory church services in an attempt to reach today’s audience.

The invention of the computer and internet in the late twentieth century and the speed of technological advances already occurring in the twenty-first century have fueled further changes in communication. The shift from broadcast to digital technology creates new modes of communication in the digital world, including high-definition podcasts, blogs, and much more. Reflecting on this, Mark Miller comments, “This new communication mode is moving the church into a time of including and participation with the audience. Worship becomes collaboration, faith now becomes a journey, and the gospel is now communicating in multiple forms and media” (Miller 2003).

This new digital world creates a platform for the narrative preacher. Since information is now widely available, the primary concern is no longer the presentation of facts, figures, and logical argumentation. A clear example of this paradigm shift in communication is noticeable in the writing style and book titles of one of the twentieth century’s most prolific apologists, Josh McDowell. McDowell, incredibly gifted at reaching the postmodern generation, wrote a book entitled Evidence that Demands a Verdict . The premise of the book was that if you give teens the right facts about God, life, and adolescence (evidence), they will make wise choices based on your facts and supporting claims (verdict). In the late 1990s, McDowell realized that this communication style was no longer connecting with the postmodern generation. Something was missing. After studying contemporary culture, McDowell realized that the younger, postmodern mindset understands and accepts truth in connection with relationship. Therefore, his new book with Bob Hostetler, Beyond Belief to Convictions , which addresses many of the same issues as the former, became an instant bestseller because it communicated truth to the postmodern generation in the context of storytelling. Here, the focus was less on proving a theological point and more on telling a story. This is essentially why narrative preaching connects best to today’s postmodern culture.

**Q:** In your own words, explain the essence of narrative preaching.

**A:** Narrative preaching uses storytelling to communicate biblical principles. The overarching story of the sermon, known as the plot or theme, is often supported by substories.

**Q:** Why was storytelling the primary communication method in the ancient world?

**A:** Because very few people prior to the Enlightenment actually knew how to read or write, truth was communicated through the art of storytelling.

### Form and Evaluation

#### 9.2.1 — Describe how to prepare a narrative sermon.

Preparing a Narrative Sermon

No two sermons are exactly the same. Multiple variables such as the personality of the speaker, his or her life experience, and even the attitudes and spiritual or cultural background of the audience contribute to the development and delivery of any particular message. However, most narrative messages contain three essential elements that when combined form a plot or theme which communicates biblical truth in a creative fashion. These essential elements are introduction, main point, and conclusion. All of these elements are vital to a well-developed message.

Describe how to prepare a narrative sermon.

Before looking at each of these elements, it is important to understand that a narrative sermon can be developed from a self-contained portion of biblical text, or from a variety of related biblical texts. Regardless of whether the minister uses a single unit of Scripture or pulls from various individual verses throughout both the Old and New Testament, what is most important is that one overarching theme is maintained throughout the message.

It is also possible and acceptable for a preacher to begin the development of the sermon with an idea or principle the Holy Spirit has laid on his heart. Then the minister can go to the Scripture to find supporting narratives and truth that reinforce this biblical lesson. When it comes to narrative preaching, and preaching in general for that matter, the point is that it connects with the audience and draws them into relationship with Christ.

After the preacher chooses a passage of Scripture, it is then necessary for him or her to analyze it and pull out the main theme. A good way to start this process of discovery is to read the passage through several times, in one sitting, and jot down some thoughts that come to mind. Next, begin to read the passage in context. Ask questions like:

“What comes before and after the Scripture or unit passage?”

“What is the context?”

“Who are the characters involved in the story?”

“What are the implications to those involved in the story?”

“What significance does this have for my life?”

As said earlier, the preacher needs to narrow the information down to one single theme and let it marinate until he or she can clearly understand it. After wrestling with this theme, he or she must be able to state it succinctly before it can be preached with passion and authority.

Once the preacher works through the text, he or she may read and study what others have said about the text. It is useful to consult commentaries and Christian books that deal with the topic. This research will help spark ideas while providing solid content for the message.

Another idea is to gain insight into the text by listening to how other preachers have preached the same material.

When the research is completed and the preacher has spent considerable time in prayer, he or she will want to consult the creative team. The role of this team, as we will see in the next chapter, is to help the minister develop the content, story, flow, transitions, and creative elements necessary for an innovative and effective presentation of the material.

Examine the three components of a narrative sermon.

Components of a Narrative Sermon

Below are three unique segments all narrative messages should include. It is best to think of these forms in terms of a sequence of events rather than a structure of components. The narrative sermon takes the listener on a journey, and each element below is simply a means of facilitating that journey. When preaching a narrative sermon series, as we will discuss in chapter 10, the minister may develop and reinforce a specific theme throughout a number of sermons delivered sequentially. Repetition is the key to learning. The preacher must work diligently and ask the Holy Spirit to help in communicating the central theme that is derived from Scripture or life experience.

Introduction

The introduction of the narrative sermon presents the subject or theme of the message to the listener. A good introduction technique used by many gifted storytelling preachers is to create tension for the listener. For instance, I recently heard a preacher begin his narrative sermon that dealt with the grip of greed and materialism in present-day culture. He had on the platform a table on which sat a basket filled with cantaloupes. As he began the message with a story about how he had a natural tendency to want to buy the newest and latest gadgets on the market, he stood next to the cantaloupes but never explained why they were there or even acknowledged their presence.

Obviously, this created tension in the mind of the listeners: Why are the cantaloupes on the table? What do they represent? At the end of the message, the speaker drew a comparison between his love for fresh fruit and his desire to spend money on material things rather than invest it in the kingdom of heaven. This consumer mentality was driven home as the preacher sliced the melon and began to eat it as he said, “I love cantaloupe, and I have been saving this all for myself. I am going to buy a new car. I am going to get a new boat. I am going to consume it all on me.” Intuitively, the audience understood that this is how God sees them when they are consumed by their desires for material things.

Again, the key to a good introduction is to create tension and interest in the mind of the listener. Most speakers I grew up listening to in church began with something like, “Good morning, if you have your Bibles, please turn with me to the Gospel of Luke, chapter 12.” That might be appealing to a committed Christian ready for some Bible knowledge, but what about the skeptic or doubter? The preacher needs to consider who is in the audience and craft the introduction so that as many different people as possible can be interested in the message. A useful method is to use illustrations and examples from everyday life that include perspectives of the young and old, rich and poor, saved and unsaved, and so on. This creates a wide funnel that enables the minister to generate entry points and engage the audience at a variety of levels. The audience not only possesses different demographics but also experiences a wide range of emotions. Some of them will be happy, others sad, some distracted or preoccupied with the busyness of life, and some self-righteous while others are overwhelmed with guilt. A good introduction that creates tension and disrupts equilibrium will stimulate interest in the subject and whet the appetite of the listener.

The preacher must be careful not to give too much away in the introduction. That would be like going into a movie theatre to see a movie you have already seen and announcing the ending to everyone sitting in the theatre. If you would not do that in a movie theatre, why do that in the message? Every good storyteller and playwright creates tension and leaves things hanging until resolution is found in the conclusion.

The Main Point

The second stage or sequence in the narrative sermon is the main point. This is the part of the message where the speaker transitions to the main lesson or theme. The minister could begin by interpreting the Bible story or passage, depending on where he or she is in the text. Then, the minister could discuss the events and characters that are involved in the narrative. By directly acknowledging genuine resistance that listeners might be experiencing, the preacher can draw the audience into the story. For example, if preaching on the issue of materialism, you might say something like, “I wish I could have it all for myself too. In fact, I’m just like you. I like to buy every new gadget that comes on the market. There is a consumer mentality inside of me that drives me to tighten my grip on material things. I wish God would turn around for just a few seconds, and I would rip this portion of Scripture out of the book.” Acknowledging the knee-jerk reactions most people have to biblical principles, such as greed, the preacher is able to dismantle fear and resentment and invite the listener to at least be open to the message.

The main point must be stated, restated, then stated again; it should be illustrated with personal, real-life stories. The preacher must be sincere and honest with the audience and even share personal struggles of how he or she applied the truth. It is extremely important during this part of the narrative to put the main theme in a principle form so it can be communicated with clarity. This is the takeaway. It answers the “so what” question. The hope is that someday the listener could come back to this passage or remember this story and say, “I know what that means. I know the point of that story.”

Transitions are critical to a good narrative sermon. Narrative sermons have a linear format and do not typically follow a Roman or Arabic numeral system. Even if the preacher does not write a transcript for the sermon, at least he or she needs to take the time to script the transitions. Transitions basically say, “We’ve been here. This is where we are now going. I don’t want to lose you.” They create flow to a story and bridge the substories as the overall plot and theme are developed. Using transitions helps avoid getting bogged down or lost in unnecessary discussions by keeping the story moving. It also is a good idea to use verbs instead of too many adjectives during the transitions. Adjectives may muddy the plot and slow down the sermon.

Conclusion

The last component to a well-organized narrative sermon is the conclusion. This is where the listener is challenged to take some sort of action. Here the tension created in the introduction finds resolution. By this time in the sermon, the story has been told, and the plot has been revealed. The audience reaches an “Aha” moment, and the stage is set for closure.

In the conclusion, the preacher must not give in to the temptation to solve every issue addressed in the theme. Creating an opportunity and understanding within listeners that a solution is now possible is enough. Although it is a good idea to share the consequences of the decisions that are on the table, the choice of how to respond remains with the listener. Recognize the freedom God has given us, and encourage the audience to embrace the positive outcomes of the story. In the end, we are called to present the story in a manner that encourages the listener to pay attention and respond positively. It is the role of the Holy Spirit to bring people to repentance, which has the power to transform their lives and create in them new beginnings.

Identify the strengths and weaknesses of a narrative sermon.

The Narrative Sermon’s Strength

1. The signal strength of a narrative sermon is its style of communication. When it comes to preaching, delivery is as important as content. Narrative sermons entice uninterested dull minds to engage the most creative and innovative story known to humanity. It utilizes humor, drama, joy, romance, and even death as key components in the story. The power of narrative preaching is that it brings the story to life in a much more entertaining and enlightening manner. Similar to a good movie that uses suspense, tension, and intrigue, the narrative sermon forces even the most disinterested and distracted listener to tune in.

2. Narrative preaching also speaks in a natural way to the story of both Scripture and contemporary culture. Alton H. McEachern wrote, “The great theology of the Bible is clothed in the flesh and blood of living characters” (McEachern 1984). Since the Bible is largely narrative, it follows that if one is going to preach the book composed of historical accounts experienced by real people, one needs to remember that it is a “story book” (Miller 2024, 93).

Storytelling connects well within our twenty-first century story-driven culture. Narrative preaching speaks the language of modern society and thus naturally finds higher degrees of cultural acceptance than other preaching styles. People who have grown accustomed to the half-hour sitcom that tells a single story with multiple subplots have been nurtured to embrace the narrative sermon.

3. A third strength of the narrative sermon is its conversational style. Conversational-style, narrative preaching is often more effective at engaging secular, non-believing people who have typically tuned out more traditional approaches to Bible exposition. This less “preachy” approach bodes well in a “don’t-preach-to-me” generation. Here we are talking about style, not content. It is often assumed that a storyteller is simply watering down the message to attract a crowd and “scratch their itchy ears” with feel-good sermons. “Spicing up a sermon with story is different than watering down the truth. It doesn’t mean you are less truthful. It simply means you are more engaging” (Batterson 2006).

Narrative preachers still talk about sin, hell, and holiness. Instead of broaching these subjects from a “thou shalt not” angle, narrative preachers help the audience find answers with a “Come now, let us reason together” approach (Isaiah 1:18). Research also indicates that information learned in a positive, inductive approach is retained for longer periods. Thus, narrative sermons that help listeners discover the main point of the text through story (inductive) will create longer memories of the message for its hearers.

The Narrative Sermon’s Weaknesses

The narrative sermon, however, is not without its weaknesses. Here are just a few cautions the preacher needs to be aware of in pursuing this style of communication in the pulpit.

1. Narrative sermons are simply harder to write and develop. It is much easier to preach verse by verse through books of the Bible and support the points through proof-texting. However, narrative preachers who preach one-point messages work much harder to develop a strand of thought through an entire message while staying on point. Developing a theme or principle through a four- or six-week narrative sermon series is even more difficult since that theme is tracked through a single passage or numerous stories in the biblical text. Narrative preaching is not for the faint of heart. It will challenge the preacher to be more creative and to rely even more heavily on the work and wisdom of the Holy Spirit.

2. Another apparent weakness in the narrative story is the subtle loss of the teaching event in the church. In addition to telling stories (see the parables of Christ, Stephen’s sermon in Acts 7, or Paul’s sermon before Agrippa), preaching has played a prominent teaching ( didache ) role since its introduction to the church (Miller 2024, 100). The sermon and preacher must teach the people. While stories have the ability to teach, they are not as direct and forthright as propositional statements. Paul mentored his protégé preacher, Timothy, and gave him these instructions in 2 Timothy:

Be a teacher of apostolic truth so that he may reproduce himself in faithful men (2:2).

Interpret and apply Scripture accurately (2:15).

Face dangerous times with a deep knowledge of the Word of God (3:1–15).

Understand that Scripture is the basis and content of all legitimate ministry (3:16–17).

Preach the Word—in season and out of season—reproving, rebuking, and exhorting with great patience and instruction (4:1–2).

Obviously, there is a high calling to uphold doctrine and orthodoxy in the church, regardless of the time and generation it exists within. The ministry of teaching principles of truth must take place from the pulpit. Admittedly, sermons that use precepts and principles are the most effective at communicating doctrinal issues within the church. “It is precisely at this point that nearly all fundamentalist and most evangelicals will not ascribe to too many narrative sermons in fear that the storytelling style will soften the church’s understanding of its role in the world” (Miller 2024, 93).

3. A final weakness of narrative preaching is that it may communicate that the sermon exists to entertain rather than transform. While I believe that preaching should be entertaining, which Webster describes as holding someone’s attention for a prolonged period, the goal of preaching is to help people become more like Christ. This transformative power always trumps any other form of communication.

**Q:** What are the three essential elements of a narrative sermon?

**A:** Introduction, main point, and conclusion

**Q:** What is the key to a good introduction?

**A:** To produce interest in the subject matter by creating tension for the audience. This tension engages the audience and draws them into the message.

**Q:** Discuss how the strengths and weaknesses of narrative preaching will affect you personally, as a preacher of the gospel.

**A:** Answers will vary.
