**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 9.2.3 — Identify the strengths and weaknesses of a narrative sermon.

Preparing a Narrative Sermon

No two sermons are exactly the same. Multiple variables such as the personality of the speaker, his or her life experience, and even the attitudes and spiritual or cultural background of the audience contribute to the development and delivery of any particular message. However, most narrative messages contain three essential elements that when combined form a plot or theme which communicates biblical truth in a creative fashion. These essential elements are introduction, main point, and conclusion. All of these elements are vital to a well-developed message.

Describe how to prepare a narrative sermon.

Before looking at each of these elements, it is important to understand that a narrative sermon can be developed from a self-contained portion of biblical text, or from a variety of related biblical texts. Regardless of whether the minister uses a single unit of Scripture or pulls from various individual verses throughout both the Old and New Testament, what is most important is that one overarching theme is maintained throughout the message.

It is also possible and acceptable for a preacher to begin the development of the sermon with an idea or principle the Holy Spirit has laid on his heart. Then the minister can go to the Scripture to find supporting narratives and truth that reinforce this biblical lesson. When it comes to narrative preaching, and preaching in general for that matter, the point is that it connects with the audience and draws them into relationship with Christ.

After the preacher chooses a passage of Scripture, it is then necessary for him or her to analyze it and pull out the main theme. A good way to start this process of discovery is to read the passage through several times, in one sitting, and jot down some thoughts that come to mind. Next, begin to read the passage in context. Ask questions like:

“What comes before and after the Scripture or unit passage?”

“What is the context?”

“Who are the characters involved in the story?”

“What are the implications to those involved in the story?”

“What significance does this have for my life?”

As said earlier, the preacher needs to narrow the information down to one single theme and let it marinate until he or she can clearly understand it. After wrestling with this theme, he or she must be able to state it succinctly before it can be preached with passion and authority.

Once the preacher works through the text, he or she may read and study what others have said about the text. It is useful to consult commentaries and Christian books that deal with the topic. This research will help spark ideas while providing solid content for the message.

Another idea is to gain insight into the text by listening to how other preachers have preached the same material.

When the research is completed and the preacher has spent considerable time in prayer, he or she will want to consult the creative team. The role of this team, as we will see in the next chapter, is to help the minister develop the content, story, flow, transitions, and creative elements necessary for an innovative and effective presentation of the material.

Examine the three components of a narrative sermon.

Components of a Narrative Sermon

Below are three unique segments all narrative messages should include. It is best to think of these forms in terms of a sequence of events rather than a structure of components. The narrative sermon takes the listener on a journey, and each element below is simply a means of facilitating that journey. When preaching a narrative sermon series, as we will discuss in chapter 10, the minister may develop and reinforce a specific theme throughout a number of sermons delivered sequentially. Repetition is the key to learning. The preacher must work diligently and ask the Holy Spirit to help in communicating the central theme that is derived from Scripture or life experience.

Introduction

The introduction of the narrative sermon presents the subject or theme of the message to the listener. A good introduction technique used by many gifted storytelling preachers is to create tension for the listener. For instance, I recently heard a preacher begin his narrative sermon that dealt with the grip of greed and materialism in present-day culture. He had on the platform a table on which sat a basket filled with cantaloupes. As he began the message with a story about how he had a natural tendency to want to buy the newest and latest gadgets on the market, he stood next to the cantaloupes but never explained why they were there or even acknowledged their presence.

Obviously, this created tension in the mind of the listeners: Why are the cantaloupes on the table? What do they represent? At the end of the message, the speaker drew a comparison between his love for fresh fruit and his desire to spend money on material things rather than invest it in the kingdom of heaven. This consumer mentality was driven home as the preacher sliced the melon and began to eat it as he said, “I love cantaloupe, and I have been saving this all for myself. I am going to buy a new car. I am going to get a new boat. I am going to consume it all on me.” Intuitively, the audience understood that this is how God sees them when they are consumed by their desires for material things.

Again, the key to a good introduction is to create tension and interest in the mind of the listener. Most speakers I grew up listening to in church began with something like, “Good morning, if you have your Bibles, please turn with me to the Gospel of Luke, chapter 12.” That might be appealing to a committed Christian ready for some Bible knowledge, but what about the skeptic or doubter? The preacher needs to consider who is in the audience and craft the introduction so that as many different people as possible can be interested in the message. A useful method is to use illustrations and examples from everyday life that include perspectives of the young and old, rich and poor, saved and unsaved, and so on. This creates a wide funnel that enables the minister to generate entry points and engage the audience at a variety of levels. The audience not only possesses different demographics but also experiences a wide range of emotions. Some of them will be happy, others sad, some distracted or preoccupied with the busyness of life, and some self-righteous while others are overwhelmed with guilt. A good introduction that creates tension and disrupts equilibrium will stimulate interest in the subject and whet the appetite of the listener.

The preacher must be careful not to give too much away in the introduction. That would be like going into a movie theatre to see a movie you have already seen and announcing the ending to everyone sitting in the theatre. If you would not do that in a movie theatre, why do that in the message? Every good storyteller and playwright creates tension and leaves things hanging until resolution is found in the conclusion.

The Main Point

The second stage or sequence in the narrative sermon is the main point. This is the part of the message where the speaker transitions to the main lesson or theme. The minister could begin by interpreting the Bible story or passage, depending on where he or she is in the text. Then, the minister could discuss the events and characters that are involved in the narrative. By directly acknowledging genuine resistance that listeners might be experiencing, the preacher can draw the audience into the story. For example, if preaching on the issue of materialism, you might say something like, “I wish I could have it all for myself too. In fact, I’m just like you. I like to buy every new gadget that comes on the market. There is a consumer mentality inside of me that drives me to tighten my grip on material things. I wish God would turn around for just a few seconds, and I would rip this portion of Scripture out of the book.” Acknowledging the knee-jerk reactions most people have to biblical principles, such as greed, the preacher is able to dismantle fear and resentment and invite the listener to at least be open to the message.

The main point must be stated, restated, then stated again; it should be illustrated with personal, real-life stories. The preacher must be sincere and honest with the audience and even share personal struggles of how he or she applied the truth. It is extremely important during this part of the narrative to put the main theme in a principle form so it can be communicated with clarity. This is the takeaway. It answers the “so what” question. The hope is that someday the listener could come back to this passage or remember this story and say, “I know what that means. I know the point of that story.”

Transitions are critical to a good narrative sermon. Narrative sermons have a linear format and do not typically follow a Roman or Arabic numeral system. Even if the preacher does not write a transcript for the sermon, at least he or she needs to take the time to script the transitions. Transitions basically say, “We’ve been here. This is where we are now going. I don’t want to lose you.” They create flow to a story and bridge the substories as the overall plot and theme are developed. Using transitions helps avoid getting bogged down or lost in unnecessary discussions by keeping the story moving. It also is a good idea to use verbs instead of too many adjectives during the transitions. Adjectives may muddy the plot and slow down the sermon.

Conclusion

The last component to a well-organized narrative sermon is the conclusion. This is where the listener is challenged to take some sort of action. Here the tension created in the introduction finds resolution. By this time in the sermon, the story has been told, and the plot has been revealed. The audience reaches an “Aha” moment, and the stage is set for closure.

In the conclusion, the preacher must not give in to the temptation to solve every issue addressed in the theme. Creating an opportunity and understanding within listeners that a solution is now possible is enough. Although it is a good idea to share the consequences of the decisions that are on the table, the choice of how to respond remains with the listener. Recognize the freedom God has given us, and encourage the audience to embrace the positive outcomes of the story. In the end, we are called to present the story in a manner that encourages the listener to pay attention and respond positively. It is the role of the Holy Spirit to bring people to repentance, which has the power to transform their lives and create in them new beginnings.

Identify the strengths and weaknesses of a narrative sermon.

The Narrative Sermon’s Strength

1. The signal strength of a narrative sermon is its style of communication. When it comes to preaching, delivery is as important as content. Narrative sermons entice uninterested dull minds to engage the most creative and innovative story known to humanity. It utilizes humor, drama, joy, romance, and even death as key components in the story. The power of narrative preaching is that it brings the story to life in a much more entertaining and enlightening manner. Similar to a good movie that uses suspense, tension, and intrigue, the narrative sermon forces even the most disinterested and distracted listener to tune in.

2. Narrative preaching also speaks in a natural way to the story of both Scripture and contemporary culture. Alton H. McEachern wrote, “The great theology of the Bible is clothed in the flesh and blood of living characters” (McEachern 1984). Since the Bible is largely narrative, it follows that if one is going to preach the book composed of historical accounts experienced by real people, one needs to remember that it is a “story book” (Miller 2024, 93).

Storytelling connects well within our twenty-first century story-driven culture. Narrative preaching speaks the language of modern society and thus naturally finds higher degrees of cultural acceptance than other preaching styles. People who have grown accustomed to the half-hour sitcom that tells a single story with multiple subplots have been nurtured to embrace the narrative sermon.

3. A third strength of the narrative sermon is its conversational style. Conversational-style, narrative preaching is often more effective at engaging secular, non-believing people who have typically tuned out more traditional approaches to Bible exposition. This less “preachy” approach bodes well in a “don’t-preach-to-me” generation. Here we are talking about style, not content. It is often assumed that a storyteller is simply watering down the message to attract a crowd and “scratch their itchy ears” with feel-good sermons. “Spicing up a sermon with story is different than watering down the truth. It doesn’t mean you are less truthful. It simply means you are more engaging” (Batterson 2006).

Narrative preachers still talk about sin, hell, and holiness. Instead of broaching these subjects from a “thou shalt not” angle, narrative preachers help the audience find answers with a “Come now, let us reason together” approach (Isaiah 1:18). Research also indicates that information learned in a positive, inductive approach is retained for longer periods. Thus, narrative sermons that help listeners discover the main point of the text through story (inductive) will create longer memories of the message for its hearers.

The Narrative Sermon’s Weaknesses

The narrative sermon, however, is not without its weaknesses. Here are just a few cautions the preacher needs to be aware of in pursuing this style of communication in the pulpit.

1. Narrative sermons are simply harder to write and develop. It is much easier to preach verse by verse through books of the Bible and support the points through proof-texting. However, narrative preachers who preach one-point messages work much harder to develop a strand of thought through an entire message while staying on point. Developing a theme or principle through a four- or six-week narrative sermon series is even more difficult since that theme is tracked through a single passage or numerous stories in the biblical text. Narrative preaching is not for the faint of heart. It will challenge the preacher to be more creative and to rely even more heavily on the work and wisdom of the Holy Spirit.

2. Another apparent weakness in the narrative story is the subtle loss of the teaching event in the church. In addition to telling stories (see the parables of Christ, Stephen’s sermon in Acts 7, or Paul’s sermon before Agrippa), preaching has played a prominent teaching ( didache ) role since its introduction to the church (Miller 2024, 100). The sermon and preacher must teach the people. While stories have the ability to teach, they are not as direct and forthright as propositional statements. Paul mentored his protégé preacher, Timothy, and gave him these instructions in 2 Timothy:

Be a teacher of apostolic truth so that he may reproduce himself in faithful men (2:2).

Interpret and apply Scripture accurately (2:15).

Face dangerous times with a deep knowledge of the Word of God (3:1–15).

Understand that Scripture is the basis and content of all legitimate ministry (3:16–17).

Preach the Word—in season and out of season—reproving, rebuking, and exhorting with great patience and instruction (4:1–2).

Obviously, there is a high calling to uphold doctrine and orthodoxy in the church, regardless of the time and generation it exists within. The ministry of teaching principles of truth must take place from the pulpit. Admittedly, sermons that use precepts and principles are the most effective at communicating doctrinal issues within the church. “It is precisely at this point that nearly all fundamentalist and most evangelicals will not ascribe to too many narrative sermons in fear that the storytelling style will soften the church’s understanding of its role in the world” (Miller 2024, 93).

3. A final weakness of narrative preaching is that it may communicate that the sermon exists to entertain rather than transform. While I believe that preaching should be entertaining, which Webster describes as holding someone’s attention for a prolonged period, the goal of preaching is to help people become more like Christ. This transformative power always trumps any other form of communication.

**Q:** What are the three essential elements of a narrative sermon?

**A:** Introduction, main point, and conclusion

**Q:** What is the key to a good introduction?

**A:** To produce interest in the subject matter by creating tension for the audience. This tension engages the audience and draws them into the message.

**Q:** Discuss how the strengths and weaknesses of narrative preaching will affect you personally, as a preacher of the gospel.

**A:** Answers will vary.

## 10 - Developing a Preaching Plan and Team

### Developing the Preaching Plan

#### 10.1.1 — Discuss the rationale for a preaching plan.

Organizing an Annual Preaching Calendar

A preaching calendar is simply a guide to help the minister develop and plan a preaching schedule. This yearly plan forces the preacher to chart a course for communicating the topics he or she would like to cover. If the goal of preaching is to see lives transformed by the power of God’s Word, then the preacher will need to plan well in advance and decide, early on, what he or she would like to address for those first-time guests and regular attendees in the weekend services.

Discuss the rationale for a preaching plan.

Developing an Annual Preaching Calendar

There are a few essential ingredients to building an effective preaching calendar, and it begins with intentional planning. Preaching is not something to approach week to week without direction—it requires prayerful foresight and disciplined preparation. At times, the work of crafting a message can feel weighty, especially when navigating difficult texts or seasons, but it is ultimately a privilege to stand and steward God’s Word. The greatest joy comes in watching how God moves through those moments—seeing lives changed, faith strengthened, and people drawn closer to Christ, often in ways far beyond what was prepared.

A foundational element of any preaching plan is simply recognizing the rhythm of the calendar. As we will explore further, understanding the unique rhythms, needs, and context of your community helps maximize the impact of each of those opportunities.

Another key component of a well-developed preaching calendar is the intentional use of sermon titles and series themes. Clear, compelling titles help frame the message and invite engagement, while well-planned series provide continuity and depth over time. When done effectively, sermon series become a powerful tool for shaping spiritual growth and ensuring a balanced, holistic approach to preaching throughout the year.

List the advantages of developing a preaching calendar.

Advantages of a Preaching Calendar

There are many advantages of developing a preaching calendar. The following list, although not exhaustive, will demonstrate the strengths and added value that a preaching calendar brings to both the preacher individually and the church body as a whole.

Developing a yearly preaching calendar can reduce the minister’s stress levels significantly. When preachers know what they are going to be preaching several months ahead of schedule, they can begin compiling content, illustrations, and stories; reading books on the topic; and preparing well ahead of time for the sermon or sermon series. This will increase their confidence as they step into the pulpit. Confidence in the pulpit makes better preachers.

A good friend of mine has a rule that everything for the weekend service, including the sermon and all other creative elements, must be completely prepared by Thursday at midnight. This “Thursday at midnight” rule forces the entire weekend team to work ahead of schedule. It also allows the speaker to let the sermon marinate for a few days before he or she actually preaches it. This slow-cook approach ensures enough time for the speaker to internalize the message, so when the sermon is delivered, it is almost as if the preacher is giving away part of himself or herself. Audiences can perceive this passion and thus engage the communicator intently. This provides a huge payoff for the preacher in that it makes the job more fun.

1. An advantage to developing a preaching calendar is that it helps avoid repetition while ensuring balance to sermon topics. A lot of preachers tend to repeat themselves. They might have a new title, but eventually all their sermons sound the same. If he or she is not careful, the minister will fall back on familiar and comfortable subjects, which can become exhausted over time. The Bible is full of stories and lessons that cover a broad range of truths and topics. By developing a preaching calendar, the minister will be able to cultivate a well-balanced preaching plan that will allow him or her to gain variety in his or her sermons.

2. Another advantage to preaching calendars is that the minister is able to bring the entire church ministry team into alignment. The pulpit is much like a rudder on a ship. It provides direction and guidance for the entire church body and facilitates a cooperative team effort among the various ministry teams.

When the leadership team knows the sermon topics well in advance, they can plan to integrate into the messages what is happening in other ministry areas. Thus, the church works together and uses its focused energy on building the kingdom. For example, the week that the speaker preaches on getting involved in ministry, the ministry leaders might plan a ministry fair, which will introduce all the different ministry teams to the congregation at large. That weekend the plan would be to mobilize people into ministry by preaching on the importance of using the gifts God has given each believer to build the Church.

3. A final benefit to developing a preaching calendar is that it builds anticipation among the congregation. When the preacher announces preaching topics a couple of weeks in advance, he or she creates the opportunity to establish interest among those in the congregation. The best way to maximize this interest is to preach series that deal with aspects of the organizing theme for typically two to six weeks. After six weeks, sometimes themes or series can lose energy or focus.

The preaching calendar not only creates interest among listeners, but it can also inspire those within the church to invite their friends and loved ones to come and hear a particular message.

Describe two steps to developing a preaching calendar.

Developing a Preaching Calendar

Planning a yearly preaching calendar is not as hard as it might sound. Following these simple steps will help the minister organize a year of exciting and creative sermons (see Appendix A: Preaching Calendar).

1. The first step to planning a preaching calendar is to organize calendar dates. After listing all the dates in one column, insert some key dates on the calendar, such as holidays, the days you intend to be away on vacation, and other special community events you need to be aware of. This is an incredibly important step. Be sure to capitalize strategically on events that may be happening in your ministry context.

When organizing the preaching calendar, the preacher must ask some key questions, such as, “What time of the year does my ministry best attract unchurched visitors?” This information will help the minister maximize those natural times in the community and design sermons and sermon series that will better attract newcomers. For example, Easter is traditionally a good time to kick off a new sermon series because it is one day of the year that unchurched people are more likely to attend a church service. Plan the preaching calendar to seize those prime opportunities for reaching new people for Christ.

Another key question to ask is, “When does my ministry have the most steady attendance record?” This information will help the minister plan key sermon series where each message builds on the previous message. The goal here is to maintain the momentum during this crucial time.

A final question to ask when planning a preaching series is, “When does attendance fluctuate the most?” Most churches traditionally experience the most fluctuation during the summer, when many families take vacations. However, each church or ministry has its own set of variables. The key is for the preacher to know when the church naturally experiences the ebbs and flows of attendance and then plan the preaching calendar accordingly.

2. The second step to planning a preaching calendar is to design a sermon series at strategic times throughout the year. A sermon series is a number of related messages that communicate one theme over the course of several weeks. However, sermon series can be as short as two weeks and as long at ten or twelve weeks. Most pastors agree that six weeks is generally an average time frame for a good series. It is difficult to hold the congregation’s attention in a sermon series that lasts longer than six weeks. In essence, the preaching calendar becomes a series with standalone messages scattered throughout the year. Sermon series help pique the interest of the listener and stabilize attendance as people are encouraged to return each week.

Types of Sermon Series

There is a variety of sermon series to choose from. Although there are many ways to design a sermon series, here are five common types with some examples in parentheses: topical (fruit of the Spirit, faith), book studies (Philippians or James), passage studies (Ten Commandments), theme series (core values), and biographical character studies (life of David). It is helpful to choose a variety of these series throughout the year. The change adds spice and flavor to topics and challenges the preacher’s creativity.

In addition to styles of sermon series, there are strategic kinds of series that are aimed at accomplishing specific goals. For instance, need-based series do well at attracting unchurched visitors. These series are based on focused audience analysis. The perceived needs of the listeners (and potential listeners who are unchurched) determine the organizing theme of the series. A series on living according to biblical priorities might be well received by the young family demographic—couples who are struggling to balance work, marriage, and raising children.

A second kind of series emphasizes personal, spiritual growth. The focus of this type of sermon series is to move believers into spiritual maturity. The themes revolve around discipleship and spiritual practices. Installments may include something like prayer, morality, intimacy, fruit of the Spirit, etc.

Another possible series theme might be the exploration of contemporary issues. This series would address specific phenomena within contemporary culture. In view here are topics such as abortion, sexuality, marriage, war, terrorism, and drug use. It is important to analyze congregational interest well when planning series that connect with issues that are relevant to your listeners.

3. The final and most important step to planning the preaching calendar is to seek God and His Holy Spirit in prayer. Some people believe the Holy Spirit cannot help the minister prepare messages in advance, as if to insinuate that the Holy Spirit only works in the immediacy of the moment. The fact is that the Holy Spirit can help the preacher prepare a month or year in advance. God is sovereign and providential; He knows who is going to be visiting your church or ministry each weekend. The minister must seek God’s wisdom and guidance in advance, and He will help direct the sermon preparation.

Many pastors find it helpful to take annual retreats to seek the Lord regarding the annual preaching calendar. Pastors can fast and prayerfully ask God where He wants to take the church in the months ahead. One of the things God wants to do in every church is grow it into spiritual maturity and create a climate of righteousness and holiness.

It is also a good idea to preach regularly on the core values of your church. This involves things like evangelism, worship, discipleship, ministry, and fellowship, which are important to the life and health of the church. Ask the Holy Spirit to give creative ways to address these important matters of faith and church health.

Planning a preaching calendar and the art of preaching are prayer endeavors. The minister must pray strategically over the preaching calendar, and God will order his or her steps and honor the faithful and continued dependence upon Him. In fact, a monthly or biweekly prayer retreat (for a full day or two) is invaluable for staying on course with God in one’s preaching.

Pitfalls of Planning a Preaching Calendar

Identify three potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

Following are several potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

Procrastination

The old adage rings true: Do not put off for tomorrow what you can do today. Be careful not to wait until the last minute to organize and plan the preaching calendar. The minister’s primary responsibility is to lead and feed the church family. It is okay not to have every message titled or know every date to begin a series. The goal is to fill in the “big chunks” in the calendar a year ahead of time. Then, six to eight weeks out, the preacher puts the titles down on paper and begins outline preparation. It is critically important to make every effort to get out ahead of the growth of the church and give oneself and the team plenty of lead time to develop creative elements of the preaching event.

Flying Solo

Pastors should not plan preaching alone. They must learn how to seek and hear God’s voice in the context of community. Therefore, ministers may gather leaders and key staff and seek God’s discernment together. As we will see in the next lesson, creative preaching teams are more effective and fun.

Changing the Plan

Life changes, as we know. However, after the prayer and planning process, the preacher must trust that God is leading and stick to the game plan. Resisting the urge to change midstream, he or she should move forward in faith.

**Q:** How would developing an annual preaching calendar impact your preaching ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** State in your own words the advantages of developing a preaching calendar.

**A:** Answers will vary. (1) Makes preaching easier and more fun; (2) helps you avoid repetition; (3) engages other ministry teams and releases team planning for church growth; and (4) creates anticipation among the congregation to want to hear what God has to say on the subjects announced.

**Q:** What role does the Holy Spirit play in sermon preparation?

**A:** The Holy Spirit guides and directs the preacher’s plans as he or she prepares sermons in advance. The Holy Spirit and anointing are just as powerful in the preacher’s preparation as in the preacher’s time in the pulpit.

**Q:** List the three potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

**A:** Procrastination, flying solo, and changing plans

For example, Easter is traditionally a good time to kick off a new sermon series because it is one day of the year that unchurched people are more likely to attend a church service. Plan the preaching calendar to seize those prime opportunities for reaching new people for Christ.

Another key question to ask is, “When does my ministry have the most steady attendance record?” This information will help the minister plan key sermon series where each message builds on the previous message. The goal here is to maintain the momentum during this crucial time.

A final question to ask when planning a preaching series is, “When does attendance fluctuate the most?” Most churches traditionally experience the most fluctuation during the summer, when many families take vacations. However, each church or ministry has its own set of variables. The key is for the preacher to know when the church naturally experiences the ebbs and flows of attendance and then plan the preaching calendar accordingly.

2. The second step to planning a preaching calendar is to design a sermon series at strategic times throughout the year. A sermon series is a number of related messages that communicate one theme over the course of several weeks. However, sermon series can be as short as two weeks and as long at ten or twelve weeks. Most pastors agree that six weeks is generally an average time frame for a good series. It is difficult to hold the congregation’s attention in a sermon series that lasts longer than six weeks. In essence, the preaching calendar becomes a series with standalone messages scattered throughout the year. Sermon series help pique the interest of the listener and stabilize attendance as people are encouraged to return each week.

Types of Sermon Series

There is a variety of sermon series to choose from. Although there are many ways to design a sermon series, here are five common types with some examples in parentheses: topical (fruit of the Spirit, faith), book studies (Philippians or James), passage studies (Ten Commandments), theme series (core values), and biographical character studies (life of David). It is helpful to choose a variety of these series throughout the year. The change adds spice and flavor to topics and challenges the preacher’s creativity.

In addition to styles of sermon series, there are strategic kinds of series that are aimed at accomplishing specific goals. For instance, need-based series do well at attracting unchurched visitors. These series are based on focused audience analysis. The perceived needs of the listeners (and potential listeners who are unchurched) determine the organizing theme of the series. A series on living according to biblical priorities might be well received by the young family demographic—couples who are struggling to balance work, marriage, and raising children.

A second kind of series emphasizes personal, spiritual growth. The focus of this type of sermon series is to move believers into spiritual maturity. The themes revolve around discipleship and spiritual practices. Installments may include something like prayer, morality, intimacy, fruit of the Spirit, etc.

Another possible series theme might be the exploration of contemporary issues. This series would address specific phenomena within contemporary culture. In view here are topics such as abortion, sexuality, marriage, war, terrorism, and drug use. It is important to analyze congregational interest well when planning series that connect with issues that are relevant to your listeners.

3. The final and most important step to planning the preaching calendar is to seek God and His Holy Spirit in prayer. Some people believe the Holy Spirit cannot help the minister prepare messages in advance, as if to insinuate that the Holy Spirit only works in the immediacy of the moment. The fact is that the Holy Spirit can help the preacher prepare a month or year in advance. God is sovereign and providential; He knows who is going to be visiting your church or ministry each weekend. The minister must seek God’s wisdom and guidance in advance, and He will help direct the sermon preparation.

Many pastors find it helpful to take annual retreats to seek the Lord regarding the annual preaching calendar. Pastors can fast and prayerfully ask God where He wants to take the church in the months ahead. One of the things God wants to do in every church is grow it into spiritual maturity and create a climate of righteousness and holiness.

It is also a good idea to preach regularly on the core values of your church. This involves things like evangelism, worship, discipleship, ministry, and fellowship, which are important to the life and health of the church. Ask the Holy Spirit to give creative ways to address these important matters of faith and church health.

Planning a preaching calendar and the art of preaching are prayer endeavors. The minister must pray strategically over the preaching calendar, and God will order his or her steps and honor the faithful and continued dependence upon Him. In fact, a monthly or biweekly prayer retreat (for a full day or two) is invaluable for staying on course with God in one’s preaching.

Pitfalls of Planning a Preaching Calendar

Identify three potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

Following are several potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

Procrastination

The old adage rings true: Do not put off for tomorrow what you can do today. Be careful not to wait until the last minute to organize and plan the preaching calendar. The minister’s primary responsibility is to lead and feed the church family. It is okay not to have every message titled or know every date to begin a series. The goal is to fill in the “big chunks” in the calendar a year ahead of time. Then, six to eight weeks out, the preacher puts the titles down on paper and begins outline preparation. It is critically important to make every effort to get out ahead of the growth of the church and give oneself and the team plenty of lead time to develop creative elements of the preaching event.

Flying Solo

Pastors should not plan preaching alone. They must learn how to seek and hear God’s voice in the context of community. Therefore, ministers may gather leaders and key staff and seek God’s discernment together. As we will see in the next lesson, creative preaching teams are more effective and fun.

Changing the Plan

Life changes, as we know. However, after the prayer and planning process, the preacher must trust that God is leading and stick to the game plan. Resisting the urge to change midstream, he or she should move forward in faith.

**Q:** How would developing an annual preaching calendar impact your preaching ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** State in your own words the advantages of developing a preaching calendar.

**A:** Answers will vary. (1) Makes preaching easier and more fun; (2) helps you avoid repetition; (3) engages other ministry teams and releases team planning for church growth; and (4) creates anticipation among the congregation to want to hear what God has to say on the subjects announced.

**Q:** What role does the Holy Spirit play in sermon preparation?

**A:** The Holy Spirit guides and directs the preacher’s plans as he or she prepares sermons in advance. The Holy Spirit and anointing are just as powerful in the preacher’s preparation as in the preacher’s time in the pulpit.

**Q:** List the three potential pitfalls to avoid when planning a preaching calendar.

**A:** Procrastination, flying solo, and changing plans

#### 10.1.3 — Describe two steps to developing a preaching calendar.

Organizing an Annual Preaching Calendar

A preaching calendar is simply a guide to help the minister develop and plan a preaching schedule. This yearly plan forces the preacher to chart a course for communicating the topics he or she would like to cover. If the goal of preaching is to see lives transformed by the power of God’s Word, then the preacher will need to plan well in advance and decide, early on, what he or she would like to address for those first-time guests and regular attendees in the weekend services.

Discuss the rationale for a preaching plan.

Developing an Annual Preaching Calendar

There are a few essential ingredients to building an effective preaching calendar, and it begins with intentional planning. Preaching is not something to approach week to week without direction—it requires prayerful foresight and disciplined preparation. At times, the work of crafting a message can feel weighty, especially when navigating difficult texts or seasons, but it is ultimately a privilege to stand and steward God’s Word. The greatest joy comes in watching how God moves through those moments—seeing lives changed, faith strengthened, and people drawn closer to Christ, often in ways far beyond what was prepared.

A foundational element of any preaching plan is simply recognizing the rhythm of the calendar. As we will explore further, understanding the unique rhythms, needs, and context of your community helps maximize the impact of each of those opportunities.

Another key component of a well-developed preaching calendar is the intentional use of sermon titles and series themes. Clear, compelling titles help frame the message and invite engagement, while well-planned series provide continuity and depth over time. When done effectively, sermon series become a powerful tool for shaping spiritual growth and ensuring a balanced, holistic approach to preaching throughout the year.

List the advantages of developing a preaching calendar.

Advantages of a Preaching Calendar

There are many advantages of developing a preaching calendar. The following list, although not exhaustive, will demonstrate the strengths and added value that a preaching calendar brings to both the preacher individually and the church body as a whole.

Developing a yearly preaching calendar can reduce the minister’s stress levels significantly. When preachers know what they are going to be preaching several months ahead of schedule, they can begin compiling content, illustrations, and stories; reading books on the topic; and preparing well ahead of time for the sermon or sermon series. This will increase their confidence as they step into the pulpit. Confidence in the pulpit makes better preachers.

A good friend of mine has a rule that everything for the weekend service, including the sermon and all other creative elements, must be completely prepared by Thursday at midnight. This “Thursday at midnight” rule forces the entire weekend team to work ahead of schedule. It also allows the speaker to let the sermon marinate for a few days before he or she actually preaches it. This slow-cook approach ensures enough time for the speaker to internalize the message, so when the sermon is delivered, it is almost as if the preacher is giving away part of himself or herself. Audiences can perceive this passion and thus engage the communicator intently. This provides a huge payoff for the preacher in that it makes the job more fun.

1. An advantage to developing a preaching calendar is that it helps avoid repetition while ensuring balance to sermon topics. A lot of preachers tend to repeat themselves. They might have a new title, but eventually all their sermons sound the same. If he or she is not careful, the minister will fall back on familiar and comfortable subjects, which can become exhausted over time. The Bible is full of stories and lessons that cover a broad range of truths and topics. By developing a preaching calendar, the minister will be able to cultivate a well-balanced preaching plan that will allow him or her to gain variety in his or her sermons.

2. Another advantage to preaching calendars is that the minister is able to bring the entire church ministry team into alignment. The pulpit is much like a rudder on a ship. It provides direction and guidance for the entire church body and facilitates a cooperative team effort among the various ministry teams.

When the leadership team knows the sermon topics well in advance, they can plan to integrate into the messages what is happening in other ministry areas. Thus, the church works together and uses its focused energy on building the kingdom. For example, the week that the speaker preaches on getting involved in ministry, the ministry leaders might plan a ministry fair, which will introduce all the different ministry teams to the congregation at large. That weekend the plan would be to mobilize people into ministry by preaching on the importance of using the gifts God has given each believer to build the Church.

3. A final benefit to developing a preaching calendar is that it builds anticipation among the congregation. When the preacher announces preaching topics a couple of weeks in advance, he or she creates the opportunity to establish interest among those in the congregation. The best way to maximize this interest is to preach series that deal with aspects of the organizing theme for typically two to six weeks. After six weeks, sometimes themes or series can lose energy or focus.

The preaching calendar not only creates interest among listeners, but it can also inspire those within the church to invite their friends and loved ones to come and hear a particular message.

Describe two steps to developing a preaching calendar.

Developing a Preaching Calendar

Planning a yearly preaching calendar is not as hard as it might sound. Following these simple steps will help the minister organize a year of exciting and creative sermons (see Appendix A: Preaching Calendar).

1. The first step to planning a preaching calendar is to organize calendar dates. After listing all the dates in one column, insert some key dates on the calendar, such as holidays, the days you intend to be away on vacation, and other special community events you need to be aware of. This is an incredibly important step. Be sure to capitalize strategically on events that may be happening in your ministry context.

When organizing the preaching calendar, the preacher must ask some key questions, such as, “What time of the year does my ministry best attract unchurched visitors?” This information will help the minister maximize those natural times in the community and design sermons and sermon series that will better attract newcomers. It is important to analyze congregational interest well when planning series that connect with issues that are relevant to your listeners.

Pastors can fast and prayerfully ask God where He wants to take the church in the months ahead. One of the things God wants to do in every church is grow it into spiritual maturity and create a climate of righteousness and holiness.

It is also a good idea to preach regularly on the core values of your church. This involves things like evangelism, worship, discipleship, ministry, and fellowship, which are important to the life and health of the church. Ask the Holy Spirit to give creative ways to address these important matters of faith and church health.

### Leading a Creative Preaching Planning Team

#### 10.2.1 — Describe what a creative preaching team is and does.

A Creative Preaching Team

A simple definition of a preaching team is a group of individuals that works together to design and develop the annual preaching calendar as well as produce and deliver the messages.

Describe what a creative preaching team is and does.

Four Advantages to Creative Preaching Teams

1. One of the greatest advantages of developing a preaching team is that it places the responsibility of communicating God’s Word into the hands of many rather than one. Preaching is not a small task—it carries spiritual weight, theological depth, and pastoral influence, and it is too significant to rest on a single voice alone. When multiple communicators are equipped and entrusted, it reflects the biblical model of shared ministry and allows a diversity of gifts, perspectives, and experiences to strengthen the overall impact. This approach not only serves the local church, but also broadens influence across ministry contexts, raising up leaders who can faithfully handle Scripture and speak into a variety of environments. In doing so, preaching becomes a shared calling, not an individual burden, and the ministry is strengthened through collective stewardship of the message.

2. A second significant advantage to the preaching team concept is increased levels of creativity. Few individuals are creative enough, intelligent enough, and strong enough to prepare sermons all alone year after year with the same level of freshness and theological depth necessary to grow a congregation. Preaching teams that include multiple people and their life experiences dramatically raise the level of knowledge and creativity in the preaching event.

First, ask the Lord to help you find a few people in your church (approximately eight is a healthy number) from different backgrounds, spiritual maturity levels, genders, and other life experiences and get them in a comfortable environment together. Explain to them that you need help in thinking through an upcoming sermon or series you would like to preach. Share some Scriptures or thoughts that God has already given you in a time of prayerful preparation and seek the group’s input in presenting those ideas in a creative way. Consider also if there is a story or visual that would best illustrate the point. Once the creative juices begin to flow in that team, you will never want to go back to preparing sermons on your own.

When putting the team together, the minister must keep in mind that while there may be one or two constant team members (usually staff pastors), he or she may want to rotate new people onto the team to keep it fresh and innovative. For instance, if the minister is going to preach a sermon series on raising teenagers, it would be useful to include on the creative team a mom or dad from the congregation who actually has teens in the home. Also included in the planning process may be the worship leader, video multimedia team leader, and drama director. These individuals might be paid staff or volunteers. These elements add a great deal of creativity and excitement to the preaching.

3. A third advantage to creative team planning is that the minister is intuitively developing other communicators within the congregation. By simply taking them through the sermon development process, he or she is teaching them how to prepare a message.

4. A fourth advantage to creative team preaching is instant evaluation. Working on a message together as a team presents the distinct advantage of critiquing ideas, points, and transitions prior to actually delivering the sermon. In most cases, ministers have to wait until they have preached the sermon before they can obtain any feedback. In the creative team approach, feedback occurs in real time. This collaborative team effort makes good sermons great. One word of caution when it comes to feedback: model humility in constructive criticism but always maintain veto power. As the lead communicator, the minister makes the final call when it comes to the message. He or she is ultimately responsible for the message.

**Q:** How would the use of a preaching team benefit your preaching ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary. May include any of the following: reduction in stress on lead communicator; increased levels of creativity in preaching; developing other potential communicators within the congregation; congregation hears the gospel through multiple voices; evaluation prior to message delivery

## 11 - Getting the Message Across

### Connecting with Culture

#### 11.1.1 — Explain the effectiveness of twentieth-century Pentecostal missionaries and how their perspective on speaking in tongues impacted their evangelistic strategy.

The contemporary Pentecostal movement finds its roots in a revival that took shape on the European continent. After making its way across the Atlantic Ocean around the turn of the twentieth century, the outpouring of the Holy Spirit fell on Charles Parham’s faith-healing house, later known as Bethel Bible College in Topeka, Kansas. History tells us that on New Year’s Eve in 1901, Agnes Ozman, one of Parham’s students, received the baptism in the Holy Spirit as evidenced by speaking in other tongues. This experience was then shared by other students, eventually leading to Parham’s personal Spirit baptism. God was beginning a fresh move of the Holy Spirit in the United States.

That spark ignited a flame that blazed across the midwestern plains down to Texas where William Seymour, an African-American Methodist preacher, sat on the front steps of a Pentecostal preacher’s seminar (he was not allowed inside because of segregation). As Seymour listened to Parham preach about the baptism of the Holy Spirit, the power of God came on him, and he took that message by way of train to Azusa Street in Los Angeles, California. It is there that God ignited the beginnings of a worldwide Pentecostal move of His Holy Spirit.

Explain the effectiveness of twentieth-century Pentecostal missionaries and how their perspective on speaking in tongues impacted their evangelistic strategy.

That movement began to take shape in the early 1900s when a group of relatively unschooled and uneducated men and women, full of the Holy Spirit, met in Hot Springs, Arkansas, in 1914 and laid the foundation for what we know as the General Council of the Assemblies of God. After raising a lot of questions about doctrine and beliefs, how to train and ordain ministers, and how to form a movement, they agreed to convene again in Chicago only months later. Later at Stone Church in Chicago, that group committed itself to the greatest evangelization the world had ever seen (McGee 2003). Now, just over one hundred years after the move of the Spirit and the sacrifice of many pioneer missionaries, there are over an estimated 500 million Pentecostals and Charismatics worldwide.

Most everyone has heard that part of the story. Now here is part of the story that many do not know. Those early missionaries who were full of zeal and the power of the Holy Spirit did not fare too well in their initial evangelizing efforts overseas. While those pioneer missionaries and evangelists were long on passion, they were short on cross-cultural communication theory and practice. Undeniably, they felt compelled by the Holy Spirit to take this message to the four corners of the globe due to their certainty that Jesus would soon return. Gary McGee, the leading historian and missiologist for the Assemblies of God, writes,

The first (missionaries) who had been called, yet, due to their feelings concerning the urgency of the hour and confidence they had received the necessary languages through Spirit baptism, spent little or no time in gathering financial resources. Neither did they study the history and culture of the different peoples they hoped to convert. Ministerial credentials, legal recognition, and mission theory took a backseat to the individual guidance of the Holy Spirit. (McGee 2003)

In effect, the early missionaries were largely a group of on-fire preachers who felt the call of God to take the gospel overseas. They were filled with the Holy Ghost and speaking in tongues. Many of them actually believed that the tongues they were given would be the human languages of the cultures where they would minister. Their plan was simple: pack some personal belongings, get on a boat, go overseas, and upon arrival simply start speaking in tongues. Then God would perform a miracle in that act of faith and those listening to the message would understand and believe. McGee says, in most cases, their attempts to evangelize ran aground because they failed to take the time to learn the language and culture of their ministry environments.

McGee goes on to say, “In numerous cases, their overall impact proved shortlived and disappointing. Disillusionment crept in as harsh realities defied their best efforts” (McGee 2003). The attempts of some early missionaries to evangelize the world ran amiss without the awareness of the culture and language. In fact, without a long-term strategy to achieve success in their new environments, says McGee, many missionaries returned home heartbroken and devastated.

However, there was a second group of early missionaries who fared much better. These missionaries built a cross-cultural strategy to create opportunity to effectively evangelize the lost. Furthermore, they understood the gift of tongues to be a gift of praying in the Spirit, rather than instantaneous ability to speak another earthly language. McGee (2003) notes,

In the second category, hardy souls survived by learning the language, adjusting to different cultural context, and adapting to the challenges that confronted them. Even when they discovered that speaking in tongues represented prayer in the Spirit instead of linguistic expertise, they accepted the transition in meaning since they shared in the experience of tongues that had inflamed the disciples with zeal on the Day of Pentecost.

The efforts of these cross-cultural communicators of the gospel proved to be successful.

Today’s preacher needs the empowering and anointing work of the Holy Spirit in order to be effective. He or she also needs a long-term strategy as a communicator of the gospel. We need to learn from our missionary forebears who paved the way. We need to speak the language of the day in a style that relates the unchanging truths of Scripture to an ever-changing culture. This is one of the great lessons learned by our missionaries. In fact, once discovered by our missionaries, this mission theory profoundly changed how they set out to reach people.

Relate colonization and contextualization to early missionary efforts and training.

Colonization and Contextualization

The missionary strategy began to change when men of faith like Melvin Hodges realized that it was both impractical and impossible to franchise or colonize Christianity. Hodges taught that Christianity was based on principles and truths that transcend both time and culture. Hodges and his contemporary missionary strategists discovered that Christianity can be preached anywhere, anytime, and thrive in any community, regardless of culture or time. Hodges is credited with developing the first Assemblies of God missiology, known today as the “Indigenous Church Principle.” The principle is that a viable church in any culture must be self-propagating, self-governing, and self-supporting. These three elements are still in effect today and continue to lead the Assemblies of God and many other organizations in the ever-expanding missions effort that sees hundreds of thousands of salvations each year.

Hodges discovered that while Christianity cannot be colonized, it can certainly be contextualized to any culture. Missionaries have been engaged in this principle every day for the past one hundred years. Contextualizing Christianity works at finding ways to fit Christianity into a culture and world system. One simply needs to maintain the spiritual discernment to decipher between what is cultural and what is eternal.

Contextualizing Christianity simply means studying the language and the culture of the people one is trying to reach and then using that knowledge to communicate the message in a way that makes sense and is understandable. Next, the communicator teaches the hearers how to live out the principles of Christianity in the context where they live. In other words, contextualizing Christianity makes the faith and its corresponding teachings relevant to the contemporary life and culture of the hearer.

Lessons from Early Missionaries

Missionaries learned three valuable lessons from the development of their early strategic lessons. Effective preachers of the twenty-first century would do well to remember and apply these simple yet profound lessons which will result in increased ministry effectiveness in today’s contemporary culture.

Understand the World

After the initial failure to reach across cultural lines with the gospel, early missionaries regrouped and began to rethink how to share the love of Christ with people groups that neither understood their language nor their culture. Instead of seeking first to be understood, early missionaries focused their evangelism efforts on first understanding the culture of those they were trying to reach. All understanding begins with language. It is nearly impossible to communicate Christ and the message of the cross to someone, or an entire nation, who does not understand the language you speak. This can be applied to contemporary preachers living in the twenty-first century. The language of our culture today is digital. Popular culture in America is driven by digital media, such as the internet, smartphones, movies, high definition television, podcasts, blogs, email, and much more. Contemporary preachers ought to understand this world. To some extent, they need to become cultural anthropologists, studying their culture from the ground up. For instance, they may ask, “What makes this culture tick?” “How can I incorporate the things I learn about the culture into communicating Christ?”

Only when we understand our world can we communicate with any relevance. David Henderson (1998) recommends asking the following questions to better understand the world around us:

What drives the world around you?

What makes it different from other places and times?

How do people spend their time and money?

What is most on the heart of people who share the freeway and supermarket with you?

What are people dreaming about, thinking about, longing for?

How do people make sense of life?

What worldviews are you most likely to encounter among your friends and acquaintances?

Who or what has the ear of people around you?

How does sin most often express itself?

Becoming a World Student

Following are some practical ways ministers can become students of the world. One, ministers must read everything they can in popular culture. This will expose them to what is going on in society and give fresh examples and creative insights on how to communicate the gospel in contemporary culture. These reading resources include newspapers, magazines, professional journals, novels, and even comic books to discover ways to better understand and build a bridge to the community.

Going to the movies and watching television are equally helpful in learning the culture. As discussed earlier, the screen, whether in the home or a theater, is the most powerful communication medium in contemporary culture. By studying not only the content of the story, but also the manner in which the story is told, ministers will become more effective communicators in the twenty-first century.

Another method in understanding the pulse of contemporary culture is to practice observing life. Preachers must relate the Scriptures to people who face diverse situations and needs. One of the best ways for ministers to discover those needs is to train themselves to be sensitive to the needs of others. In his book Between Two Worlds , author John R. W. Stott argues that preaching is not exposition only but communication that enables the preacher to cross the cultural gulf that exists between two worlds that would otherwise be cut off from each other (Stott 1982). This chasm can be crossed through conscientious study of culture and a willingness to implement those hard-learned lessons for the gospel.

Build Relationships

Postmodernists respond best to truth when it comes packaged in relationship. Most people in America do not have time for a conversation with a Christian who sees them as another notch on their evangelistic belt. However, they are open to friendship and relationship from a genuine Christian who shows authentic interest in their needs and life. If we are going to reach this generation, we need to cross the cultural divide and begin to form relationships with those outside the church.

Preaching to people you do not understand is a recipe for disaster. Expounding on rules without first developing understanding will not maximize the effectiveness of your preaching for Christ. Consider the incarnation. In one sense, it was God becoming one of us to understand us firsthand (Hebrews 4:14–16).

The fact is, we must spend time with people and build friendships, not only because we are trying to win them to Christ, but because it is the right thing to do. Jesus modeled and taught us that we are to love our neighbors as ourselves (Matthew 22:39) and to do to others as we would have them do unto us (Matthew 7:12). The motivation of the heart is everything when it comes to reaching lost people in a contemporary wilderness, and while we cannot know everyone personally, we can understand people and their cultural perspectives.

Seek God’s Help

The third component to building cultural bridges that span the great divide between the kingdom of God and the kingdom of this world is to seek divine guidance and help. Ultimately, this is a spiritual pursuit that will take supernatural power to accomplish. The early missionaries prayed earnestly for God’s wisdom, direction, and creative insight regarding their missions strategy. Pastors today must continue to seek God’s face and daily declare their dependence upon Him if they are going to be effective for the Kingdom.

**Q:** What can you learn from the pioneer missionaries who first took the gospel overseas?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** In your own words, describe the difference between colonizing Christianity and contextualizing Christianity.

**A:** Colonizing Christianity is another phrase for franchising Christianity. It is when you take a certain “style” of Christianity that emerges from a specific culture and impose that on a completely different culture. For example, some missions groups imposed the westernized form of Christianity, which emphasized the individual over the group, on Eastern and / or African societies. That core value was not a biblical mandate, and was adverse to spreading the gospel in the culture. On the other hand, contextualizing Christianity finds the foundational, non-negotiable core values of Scripture and then appropriately applies them in the context of the selected culture.

**Q:** In your own words, describe the three lessons learned by early missionaries and how they might apply in your ministry context.

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What are some ways you can better acquaint yourself with the culture you minister to?

**A:** Answers will vary.
