### The Case for Relevance in America

#### 11.2.1 — Discuss twenty-first century growth trends in the American church and the implications for relevance.

Relevant Contemporary Preaching

If ministers are going to be effective communicators and reach this generation, we have to know something about what is going on in our culture. While we live in a day and age where more people are open to God and spirituality, the church may be missing out on a great opportunity to connect with the audience. Today’s audience needs more than pithy insights and spiritual platitudes. It needs contemporary preachers who, like the men of Issachar (1 Chronicles 12:32), understood the times in which they lived and engaged culture with hard-hitting truth delivered with grace and humility.

Discuss twenty-first century growth trends in the American church and the implications for relevance.

While the message of the gospel must never change, the methods must always change with each new generation. The message never changes because God never changes. The Bible says He “is the same yesterday and today and forever” (Hebrews 13:8). Too much of preaching today is merely pedantic explanation of biblical minutia. At the same time, the number one reason for leaving the church among this generation is that church is “boring and irrelevant.” What the church needs today are preachers who can present the timeless, unchanging truths of Scripture in a timely fashion that applies to everyday life. In other words, the goal of contemporary preaching is not merely to present information but to preach for transformation.

Relevance is the art of presenting the old message in a new way that applies to a contemporary audience. The word traces back to a Medieval Latin word, relevaré , which means “to bear upon” (Henderson 1998). Something is relevant, says Henderson, when it has to do with one’s circumstances and applies to one’s everyday life. Many preachers in today’s pulpit do a fairly decent job at explaining the text. However, most fail at demonstrating its relevance to the audience. A preacher does the greatest injustice to the Word of God when he or she presents the truth and fails to connect how that truth applies to the listener’s life. One preacher said, “Information without application is an abortion of the text.” In other words, preachers kill the power of the text when they fail to explain the implications of the biblical truth in the contemporary context.

Clearly, relevance is right for the contemporary preacher when properly understood as relating the appropriateness of Scripture to contemporary culture. The challenging task is to live up to king David’s epitaph memorialized in Acts 13:36, “When David had served God’s purpose in his own generation, he fell asleep” (Guinness 2003, 12). According to the writer of Acts, king David demonstrated relevance in his communication and leadership in that he served God’s eternal purpose in his own contemporary generation. Much like David, we need to understand that God has raised up this twenty-first century generation for a reason, and therefore, we should seize the moment by communicating the gospel in a way that connects with contemporary culture. We must accomplish God’s purposes (eternal) in our generation (cultural).

The Relevance of Christianity

Relevance is at the heart of the gospel. Willhite rightly points out that “Scripture does not need to be made relevant; it is already relevant” (Gibson 2004). The task of today’s preacher is to demonstrate its relevance in an understandable manner. For instance, the Old Testament says that a man should not covet another man’s donkey. Now, most people living in urban areas in this century do not interact too often with donkeys. One way to demonstrate relevance in this instance, for example, would be to drill down into the heart of that principle, explain the culture and context of the day in which the principle was given, and then apply it to today’s audience. In this case, the donkey was typically the source of work for those living in an agricultural society. It helped the farmers plow their fields and cart their goods to the marketplace, the center of commerce and economy. Today, the computer often fills that role. Computers drive the twenty-first century economy and commerce. Rightly understood, the principle is to not covet another individual’s computer, its memory or hard drive space, or other electronic bells and whistles; more than that, do not covet another’s belongings in general. Simply be thankful for what God has given, and do your best with what has been entrusted to your economic stewardship. If you can be trusted with little, you can be trusted with more.

This is just one example of relevance. There is no excuse for presenting the gospel without connecting to culture in a relevant fashion. Perhaps that might mean preaching style, worship service environment, music preference, or a host of other ministry elements. Jesus is relevant in every culture and every generation. We who follow in Jesus’ footsteps should be the most relevant people on the planet. Down through the centuries, new wine has called for new wineskins and endless creativity and innovation (Guinness 2003). Henderson argues that with each new generation, the Bible’s relevance is harder to see (Henderson 1998). This is why contemporary preachers are charged with demonstrating how the Bible impacts everyday life, in real-world settings.

Identify two inherent dangers of pursuing relevance.

Dangers in Pursuing Relevance

When pursuing relevance in preaching and ministry, ministers must be especially cautious to maintain biblical authority and authenticity. There is a tendency for preachers to conclude that when something is difficult to understand, it is rendered irrelevant. For example, when a biblical passage or principle does not make immediate sense to the reader or hearer, one might prematurely conclude that it is therefore irrelevant. The assumption that unfamiliarity equates to irrelevance is simply wrong. Contemporary preachers must avoid the huge pitfall of writing off theological principles, difficult narratives, and unfamiliar concepts of Scripture as being irrelevant to the contemporary audience.

Furthermore, another potential pitfall in the pursuit of relevance is the propensity to make the principles of Scripture more palatable to the current culture. The reality of Scripture is that it presents many uncomfortable, hard truths of life and the kingdom of God. The contemporary preacher must avoid the penchant of smoothing over difficult truths to accommodate the Bible to fit modern society. Those who are susceptible to this line of thinking often will try to change or soften the message of Scripture to attract a broader audience. Upholding biblical integrity is crucial in relating relevance to contemporary culture.

**Q:** Why is the church in America in decline?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What is your understanding of relevant ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How can you avoid falling prey to the dangers of relevance in your ministry setting?

**A:** Answers will vary.

#### 11.2.2 — Identify two inherent dangers of pursuing relevance.

Relevant Contemporary Preaching

If ministers are going to be effective communicators and reach this generation, we have to know something about what is going on in our culture. While we live in a day and age where more people are open to God and spirituality, the church may be missing out on a great opportunity to connect with the audience. Today’s audience needs more than pithy insights and spiritual platitudes. It needs contemporary preachers who, like the men of Issachar (1 Chronicles 12:32), understood the times in which they lived and engaged culture with hard-hitting truth delivered with grace and humility.

Discuss twenty-first century growth trends in the American church and the implications for relevance.

While the message of the gospel must never change, the methods must always change with each new generation. The message never changes because God never changes. The Bible says He “is the same yesterday and today and forever” (Hebrews 13:8). Too much of preaching today is merely pedantic explanation of biblical minutia. At the same time, the number one reason for leaving the church among this generation is that church is “boring and irrelevant.” What the church needs today are preachers who can present the timeless, unchanging truths of Scripture in a timely fashion that applies to everyday life. In other words, the goal of contemporary preaching is not merely to present information but to preach for transformation.

Relevance is the art of presenting the old message in a new way that applies to a contemporary audience. The word traces back to a Medieval Latin word, relevaré , which means “to bear upon” (Henderson 1998). Something is relevant, says Henderson, when it has to do with one’s circumstances and applies to one’s everyday life. Many preachers in today’s pulpit do a fairly decent job at explaining the text. However, most fail at demonstrating its relevance to the audience. A preacher does the greatest injustice to the Word of God when he or she presents the truth and fails to connect how that truth applies to the listener’s life. One preacher said, “Information without application is an abortion of the text.” In other words, preachers kill the power of the text when they fail to explain the implications of the biblical truth in the contemporary context.

Clearly, relevance is right for the contemporary preacher when properly understood as relating the appropriateness of Scripture to contemporary culture. The challenging task is to live up to king David’s epitaph memorialized in Acts 13:36, “When David had served God’s purpose in his own generation, he fell asleep” (Guinness 2003, 12). According to the writer of Acts, king David demonstrated relevance in his communication and leadership in that he served God’s eternal purpose in his own contemporary generation. Much like David, we need to understand that God has raised up this twenty-first century generation for a reason, and therefore, we should seize the moment by communicating the gospel in a way that connects with contemporary culture. We must accomplish God’s purposes (eternal) in our generation (cultural).

The Relevance of Christianity

Relevance is at the heart of the gospel. Willhite rightly points out that “Scripture does not need to be made relevant; it is already relevant” (Gibson 2004). The task of today’s preacher is to demonstrate its relevance in an understandable manner. For instance, the Old Testament says that a man should not covet another man’s donkey. Now, most people living in urban areas in this century do not interact too often with donkeys. One way to demonstrate relevance in this instance, for example, would be to drill down into the heart of that principle, explain the culture and context of the day in which the principle was given, and then apply it to today’s audience. In this case, the donkey was typically the source of work for those living in an agricultural society. It helped the farmers plow their fields and cart their goods to the marketplace, the center of commerce and economy. Today, the computer often fills that role. Computers drive the twenty-first century economy and commerce. Rightly understood, the principle is to not covet another individual’s computer, its memory or hard drive space, or other electronic bells and whistles; more than that, do not covet another’s belongings in general. Simply be thankful for what God has given, and do your best with what has been entrusted to your economic stewardship. If you can be trusted with little, you can be trusted with more.

This is just one example of relevance. There is no excuse for presenting the gospel without connecting to culture in a relevant fashion. Perhaps that might mean preaching style, worship service environment, music preference, or a host of other ministry elements. Jesus is relevant in every culture and every generation. We who follow in Jesus’ footsteps should be the most relevant people on the planet. Down through the centuries, new wine has called for new wineskins and endless creativity and innovation (Guinness 2003). Henderson argues that with each new generation, the Bible’s relevance is harder to see (Henderson 1998). This is why contemporary preachers are charged with demonstrating how the Bible impacts everyday life, in real-world settings.

Identify two inherent dangers of pursuing relevance.

Dangers in Pursuing Relevance

When pursuing relevance in preaching and ministry, ministers must be especially cautious to maintain biblical authority and authenticity. There is a tendency for preachers to conclude that when something is difficult to understand, it is rendered irrelevant. For example, when a biblical passage or principle does not make immediate sense to the reader or hearer, one might prematurely conclude that it is therefore irrelevant. The assumption that unfamiliarity equates to irrelevance is simply wrong. Contemporary preachers must avoid the huge pitfall of writing off theological principles, difficult narratives, and unfamiliar concepts of Scripture as being irrelevant to the contemporary audience.

Furthermore, another potential pitfall in the pursuit of relevance is the propensity to make the principles of Scripture more palatable to the current culture. The reality of Scripture is that it presents many uncomfortable, hard truths of life and the kingdom of God. The contemporary preacher must avoid the penchant of smoothing over difficult truths to accommodate the Bible to fit modern society. Those who are susceptible to this line of thinking often will try to change or soften the message of Scripture to attract a broader audience. Upholding biblical integrity is crucial in relating relevance to contemporary culture.

**Q:** Why is the church in America in decline?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** What is your understanding of relevant ministry?

**A:** Answers will vary.

**Q:** How can you avoid falling prey to the dangers of relevance in your ministry setting?

**A:** Answers will vary.

## 12 - Preaching with Imagination

### Right-Brain Preaching

#### 12.1.1 — Explain why right-brain preaching facilitates creative communication.

Neurologists subdivide the brain into regions that are responsible for a variety of neurological functions. The visual cortex handles all input from the optic nerve. The posterior hippocampus stores spatial memory. The medial ventral prefrontal cortex is the seat of humor. Whether you are humming a song, solving a Sudoku puzzle, or interpreting facial expressions, there is a unique part of the brain that is responsible for performing those functions.

Explain why right-brain preaching facilitates creative communication.

The brain is also divided into two hemispheres: the right brain and left brain. Those two hemispheres are connected by approximately three hundred million nerve fibers called the corpus callosum (fibers uniting the cerebral hemispheres). Think of the two hemispheres of the brain as parallel processors. They certainly overlap in function. And this is a gross simplification of something that is divinely complex. The left brain is the logical half of the brain, and the right brain is the creative half of the brain.

Now juxtapose brain topography with Matthew 22:37: “Love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your mind.” Loving God with half your mind does not cut it. Half-minded is no better than halfhearted. But many preachers are trying to preach with half their brain tied behind their back. This is about as effective as running on one leg, clapping with one hand, or twiddling one thumb.

Half-Formed Imaginations

Ministers take systematic theology in seminary, develop three-point sermons with alliterations in homiletics, and learn how to put together an order of service in practical theology. While there is nothing wrong with such training, our sermons ought to be logical, and we have to develop the theological paradigms. The key to postmodern preaching is combining right-brain creativity with left-brain logic.

C. S. Lewis is the patron saint of whole-brain preachers. Can you think of anyone in the last century who was more left-brain logical? His theological writings—from Mere Christianity to The Problem of Pain —are as logical as possible. But Lewis combined left-brain logic with right-brain creativity. The Chronicles of Narnia series is evidence of this brilliant imagination.

Lewis once referred to himself as “the most reluctant convert in all of Christendom.” The night before his conversion, Lewis had a long conversation with J. R. R. Tolkien, the novelist who wrote The Lord of the Rings trilogy. Tolkien tried to convince Lewis of the credibility of Christ, but Lewis was full of objections. At one point, Tolkien countered Lewis’s objections by saying, “Your inability to understand stems from a failure of imagination on your part!”

Maybe lack of faith is really a failure of imagination? In his book Following the Celtic Way , Ian Bradley (2020) writes about the celebration of the imagination in the Celtic tradition. Inspired in part by the Psalms, influential Celtic Christians “were associated with an approach to expressing and explaining the mysteries of the Christian faith through story, symbol and verse [poetry]” (48, 49). God wants to sanctify our imaginations and use them for His purposes. The first verse of Hebrews offers a good definition of right-brain preaching: “In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets at many times and in various ways.” God does not say the same thing the same way every time. He mixes it up. He finds new ways to say old things.

Consider how the Old Testament prophets communicated; it bordered on the absurd. Jeremiah hid his belt in Perath (Jeremiah 13:3–5). Hosea married a prostitute (Hosea 1:2–3). Ezekiel baked his bread over cow dung for 390 days (Ezekiel 4:9–15). This is some strange stuff, but one thing is for sure: God is not a broken record. He loves to communicate in various ways.

Berlyne’s Law

According to the research of psychologist Daniel Berlyne, what strikes us as good art is usually “a slight deviation from our expectations” (Roese 2005, 168–169). Art that is too large a deviation from what we already know is considered bizarre. Art that fits our expectations perfectly is considering boring. Good art is somewhere in between. We like mild surprises that fall somewhere between boring and bizarre. What most people consider to be great art is a modest change from the status quo. Great preaching is a slight deviation from expectation.

In the words of Thomas Moore, “It’s my conviction that slight shifts in imagination have more impact on living than major efforts at change.” Slight shifting in imagination is what right-brain preaching is all about. So how do we produce slight shifts in imagination? The key is using metaphors. Aristotle said, “The greatest thing by far is to be the master of metaphor.” No one was more masterful than Jesus. The parables are case studies in right-brain preaching. Hear them once, and you will remember them forever. Why? Because Jesus used metaphors that created mental pictures in the right brain of His listeners.

One key to right-brain preaching is cross-pollination. The speaker redeems metaphors from a variety of disciplines and uses them to communicate spiritual truth. Metaphors enable us to reframe truth in ways that are biblically accurate and culturally relevant.

Distinguish between cultural exegesis and biblical exegesis.

Cultural Exegesis

Too many pastors ace biblical exegesis and fail in cultural exegesis. They know Scripture but are out of touch with the times. The end result is irrelevance—a growing gap between theology and reality. When that gap widens, people don’t necessarily reject the truth of Scripture; they simply fail to see how it connects to their everyday lives. As a result, the church can unintentionally retreat into the comfortable confines of its own subculture, speaking a language that makes sense internally but feels foreign to those on the outside. To bridge that gap, we must learn to exegete not only the text, but also the context. That is what incarnation is all about—truth stepping into the world of the listener.

According to Anthony Mayo and Nitin Nohria (2005), there is one common denominator among all great leaders, regardless of age or industry: “They possessed acute sensitivity to the social, political, technological, and demographic contexts that came to define their eras.” These authors call it “contextual intelligence.” After studying one thousand leaders, they concluded that contextual intelligence is “an underappreciated but all-encompassing differentiator between success and failure.” This insight translates directly into ministry. Effective communicators of the gospel are not just biblically literate; they are culturally aware. They pay attention to what people are feeling, fearing, watching, and wrestling with.

Scripture affirms this principle. In 1 Chronicles 12:32, the men of Issachar are described as those who “understood the times and knew what Israel should do.” They had both insight and foresight. They weren’t just students of the past—they were interpreters of the present. Their leadership flowed from their awareness. In the same way, today’s preacher must develop the ability to discern cultural currents and respond with biblical clarity. Foresight in preaching is born out of insight into people.

Practically, this means building rhythms that keep you connected to the world you are called to reach. Listen to what people are talking about—on social media, in conversations, and in the community. Pay attention to the questions people are asking, not just the answers you want to give. Watch how culture tells stories through film, music, and media, and ask what those stories reveal about deeper spiritual hunger. This doesn’t mean consuming everything uncritically, but it does mean staying aware. A helpful practice is to regularly ask, “What are the dominant narratives shaping my congregation this week?” and “Where does the gospel intersect with those narratives?”

Another practical step is to translate, not dilute. Redeeming culture does not mean watering down the message—it means making it understandable. Jesus modeled this by using everyday imagery: seeds, soil, vineyards, coins, and families. In the same way, modern communicators can use current metaphors—apps, algorithms, relationships, work pressures, mental health struggles—to bridge the gap between ancient truth and present reality. The goal is not to make Scripture trendy, but to make it tangible.

There is, of course, a fine line between redeeming culture and compromising truth, but it is a line we must be willing to walk with wisdom and conviction. God is omnirelevant—He speaks into every culture, every generation, and every context. Redeeming culture means faithfully translating the unchanging message of the gospel into language people can actually hear and understand. When we do this well, we don’t lose the power of the message—we release it.

**Q:** Who are some examples of whole-brain communicators?

**A:** C. S. Lewis; J. R. R. Tolkien

**Q:** How does Berlyne’s Law impact the way one should preach?

**A:** Preaching, like art, should create curiosity and stir the imagination. The best way to preach is in a way that slightly deviates from what is expected.

**Q:** Why is cultural exegesis just as important as biblical exegesis?

**A:** Divorcing biblical exegesis from cultural exegesis results in dysfunctional truth. If one is to be relevant, one must be able to communicate the timeless truths of Scripture in a way the audience can understand and embrace.

#### 12.1.3 — Identify the seven steps to sermon branding.

I am neither a brain surgeon nor the son of a brain surgeon, but my bookshelves are filled with books on neurology. Nothing in the universe is more fascinating to me than the three pounds of gray matter housed within the human cranium. I think the human mind is the magnum opus (greatest achievement) of God’s creative genius.

Neurologists subdivide the brain into regions that are responsible for a variety of neurological functions. The visual cortex handles all input from the optic nerve. The posterior hippocampus stores spatial memory. The medial ventral prefrontal cortex is the seat of humor. Whether you are humming a song, solving a Sudoku puzzle, or interpreting facial expressions, there is a unique part of the brain that is responsible for performing those functions.

The brain is also divided into two hemispheres: the right brain and left brain. Those two hemispheres are connected by approximately three hundred million nerve fibers called the corpus callosum (fibers uniting the cerebral hemispheres). Think of the two hemispheres of the brain as parallel processors. They certainly overlap in function. And this is a gross simplification of something that is divinely complex. The left brain is the logical half of the brain, and the right brain is the creative half of the brain.

Now juxtapose brain topography with Matthew 22:37: “Love the Lord your God with all your heart and with all your soul and with all your mind.” Loving God with half your mind does not cut it. Half-minded is no better than half-hearted. But many preachers are trying to preach with half their brain tied behind their back. This is about as effective as running on one leg, clapping with one hand, or twiddling one thumb.

Half-Formed Imaginations

When looking at church history, I see a focus on left-brain logic during the modern era. Ministers take systematic theology in seminary, develop three-point sermons with alliterations in homiletics, and learn how to put together an order of service in practical theology. While there is nothing wrong with such training, our sermons ought to be logical, and we have to develop the theological paradigms. The key to postmodern preaching is combining right-brain creativity with left-brain logic.

C. S. Lewis is the patron saint of whole-brain preachers. Can you think of anyone in the last century who was more left-brain logical? His theological writings—from Mere Christianity to The Problem of Pain— are as logical as possible. But Lewis combined left-brain logic with right-brain creativity. The Chronicles of Narnia series is evidence of this brilliant imagination.

Lewis once referred to himself as “the most reluctant convert in all of Christendom.” The night before his conversion, Lewis had a long conversation with J. R. R. Tolkien, the novelist who wrote the Lord of the Rings trilogy. Tolkien tried to convince Lewis of the credibility of Christ, but Lewis was full of objections. At one point, Tolkien countered Lewis’ objections by saying, “Your inability to understand stems from a failure of imagination on your part!”

Maybe lack of faith is really a failure of imagination? In his book The Celtic Way , Ian Bradley (1993) writes about the celebration of the imagination in the Celtic tradition. “Celtic Christianity may offer us a lifeline in the form of an approach to faith which is rooted in imagination. Too many Christians today, brought up on the penny plain prose favoured by Rome and even more by the Reformers, have half-formed imaginations.” God wants to sanctify our imaginations and use them for His purposes. The first verse of Hebrews offers a good definition of right-brain preaching: “In the past God spoke to our forefathers through the prophets at many times and in various ways.” God does not say the same thing the same way every time. He mixes it up. He finds new ways to say old things.

Consider how the Old Testament prophets communicated; it bordered on the absurd. Jeremiah hid his belt in Perath (Jeremiah 13:3–5). Hosea married a prostitute (Hosea 1:2–3). Ezekiel baked his bread over cow dung for 390 days (Ezekiel 4:9–15). This is some strange stuff, but one thing is for sure: God is not a broken record. He loves to communicate in various ways.

Berlyne’s Law

According to the research of psychologist Daniel Berlyne, what strikes us as good art is usually “a slight deviation from our expectations” (Roese 2005, 168–169). Art that is too large a deviation from what we already know is considered bizarre. Art that fits our expectations perfectly is considering boring. Good art is somewhere in between. We like mild surprises that fall somewhere between boring and bizarre. What most people consider to be great art is a modest change from the status quo. I think that has huge implications for the art of preaching. My preaching modus operandi is this: say old things in new ways. I try to come at truth from slightly different angles, almost like turning a kaleidoscope. Great preaching is a slight deviation from expectation.

In the words of Thomas Moore, “It’s my conviction that slight shifts in imagination have more impact on living than major efforts at change.” Slight shifting in imagination is what right-brain preaching is all about. So how do we produce slight shifts in imagination? The key is using metaphors. Aristotle said, “The greatest thing by far is to be the master of metaphor.” No one was more masterful than Jesus. The parables are case studies in right-brain preaching. Hear them once, and you will remember them forever. Why? Because Jesus used metaphors that created mental pictures in the right brain of His listeners.

One key to right-brain preaching is cross-pollination. The speaker redeems metaphors from a variety of disciplines and uses them to communicate spiritual truth. I recently did a series titled The Physics of Faith . I borrowed some basic laws of physics like Heisenberg’s Uncertainty Principle, Bell’s Theorem, and The Law of Entropy and used them to talk about spiritual principles. Each message in that series was a scientific parable. Metaphors enable us to reframe truth in ways that are biblically accurate and culturally relevant.

Cross-pollination also gives intellectual leverage. Quoting Scripture may give the preacher credibility with people who are churched. However, non-biblical quotes can offer credibility with people who are unchurched. When I reference a law of physics or quote Aristotle or cite a Fast Company article, I score points with skeptics.

Cultural Exegesis

Too many pastors ace biblical exegesis and fail in cultural exegesis. They know Scripture but are out of touch with the times. The end result is irrelevance, a gap between theology and reality. We withdraw to the comfortable confines of our Christian subculture, so we are out of touch with the audience, the unchurched and de-churched. We have to exegete our culture, so we can close the gap. That is what incarnation is all about.

According to Anthony Mayo and Nitin Nohria (2005), there is one common denominator among all great leaders, regardless of age or industry: “They possessed acute sensitivity to the social, political, technological, and demographic contexts that came to define their eras.” These authors call it “contextual intelligence.” After studying one thousand leaders, they came to the conclusion that contextual intelligence is “an underappreciated but all-encompassing differentiator between success and failure” (2005, xv).

1 Chronicles 12:32 says the men of Issachar “understood the times and knew what Israel should do.” They had tremendous insight—“they understood the times.” And they had tremendous foresight—“they knew what Israel should do.” Here is another way of saying it: they had foresight because they had insight. They were visionary leaders because they were experts in cultural exegesis.

There is a fine line between redeeming culture and compromising truth, but it is a line we must be willing to walk. God is omni-relevant. He speaks more than seven billion languages. Redeeming culture does not mean watering down or dumbing down the gospel. It means translating the gospel into a language people can understand. It is using culturally relevant metaphors so people can grasp spiritual truths. Isn’t that what Jesus did? He used agrarian metaphors to communicate spiritual truths. We call them parables.

Every generation needs to redeem cultural lingo and use it to communicate timeless truth. The only alternative is irrelevance, and irrelevance is irreverence. The key to unforgettable preaching is packaging truth in ways that are biblically sound and culturally relevant. Let me borrow from the parable of the wineskins. Think of biblical exegesis as the wine. Think of cultural relevance as the wineskin. Having one without the other will not quench anybody’s thirst. There needs to be the substance (biblical exegesis) and the container (cultural relevance). Divorcing biblical exegesis from cultural exegesis results in dysfunctional truth. Hence, preachers end up answering questions no one is asking or giving the wrong answers to questions.

Every year, National Community Church does two sermon series that redeem culture: God @ the Box Office and God @ the Billboards . Here is why: the sixty percent of Americans who do not attend church get their theology from movies and music. For better or for worse, musicians and movie makers are the chief theologians in our culture.

The eighteenth century Scottish thinker Andrew Fletcher said, “Give me the making of the songs of a nation and I care not who writes its laws.” Our culture is shaped, even more than we know, by the movies we watch and the music we listen to. God @ the Box Office and God @ the Billboards are attempts to exegete the movies and music that are shaping the cultural consciousness of 175 million unchurched Americans. Then we juxtapose them with Scripture.

Those series are two of the hardest hitting series we do all year. The reason is simple: movies and music are brutally honest about the human condition. They may not contain the truth, but they are in touch with reality. Ravi Zacharias writes, “I credit them with a greater degree of honesty and unmasked vulnerability in recognizing the anguish within the human heart than the academician, who often conceals such a struggle behind a façade of self-assurance.”

Sermon Branding

John 12:49 has always been my preaching mantra. Jesus said, “I did not speak of my own accord, but the Father who sent me commanded me what to say and how to say it.” What is sermon content. How is sermon branding.

I have a core preaching conviction: the greatest truths ought to be communicated in the most unforgettable ways. When it comes to communicating things in unforgettable ways, how is just as important as what .

Sermon branding is nothing new. It is as old as the ancient prophets using God-inspired props to make their messages stick. Jesus took the Old Testament art form to a new level. No one was better at branding truth than The Truth. His parables are pure genius. Sermon branding is hard work, but it is not optional if ministers are serious about engaging the right brain of listeners as Jesus did with His parables.

Seven Steps to Sermon Branding

Come Up with a Series Title

There is a fine line between catchy and cheesy. The goal is to reduce an entire series to a single word, phrase, or symbol that captures the essence of the series. Book titles, magazine ads, TV shows, board games, and movies are a great source of creative inspiration. Here are some recent NCC series titles: The Physics of Faith, Why We Do What We Do, Soulprint, The Game of Life, The Wild Goose Chase, Creed, God @ the Billboards, Wired for Worship.

Create a Series Logo

The old aphorism is wrong: a picture is not worth a thousand words. According to neurological research, the brain is able to process print on a page at a rate of approximately one hundred bits per second, but the brain can process a picture at approximately one billion bits per second. Mathematically speaking, a picture is literally worth ten million words.

Using logos, for example, is important because of the way the brain processes information. The brain recognizes and remembers shapes first, colors second, and content third. It is the sequence of cognition. If a speaker wants people to listen to his or her content, the speaker had better think about shapes and colors. If choosing color schemes seems to be void of spiritual significance, read the book of Exodus. A dozen chapters are devoted to design. God gives very specific instructions about colors and scents.

Design a Series E-Vite and Invite

Aesthetics are important. The key to buzz is word of mouth and word of mouse. One way to generate buzz about a sermon series is to send an e-vite to the church’s e-mail list. Encourage the congregation to forward it to friends and give them invite cards to give to friends.

Brainstorm Big Ideas

The more you say the less people will remember. It is the law of scope: more is less, and less is more. That is why I try to reduce every message into one big idea. Making too many points turns one’s message into a bed of nails. Lie down on a thousand nails, and they would not penetrate the skin. Why? The pressure of each point is diffused by all the others around it. Likewise, too many points in a sermon are a bed of nails. But a single point will penetrate the heart and soul.

During The Game of Life series, we played off the board game by the same name and used seven spaces to symbolize seven big ideas:

• Graduation Day: Enjoy the Journey

• Tour Europe: Take Calculated Risks

• Night School: Keep Asking Questions

• Win Marathon: Set God-Sized Goals

• Start a Career: Pursue God-Ordained Passions

• Get Married: Fall in Love

• Pay Day: Pay It Forward

Shoot a Series Trailer

One way to brand a series and generate excitement is to add creative video elements. The minister can show a series trailer the week before kicking off the series and put it on the church’s Web site. For trailer samples, visit www.theaterchurch.com.

Add Sermon Props

Jesus used everything from mustard seeds to Roman coins to make His messages stick. He preached from boats, washed feet, and used little children as sermon props. Sermon props make messages more memorable because they involve more than one sense. The more multisensory one’s message is the more memorable it will be.

Over the years we have used everything from nails to pop rocks to silly putty to make our messages more memorable. You may even want to design series clothing and accessories. It is a great way of turning your congregation into walking billboards.

Add Sermon Staging

Though this will take some time and effort, I would recommend redesigning the stage for every sermon series. It is a great way of keeping things fresh. A new look will generate new excitement. Have fun with it. During our annual God @ the Box Office series, we literally roll out the red carpet and treat every NCCer like an Oscar nominee. It gives us an excuse to give them the red carpet treatment.

**A:** a) William Seymour. b) Phoebe Palmer. c) Agnes Ozman. d) A. B. Simpson.
